CCNA Study notes


1. Network Fundamentals (20%):
Network Components:

Routers, Switches, Servers, Access Points, Controllers, Endpoints, Firewalls

Network Topologies:

Two─tier, Three─tier, Spine─leaf, WAN, SOHO, On─premise, Cloud computing

Compare: 

- TCP to UDP.
- Physical interface and cabling types: Single-mode fiber, multimode fiber, copper.
- Identify interfaceand cable issues (collisions, errors, mismatch duplex, and/or speed).

IPv4 and IPv6:

Virtualization fundamentals (server virtualization, containers, and VRFs)
IP parameters for Client OS (Windows, Mac OS, Linux)


1. Router:

─ Provides connectivity between multiple subnets/networks; routers forward traffic between different networks.

─ Keeps networks separated and does not forward broadcast traffic between subnets by default.

─ Forwards data based on IP addresses (Layer 3 of the OSI model).

─ Supports dynamic routing protocols like OSPF, EIGRP, and BGP to learn routes automatically.

─ For the same destination, multiple paths may be available; the router selects the best path using the routing table (based on metrics).

─ For fast packet forwarding, it uses the FIB (Forwarding Information Base), built from the routing table via CEF (Cisco Express Forwarding).

─ Additionally, routers support functions such as NAT, QoS, and security features like ACLs, VPN termination, and basic firewalling.

─ Router creates (establishes) and terminates VPN tunnel.

─ Routing table = control plane, FIB = data plane (actual forwarding)

─ Packet forwarding flow: Routing Protocol → RIB → FIB → ASIC forwarding

Legacy notes for core understanding:

Hub:

─ Layer 1 device, One collision domain, Half-duplex, Uses CSMA/CD, If two devices transmit at the same time → collision.

─ Carrier Sense Multiple Access with Collision Detection (CSMA/CD) = Listen → Transmit (immediate) → Detect collisions = Collision Detection

─ 2 pc listen for silence and decide "Looks free, I'll transmit" at the same time, collision happen, so collision detection (CD) is required.

Bridge:

─ Early Layer 2 device, Usually connected two LAN segments, Reduced collision domains compared to hubs, Mostly historical now.

─ Layer 2 device (Usually has two ports/interfaces only); also possible software-based bridging.

─ LAN Segment A (HUB ← Bridge → (HUB) LAN Segment B

─ Bridge Learns/maintains MAC address table per interface/port; where each port connects to a LAN segment (many devices under same port, usig HUB).

─ Switch learns/maintains the same; usually each port connects to a single device.

─ Bridge Used to divide a large collision domain into smaller collision domains (typically two); collisions still exist within each segment (e.g., HUB-based segment).

─ Switch reduces collision domain further by making each port its own collision domain (one device per port in typical modern networks)/ microsegmentation).

─ Bridge/switch forwards or filters frames based on MAC addresses.

─ Floods unknown frames (except incoming port), like a switch does.

Bridge MAC Table

MAC Address Interface (Port)
AA:AA:AA    Port 1 (Segment A)
BB:BB:BB    Port 1 (Segment A)
CC:CC:CC    Port 2 (Segment B)
DD:DD:DD    Port 2 (Segment B)

2. Switch:

─ No CSMA/CD needed (full-duplex), Reduce collision by breaking “colision domains” per port; then each port have its own collision domain (removes collisions entirely).

─ Forwards data based on MAC addresses using the MAC address table (CAM table).

─ Learns MAC addresses dynamically by examining the source MAC of incoming frames.

─ Floods frames out all ports (except the incoming port) if the destination MAC is unknown, broadcast, or multicast.

─ Operates primarily at Layer 2, but multilayer switches can operate at Layer 3 (inter-VLAN routing and other L3 functions).

─ Supports VLANs to divide networks into separate broadcast domains.

─ Each VLAN have its own broadcast domain.

─ Uses trunk ports (802.1Q) to carry multiple VLANs between switches.

─ Supports STP (Spanning Tree Protocol) to prevent Layer 2 loops.

─ Supports security features such as port security, DHCP snooping, and Dynamic ARP Inspection (DAI).

─ Collision domain = per port, Broadcast domain = per VLAN

─ A switch is essentially a multiport hardware bridge, using the same logic but with greater efficiency and more advanced features.

Switch MAC Table (CAM Table)

MAC Address        Port
AA:AA:AA           Fa0/1
BB:BB:BB           Fa0/2
CC:CC:CC           Fa0/10
DD:DD:DD           Fa0/24

3. Access Point (AP):

─ An AP is essentially a bridge between: Wireless LAN <----> Wired LAN, But the wireless side behaves differently from switched Ethernet.

─ More then a Layer 2 bridge (2-port bridge), AP connects/bridges wireless clients (802.11) and wired LAN (802.3).

─ 802.11 wireless medium <--> AP <--> 802.3 Ethernet.

─ Forwards traffic based on MAC addresses, similar to a switch.

─ Between wireless clients data frames are Sent to specific client (MAC), BUT physically visible to all (shared air).

─ Provides wireless connectivity using radio frequencies (2.4 GHz, 5 GHz, and 6 GHz bands).

─ Supports multiple SSIDs, which can be mapped to different VLANs for network segmentation.

─ Uses encryption and authentication methods such as WPA2 and WPA3 for secure access.

─ Supports client roaming between APs within the same network (mobility).

─ AP can operate in standalone (autonomous) mode or lightweight mode with a Wireless LAN Controller (WLC).

─ In lightweight mode, APs use CAPWAP to communicate with the WLC.

─ Dynamically an AP can adjust transmit power and channel selection to reduce interference.

─ Wireless is a shared medium; all clients transmit over the air, so frames can be captured by others.

─ To protect data, Wi-Fi uses encryption (WPA2/WPA3); each session uses unique keys, so frames are unreadable without them.

─ Uses CSMA/CA (Collision Avoidance) instead of CSMA/CD (used in HUB/half duplex, transmit first, wait).

─ Listen first, Wait, Transmit when channel appears free (CSMA/CA)

─ CSMA/CA = Listen → Wait (random backoff period) → Transmit = Collision Avoidance (Wireless)

─ Two PCs listen for silence. Because each waits a random backoff period before transmitting, they are less likely to transmit at the same time. Collisions can still occur, but are minimized (Collision Avoidance).

─ CSMA/CD = Listen → Transmit (immediately if idle) → Detect collisions = Collision Detection (Hub / Half-duplex Ethernet)

─ Two PCs listen for silence and both decide "Looks free, I'll transmit" at the same time. A collision occurs, so collision detection (CD), backoff, and retransmission are required.

─ Many devices on same radio channel contention is common, Collisions minimized but technically half-duplex.

─ Wireless operates as a shared medium using CSMA/CA; a device cannot effectively transmit and receive simultaneously on the same channel (practical half-duplex behavior).

─ Most modern APs have at least: One 2.4 GHz radio & One 5 GHz radio.

─ So they can operate on 2 channels simultaneously example:

- Radio 1 (2.4 GHz) -> Channel 1
- Radio 2 (5 GHz)   -> Channel 36
- Radio 3 (6 GHz)   -> Channel 37 (more modern)

─ Clients on 2.4 GHz/band share the 2.4 GHz channel. Clients on 5 GHz/band share the 5 GHz channel.

─ Some enterprise APs have multiple radios per band.

01. AP Association Table (primary):

Client MAC Status / Radio

AA:AA:AA          Associated (2.4GHz)
BB:BB:BB          Associated (2.4GHz)
CC:CC:CC          Associated (5GHz)
DD:DD:DD          Associated (5GHz)

02. AP Bridging MAC Table (Layer 2 forwarding view)

MAC Address Direction

AA:AA:AA           Wireless side
BB:BB:BB           Wireless side
CC:CC:CC           Wired side (uplink switch)
DD:DD:DD           Wired side (uplink switch)

     Dedicated cable                 ┌────────┐
PC A ────────────────────────────────│        │
PC B ────────────────────────────────│ SWITCH │─────> ROUTER ──> INTERNET
PC C ────────────────────────────────│        │
                                     └────────┘

Full duplex (per port collision domain )

     Shared air media     ┌────┐    ┌────────┐
PC A ))))))))))))))))     │    │    │        │
PC B )))))))))))))))))))))│ AP │───>│ SWITCH │────> ROUTER ──> INTERNET
PC C ))))))))))))))))     │    │    │        |
                          └────┘    └────────┘
                          Behave like Half duplex (Large shared airtime domain)

SWITCH (Ethernet 802.3)              ACCESS POINT (Wi-Fi 802.11)
Dedicated link per PC                Shared wireless channel (One device talks at a time)
Full duplex (No collisions)          Half-duplex-like behavior due to shared medium (Contention possible)
Full bandwidth/Predictable speed     Divided among users / Variable speed
Wired                                Wireless

─ PC A sends Wi-Fi frame into air, All devices “hear” it (noisy environment), but only AP accepts it

─ Only one device effectively transmits at a time per channel

─ AP Maintains MAC table, not per physical port but for per wireless client association

─ Switch Maintains MAC table per port entry

─ AP acts like a translator (Wi-Fi ↔ Ethernet), AP bridges/connect Wireless clients ↔ Ethernet

─ Switch bridges Ethernet ↔ Ethernet (many ports)

─ Both forward data using MAC address but.

─ An AP does not use a full MAC/CAM table like a switch.

─ It maintains an association table of connected clients and performs basic Layer 2 bridging:

─  If destination MAC is an associated client → forward locally

-─ If destination MAC unknown → flood over wireless medium

notes:

Bridge = MAC per segment
Switch = MAC per port
AP = MAC per client + association + bridge/connect between WiFi and Ethernet

4. Server:

─ Provides services for clients; such as web hosting, email, file storage, and authentication.

─ Often runs virtualization to host multiple servers on one machine.

─ Provides centralized services like DNS, DHCP, or Active Directory.

5. Endpoint:

─ Any user device such as PCs, laptops, phones, or IoT devices that connect to a network.

─ Endpoint Often requires security measures like antivirus and access controls.

─ Endpoint Can be managed by Mobile Device Management (MDM) or Endpoint Detection and Response (EDR) software for enhanced security.

6. Firewall:

─ Monitors and controls incoming/outgoing network traffic based on security rules.

─ Provides protection against unauthorized access, threats, and attacks.

─ Can be hardware─based (physical appliance) or software─based (host firewall).

─ Supports features like Stateful Packet Inspection (SPI) and application─layer filtering for deeper security.

[A stateful device remembers information about ongoing connections]
[Tracks connection state (e.g. TCP handshake: SYN, SYN-ACK, ACK) and allows only packets that belong to a valid, established session initiated by the client]
[Blocks unsolicited inbound packets that do not match any existing connection state] 

Note: Stateless firewall → Does not remember connection state; it filters each packet independently using predefined rules only.

7. WSA:

─ Improves web traffic performance using proxy caching.

─ A user visit a website for the first time. The WSA fetches the website from the origin server and caches the content locally.

─ another user requests the same website, the WSA serves the cached version of the page from local storage, reducing the load time and WAN traffic.

─ Over time,the WSA updates its cache based on the TTL and Cache─Control headers sent by the website.

─ Also provides content filtering, malware protection, and URL categorization in enterprise networks.

8. PoE:

─ Supports auto─negotiation to supply only the required power to connected devices,over Ethernet cables (Cat5e or higher).

─ Common standards include IEEE 802.3af (PoE), 802.3at (PoE+), and 802.3bt (PoE++).

9. Network Controller :

         ┌──────────────────────┐
         │     [User / Admin]   │
         └──────────────────────┘
                   │  Automation tools, CLI scripts, Orchestration, GUI apps,, OpenStack, Kubernetes;
                   │ ▼
▲  Northbound APIs —  REST API, gRPC(framework); protocols: HTTP, RESTCONF, WebSocket; data formats (JSON/XML). //REST(guidline)
                   │
         ┌───────────────────────────┐
         │  Network Controller       │
         │    ─ Central brain of SDN │
         │    ─ Policy & automation  │
         └───────────────────────────┘
▼
▲ Southbound APIs — protocols: OpenFlow, NETCONF, SNMP, gNMI; data formats (XML/protobuf and others)
                    │
    ┌───────────────┬────────────────────┐
    │               │                    │
[ Switch ]     [ Router ]         [ Firewall ]
(L2/L3)        (Routing)           (Security)

─ Enables automation and centralized management of network devices in SDN environments.

─ Enforces policies, provides network analytics, and manages configuration, monitoring, and troubleshooting remotely.

─ Supports REST APIs for automated access to configuration, monitoring, and integration.

Note:

REST is not a protocol itself, but a set of architectural guidelines for designing applications that are stateless, scalable, and flexible.
POST, GET, PUT, PATCH, and DELETE are standard HTTP methods used to manage resources.
Create, Read, Update, Delete = CRUD (Action Concept)

Northbound API:

─ A conceptual interface on the controller for external apps and monitoring tools, through which the controller receives instructions.

─ The controller uses software APIs (like REST API, gRPC, WebSocket) to expose the northbound interface for external applications (orchestration tools, monitoring systems, automation scripts) to communicate with it.

─  In SDN terminology:

          - Northbound API: REST API, gRPC, WebSocket.

          - data format: JSON (most common), XML (less common), Protocol Buffers (for gRPC).

           - data models: Application-specific models (no strict standard like YANG).

- Applications that USE northbound APIs: OpenStack, Kubernetes, Nagios, automation/orchestration scripts.

Southbound API:

─ Another interface on the controller for end devices, through which the controller retrieves data and sends/pushes instructions to network devices for configuration and control.

─ The controller uses southbound protocols (like OpenFlow, NETCONF, RESTCONF, SNMP, gNMI) to implement the southbound interface.

─ The controller translates and executes instructions by configuring the actual network devices (switches, routers, firewalls) using these protocols.

─ In SDN terminology:

         -  Southbound API: OpenFlow, NETCONF, RESTCONF, gNMI and SNMP.

         - data format: XML (NETCONF), JSON (RESTCONF), Protocol Buffers (gNMI), binary/structured formats (OpenFlow), ASN.1/BER (SNMP)

         - data models: YANG models (NETCONF/RESTCONF/gNMI), vendor-specific MIBs (SNMP)

Notes:

─ “Northbound/Southbound API” is the "concept interface", and OpenFlow/NETCONF/SNMP/gNMI (southbound) or REST, RESTCONF, gRPC, WebSocket (northbound) are the "protocols that implement it".

─ OpenStack is an orchestration platform (IaaS control plane), not an SDN controller; it can integrate with SDN controllers via northbound APIs.

─ Neutron provides network abstraction, has its own APIs, and may exhibit controller-like behavior for virtual networks (backend-dependent), but is not a full SDN controller.

─ From an SDN perspective, OpenStack operates as an application-layer system communicating with the SDN controller.

─ OpenFlow is historically important for SDN but much less common in modern enterprise networks.


Network Topologies: 

 Three─tier, Spine─leaf, Two─tier, WAN, SOHO, On─premise, Cloud computing

Three─Tier─network:


        [ Internet / ISP  ]
                 │ 
        [ Edge Router / Gateway ]
                 │  (WAN, NAT, Firewall)
        [ Core Switches ]
                 │
                 │  (High─speed backbone, fast L3 switching, simple routing between major network blocks)
                 │
        [ Distribution Switches ]
                 │
                 │  (Routing between VLANs, ACLs, QoS, Policy Enforcement)
                 │
        [ Access Switches ]
                 │
                 │(VLAN tagging, Port Security, PoE, 802.1X Authentication)
                 │ 
        [ End Users / Devices ]


                       ┌──────────────────────┐
                       │       Internet       │
                       └──────────┬───────────┘
                                  │
                   ┌──────────────┴─────────────┐
                   │Edge Router / Firewall Pair │
                   │(WAN, NAT, Security, IPS)   │
                   └────────┬──────────────┬────┘
                            │              │
                 ┌──────────────┐    ┌──────────────┐
                 │   Core 1     │────│   Core 2     │
                 │  (L3 Switch) │ HA │  (L3 Switch) │ CORE LAYER (Backbone)
                 └──────┬───┬───┘    └──────┬───┬───┘
                        │   │               │   │
                        │   │               │   │
         ┌──────────────────────┐       ┌─────────────────────┐
         │     Distribution1    │       │Distribution2        │
         │      (L3 Switch)     │───────│ (L3 Switch)         │ DISTRIBUTION LAYER (Policy)
         │ Inter─VLAN, ACL/QoS  │  HA   │ Inter─VLAN, ACL/QoS │
         └──────┬───┬───────────┘       └──────┬───┬──────────┘
                │   │                          │   │
           ┌────────────┐   ┌────────────┐   ┌────────────┐
           │ Access SW1 │   │ Access SW2 │   │ Access SW3 │
           │ VLAN / PoE │   │ 802.1X     │   │ Port Sec   │ ACCESS LAYER (Edge Access)
           └──────┬─────┘   └──────┬─────┘   └──────┬─────┘
                  │                │                │
                  │                │                │
              PCs / Phones      Printers        APs / IoT

communication flow: Access1 ←→ Distribution1 ←→ Core ←→ Distribution2 ←→ Access2

Note:

- Although core─core and dist─dist interconnect, communication still happen (Access1 → Distribution1 → Core → Distribution2 → Access2).
- Inteconnect helps with Redundancy/HA/locad balance, if one core or one distribution fails, your Access1 → Access2 communication still works.
- Interconnection prevent a single point of failure, they don’t alter the normal path.

core layer(backbone):

─ Internet access is typically handled by the edge router(gateway), not directly in the core.

─ No (NAT, ACL, VLAN routing or firewall) these are done at distribution layer to keep the core fast.

─ Fast, high─speed, high─capacity

— like a motorway, optimized for speed and reliability.

─ Ensures continuous connectivity and uptime ( high availability, redundancy).

─ Routing between different parts of an organisation/building/dc/Distribution layer, not local VLAN.

─ Connectivity between different distribution switches and the edge (gateway).

distribution layer:

─ Runs Layer 2 and Layer 3 technologies, aggregate "Access switch" and VLAN Traffic.

─ Policy enforcement ( ACLs, QoS, Firewall, Routing)

─ Routing (inter─VLAN routing)

─ Route summarization (aggregation of routes).

─ Connects Core and Access Layer

access layer:

─ data forwarding, Port Security, VLANs(basic), Power over Ethernet (PoE), 802.1X for authentication.

─ Endpoint connectivity (PCs, printers, phones, APs, cameras).

─ Edge of the network where devices connect.

Two─tier network:

─ Collapsed Core (Core Layer and the Distribution Layer)

─ Access Layer

Spine─and─leaf architecture:

Leaf1 → Spine → Leaf2

                         ┌──────────────────────┐

                        │  │  │              │  │  │

                    │   │     │              │           │  │

                 │  │                │  │                │  │
                 1  2                1  2                1  2
           ┌─────┴──┴──────┐   ┌─────┴──┴──────┐   ┌─────┴──┴──────┐
           │    Leaf 1     │   │    Leaf 2     │   │    Leaf 3     │
           │   (L2/L3 GW)  │   │   (L2/L3 GW)  │   │   (L2/L3 GW)  │
           │ VLAN / VXLAN  │   │ VLAN / VXLAN  │   │ VLAN / VXLAN  │  LEAF LAYER (Access + Gateway)
           │ ACL / Policy  │   │ ACL / Policy  │   │ ACL / Policy  │
           └───┬─────┬─────┘   └────┬─────┬────┘   └────┬─────┬────┘
               │     │              │     │             │     │
               │     │              │     │             │     │
            Spine1 Spine2        Spine1 Spine2      Spine1 Spine2      
               │     │              │     │             │     │
               │     │              │     │             │     │
               Servers              Storage             Endpoints
             (Bare metal/VMs)      (SAN/NAS)           (Apps/Users)

SPINE:

- Used in modern data centers, forms the high-speed L3 backbone of the fabric.

- Pure L3 transit forwarding (for point-to-point link subnets) → no VLANs, SVIs, MAC tables, VRFs, endpoint awareness (simple model)

- Each spine connects ALL leaf switches together, typically no spine-to-spine links.

- ECMP-based forwarding across multiple equal-cost paths (a leaf can transfer data to another leaf via all spines at the same time).

- Per-flow load balancing (single flow = one path, multiple flows = spread accross spine)

- High availability and fault tolerance (no single core device dependency).

- Runs protocols like: BGP, OSPF, IS-IS (mostly BGP).

- flow: [src/dst IP + ports(443) + protocol(TCP/UDP)] dowloading a large file is single flow, browsing webpage is (many small flows) HTTP,HTML,CSS,JS etc each are separate TCP session.

LEAF:

- Often used in virtualized / SDN environments to support dynamic workloads, VMs, and services.

- Connects end devices like servers, storage, and virtual machines (VMs), occasionally access switches.

- Layer 2 switching (VLANs, MAC learning via CAM table).

- Layer 3 routing (inter-VLAN routing, acts as default gateway using SVI/IRB).

- Provides policy enforcement (ACLs, segmentation, VRFs in modern designs).

- Performs routing decisions locally, then forwards traffic to spine layer for inter-leaf communication, external networks, and other subnets.

- Participates in ECMP forwarding across multiple spine paths (ECMP), improving bandwidth utilization and preventing bottlenecks.

- Each leaf connects to ALL spines (no direct leaf-to-leaf links).

- Runs protocols like:

→ BGP, OSPF, IS-IS (Underlay)

→ BGP EVPN (control-plane for VXLAN)

→ VXLAN is a data-plane encapsulation protocol used to carry Layer 2 traffic over a Layer 3 IP network, typically implemented on leaf switches acting as VTEPs.

→ VXLAN encapsulates Ethernet frames inside UDP/IP packets.

NOTE:

- Regardless of which leaf switch a server connects to, the number of hops between any two servers is always the same:
→ Leaf → Spine → Leaf (typically 2 hops)
- This ensures predictable latency and consistent performance across the fabric.


Policy vs. Policy Enforcement:

Policy:

- VoIP traffic must have high priority

- Its the intention or requirement (what we want)

Configuration:

- Setting up device/interface parameters (e.g., enabling a port, assigning an IP address).

- Configuring interface, does NOT enforce a policy

- Commands applied to a device.

Policy Enforcement:

- Applying configuration that implements a policy.

- Create and apply QoS to prioritize VoIP traffic.

- Create and apply ACLs to block unauthorized traffic.

- Configure 802.1X to require user authentication.

- Configure VLANs/VRFs to enforce segmentation.

Policy 1 ("VoIP must have priority")

      ↓

Policy Enforcement (QoS configuration implementing that requirement)  

class-map VOICE
policy-map PRIORITIZE-VOICE
service-policy output PRIORITIZE-VOICE


Policy 2 ("Guests must not reach internal servers")

     ↓

Policy Enforcement (ACLs, Firewalls, VRFs, VLANs, QoS, 802.1X, etc implementing that requirement)

ip access-list extended GUEST-BLOCK
deny ip 192.168.100.0 0.0.0.255 10.0.0.0 0.255.255.255
permit ip any any
interface VLAN100
ip access-group GUEST-BLOCK in

So policy enforcement is configuration, but not all configuration is policy enforcement.

For example:

interface Gi0/1
ip address 10.1.1.1/24
no shutdown

That's configuration, but it's not really enforcing a policy.

1. What are the main network components in a modern network?

─ Routers

─ Switches

─ Servers

─ Access Points

─ Network Controllers

─ Endpoints

─ Firewalls

2. What is the primary function of a router?

─ Separates networks

─ Stops broadcast traffic

─ Forwards packets based on IP addresses

3. How does a router determine where to send packets?

─ Uses a routing table (RIB/FIB).

─ Selects the best path based on routing protocols or static routes

4. What is the difference between the RIB and FIB?

─ RIB (Routing Information Base) is the routing table in the control plane

─ FIB (Forwarding Information Base) is used in the data plane for fast packet forwarding

─ FIB is built from the RIB using CEF

5. What is CEF (Cisco Express Forwarding)?

─ A Layer 3 forwarding design/structure/mechanism/architechture

─ Builds the FIB and adjacency table

─ Enables fast, hardware-based packet forwarding

6. What additional services can a router provide?

─ NAT (Network Address Translation)

─ QoS (Quality of Service) [QoS is traffic management ─Example: voice, video, web, file transfer] (Classification, Marking, Queuing, Congestion management, Policing, Shaping)

─ VPN termination (Remote user ──(encrypted tunnel)── Router ── Internal network

─ ACLs and basic firewall functions

7. Which routing protocols are commonly supported by routers?

─ OSPF

─ EIGRP

─ BGP

8. What is the main function of a switch?

─ Forwards frames based on MAC addresses

─ Uses a MAC (CAM) address table

9. What happens when a switch receives a frame with an unknown destination MAC address?

─ Floods the frame out all ports except the incoming port

10. How do switches reduce collisions?

─ Each switch port is its own collision domain

─ Full─duplex communication prevents collisions

11. What is a collision domain?

─ A network segment where collisions can occur

─ In switches: one per port

─ In hubs: one shared domain

12. What is a broadcast domain?

─ A group of devices that receive broadcast frames

─ Each VLAN is a separate broadcast domain

─ ARP Requests, DHCP Discover, DHCP Request (Reply’s are unicast not broadcast).

13. What is the difference between a hub, bridge, and switch?

─ Hub: Layer 1, broadcasts everything, single collision domain

─ Bridge: Layer 2, splits collision domains (few ports)

─ Switch: Multiport bridge, one collision domain per port

14. At which OSI layers can switches operate?

─ Layer 2 (MAC─based forwarding)

─ Layer 3 (routing between VLANs)

15. Why are VLANs used on switches?

─ Network segmentation

─ Improved security

─ Reduced broadcast domains

16. What is a trunk port?

─ A port that carries multiple VLANs

─ Uses 802.1Q tagging

17. What is inter-VLAN routing?

─ Communication between different VLANs

─ Requires a Layer 3 device (router or multilayer switch)

18. What is STP and why is it needed?

─ Blocks redundant paths while keeping them available as backup links.

─ STP (Spanning Tree Protocol) prevents Layer 2 switching loops.

19. What is a server?

─ A device that provides services to clients on a network

20. What common services do servers provide?

─ Web hosting

─ Email

─ File storage

─ Authentication

21. Why is virtualization commonly used on servers?

─ Run multiple virtual servers on one physical machine

─ Better hardware utilization

─ Scalability and isolation

22. What centralized services often run on servers?

─ DNS

─ DHCP

─ Active Directory

23. What is DHCP?

─ Automatically assigns IP addresses to devices

24. What is DNS?

─ Resolves domain names to IP addresses

25. What is the purpose of ARP?

─ Maps IP address to MAC address

26. What is a default gateway?

─ The router or Layer 3 interface used to reach other networks

27. What is the role of an access point?

─ provide wireless conectivity for wireless clients.

─ Connects wireless clients to a wired LAN

─ Bridges wireless (802.11) and wired networks (802.3)

28. What features do modern APs support?

─ Multiple SSIDs

─ WPA2 and WPA3 encryption

─ Client roaming

─ Multiple radios/bands (2.4 GHz, 5 GHz, 6 GHz)

─ They can operate on 2-3 channels simultaneously, channel per radio/band .

─ Dynamic channel selection (automatic channel assignment)

29. What is an SSID?

─ Service Set Identifier (SSID)

─ The name of a wireless network (e.g., "Office-WiFi", "Guest-WiFi")

─ Used by clients to identify and connect to a wireless network

─ Can be mapped to a VLAN

30. What is CAPWAP?

─ Control And Provisioning of Wireless Access Points (CAPWAP)

─ Protocol used between APs and a WLC (Wireless LAN Controller)

─ Used in lightweight AP mode

─ Allows centralized management and configuration of APs by the WLC

─ Carries control traffic and client data traffic between the AP and WLC in centralized deployments.

─ Carries only control traffic when APs operate in FlexConnect mode with Local Switching enabled.

31. How are APs managed in enterprise networks?

─ Centrally using a Wireless LAN Controller (WLC)

─ Or standalone in small networks

32. What is roaming in wireless networks?

─ When a client moves between APs without losing connection

33. What is the difference between CSMA/CD and CSMA/CA?

─ CSMA/CD: Used in half-duplex Ethernet/hubs. Two PCs listen, both may transmit at the same time → collision occurs → collision detection.

─ CSMA/CA: Used in Wi-Fi. Two PCs listen and wait a random period before transmitting → less likely to transmit at the same time → collision avoidance.

34. Why does Wi-Fi behave like half-duplex?

─ Shared medium (air)

─ Only one device can effectively transmit at a time

35. What is an endpoint?

─ Any device that connects to the network

─ PCs, laptops, phones, IoT devices

36. Why do endpoints require security controls?

─ They are common attack targets

─ Can be managed using MDM or EDR solutions

37. What is the function of a firewall?

─ Controls inbound and outbound traffic

─ Protects against unauthorized access

38. What types of firewalls exist?

─ Hardware (physical appliances)

─ Software (host─based firewalls)

39. What is Stateful Packet Inspection (SPI)?

─ Firewall tracks active connections/sessions.

─ Makes filtering decisions based on session state.

─ Stateless Firewall (Basic ACL): Each packet is inspected independently inbound/outbound. ➝ “Does this packet match my rule?”

─ Stateful Firewall (SPI): Creates and tracks active sessions/connections in a state table. “Does this packet belong to an established, valid session?” Blocks everything else.

─ All modern firewalls implement SPI, including: Cisco firewalls (ASA, Firepower), Palo Alto Networks next─gen firewalls, Fortinet FortiGate.

─ SPI = Stateful Packet Inspection

40. What is an ACL?

─ Access Control List

─ Filters traffic based on rules (IP, port, protocol)

41. What is the difference between stateless and stateful filtering?

─ Stateless: Each packet checked independently

─ Stateful: Tracks session state and allows return traffic

42. What is NAT?

─ Translates private IP addresses to public IP addresses

─ Enables internet access for internal devices

43. What is QoS used for?

─ Prioritizes important traffic (e.g., VoIP, video)

─ Manages congestion and bandwidth

44. What is a Web Security Appliance (WSA)?

─ A proxy device that inspects and optimizes web traffic

45. How does WSA improve performance?

─ Caches web content locally

─ Serves cached content to users

─ Reduces WAN bandwidth usage

46. What security functions does WSA provide?

─ Content filtering

─ Malware protection

─ URL categorization

47. What is PoE?

─ Supplies electrical power over Ethernet cables

─ Uses Cat5e or higher cables

48. How does PoE determine how much power to deliver?

─ CDP Auto─negotiation with the connected device and

─ LLDP/LLDP-MED (detailed negotiation)

49. What are common PoE standards?

─ IEEE 802.3af (PoE)

─ IEEE 802.3at (PoE+)

─ IEEE 802.3bt (PoE++)

50. What is a network controller?

─ Central control point of a Software─Defined Network (SDN)

─ Acts as the “brain” of the network

51. What are the main functions of a network controller?

─ Centralized configuration

─ Policy enforcement

─ Automation

─ Monitoring and analytics

52. What is a Northbound API?

─ Interface between controller and applications

─ Used by automation tools and orchestration systems

─ Example: REST API, HTTP, RESTCONF, WebSocket, gRPC

53. What is a Southbound API?

─ Interface between controller and network devices

─ Used to configure switches, routers, firewalls

─ Examples: OpenFlow, NETCONF, SNMP

54. Is REST a protocol?

─ No

─ REST is a set of design principles

─ Uses HTTP methods like GET, POST, PUT, DELETE

55. What is a Three─Tier network architecture?

─ Core layer

─ Distribution layer

─ Access layer

56. What is the role of the Core layer?

─ High─speed backbone

─ Fast and reliable data forwarding

─ Connects distribution switches and edge routers

functions:

─ High─speed Layer 3 switching (ip routing, but at switching speed (FIB/CEF))

─ Fast routing (e.g., OSPF, EIGRP, BGP)

─ High availability (HA) & redundancy

─ Failover

─ Load balancing (ECMP)

note:

Layer 3 switching is performed by a multilayer (L3) switch. Routing is handled by the control plane (CPU), which runs routing protocols and builds the RIB.

Packet forwarding is performed in hardware (ASIC) using CEF/FIB entries, enabling wire-speed IP forwarding(extremely fast ). After the initial routing decision (or CEF population), forwarding information is installed in hardware. Subsequent packets are forwarded directly in hardware using the FIB/CEF table, similar to how a Layer 2 switch uses a MAC address table. So logically, it behaves like a router, but forwards packets like a switch using hardware tables.

57. What functions are avoided in the Core layer?

─ NAT

─ Firewall

─ ACLs

─ VLAN routing

58. What is the role of the Distribution layer?

─ Aggregates access layer traffic

─ Enforces policies ( ACLs, QoS )

59. What functions run at the Distribution layer?

─ Inter─VLAN routing

─ Policy enforcement ( ACLs, QoS )

60. What is the role of the Access layer?

─ Connects end devices to the network

61. What features are commonly found at the Access layer?

─ VLANs

─ Port security

─ PoE

─ 802.1X authentication (YES)

62. What is a two─tier network?

─ Collapsed core design

─ Core and Distribution layers combined

63. Where is two─tier architecture commonly used?

─ Small to medium networks

─ Campus networks

64. Where is spine─leaf architecture commonly used?

─ Modern data centers

─ Cloud and virtualized environments

65. What is the role of the Spine layer?

─ High─speed Layer 3 routing

─ Connects all leaf switches

─ No spine─to─spine connections

66. What is the role of the Leaf layer?

─ Connects servers, storage, VMs, containers

─ Performs L2 switching and local routing

─ No leaf─to─leaf connection switches directly.

67. Why is spine─leaf highly scalable?

─ Equal─cost multipath (ECMP)

─ Predictable latency

─ No single point of failure

68. What is ECMP?

─ Equal-Cost Multi-Path

─ Uses multiple equal routes for load balancing.

69. What is a key advantage of spine─leaf over three─tier?

─ Consistent hop count between any two servers

─ Better east─west traffic handling

70. What is east-west vs north-south traffic?

─ East-west: Server-to-server (inside data center)

─ North-south: Client-to-internet traffic

71. Why is the spine layer kept simple?

─ To ensure fast, predictable forwarding

─ Avoids complex policies and state

72. What is VXLAN?

─  Encapsulation protocol

─  Extends Layer 2 over Layer 3 networks, allowing logical Layer-2 networks (LANs) to be stretched across a routed Layer-3 spine-leaf fabric.

─  Allows the same logical Layer-2 network (LANs) to span multiple physical locations across the Layer-3 fabric.

─  Common in spine-leaf architectures.
→ VXLAN is a data-plane encapsulation protocol used to carry Layer 2 traffic over a Layer 3 IP network, typically implemented on leaf switches which acting as VTEPs.

→ VXLAN encapsulates Ethernet frames inside UDP/IP packets.

Key details:

─ VXLAN = Virtual Extensible LAN

─ Operates over Layer 3 networks (IP underlay)

─ Creates Layer 2 “overlay” networks over Layer 3 infrastructure

VTEP (VXLAN Tunnel Endpoint):

─ Device that performs VXLAN encapsulation/decapsulation

─ Usually leaf switches or hypervisors

Encapsulation format:

Original Ethernet frame (data) (L2)
       ↓
VXLAN header (VXLAN Network Identifier – VNI e.g 10010)
       ↓
UDP header (destination port 4789) (L4)
       ↓
IP header (underlay routing) (L3)
       ↓
Outer Ethernet frame (l2)

VNI (VXLAN Network Identifier):

─ 24-bit identifier

─ Supports ~16 million logical Layer 2 segments

─  additionally this eliminates VLAN limitation (4096 VLANs)

Underlay vs Overlay:

─ Underlay: physical IP network (spine-leaf fabric)

─ Overlay: VXLAN logical Layer 2 networks carried over underlay

Key function:

- Extends Layer 2 segments across Layer 3 boundaries

- Enables scalable multi-tenant networks in data centers

- Decouples logical network from physical topology

73. What is a VTEP?

─ VXLAN Tunnel Endpoint

─ Encapsulates and decapsulates VXLAN traffic

74. What is microsegmentation?

─ Switch = breaks collision domains → (basic microsegmentation)

─ SDN/security = controls traffic between workloads → (modern microsegmentation) Layer-3-7.

─ Very fine network segmentation

─ Often implemented using VLANs, ACLs, or SDN

75. What is the difference between policy and configuration?

─ Policy: Intent (what you want)

─ Configuration: Commands applied, prepare device

─ Enforcement: Applying config to meet policy, take action.

Cabling: Single─mode fiber, multimode fiber, copper

Single-mode Fiber

─ Smaller core diameter (~8–10 microns).

─ Uses longer wavelengths (1310 nm, 1550 nm).

─ Transmits light in a single mode (straight path).

─ Supports long-distance transmission (40 km+).

─ Typically uses lasers as light source (highly focused beam).

Multimode Fiber

─ Larger core diameter (~50–62.5 microns).

─ Uses shorter wavelengths (850 nm, 1300 nm).

─ Transmits light in multiple modes (bouncing paths).

─ Suitable for shorter distances (up to ~2 km).

─ Typically uses LEDs or VCSELs as light source (wider light spread).

---------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------
Feature        | Single-mode Fiber           | Multimode Fiber
---------------|-----------------------------|----------------------------------------------------------
Core           | Smaller(~8–10 microns)      | Larger core (~50–62.5 microns)
Wavelength     | Longer (1310 nm, 1550 nm)   | Shorter (850 nm, 1300 nm)
Light path     | Single and straight         | Multiple bouncing/reflection paths
Light source   | Laser (highly focused)      | LED / VCSEL
Distance       | Long-distance 40 km+        | Short-distance up to ~2 km
Signal quality | Lower attenuation           | Higher attenuation
Use case       | ISP backbone, WAN links,    | LAN, data centers, intra-building connections
Cost           | Higher cost (transceivers   | Lower cost (cheaper optics and installation)


Copper Cabling

    ─ Uses electrical signals for data transmission.

    ─ Typically used for shorter distances (up to 100 meters for Ethernet).

    ─ Supports standards like Cat5e, Cat6, Cat6a, Cat7.


Interface Errors (Layer 1/2 physical issues)

✔ Input Errors (counter):

    ─ For INCOMING (received) traffic, it's a general problem counter (not a single specific error type).  

    ─ Also known as "Umbrella counter".

    ─ Input errors = "overall total error count (CRC/FCS + runt + other receive issues)"

    Includes:

      •  CRC/FCS errors

      •  Runt frames

      •  Alignment errors

      •  Sometimes other physical-layer receive errors (platform-dependent)

   caused by:

      • Physical layer issues (bad cable, interference, weak signal)

      • Speed mismatch (less common but possible)

      • Faulty NIC / transceiver

      • General L1/L2 link instability

✔ FCS / CRC Errors (counter):

     ─ Frame Check Sequence (FCS) / Cyclic Redundancy Check (CRC) failures

     ─ Means frame is corrupted during reception (not transmission)

caused by:

     • Bad/old cables

     • Electrical interference (EMI)

     • Faulty NIC

     • Network congestion (rare / usually not primary cause)

✔ Runt Frames (< 64 bytes) (counter):

    ─ Frames smaller than minimum Ethernet size

    ─ Frame is incomplete / cut off

caused by:

   • Cable damage

   • Signal loss

   • Faulty hardware

   • Collisions (especially in bad half-duplex links)


✔  Alignment Errors (counter)

    ─ Frame received is not properly aligned to byte boundaries

caused by:

    • Physical layer issues

    • Bad NIC / driver issues

    • Noise / interference on cable

    • Sometimes duplex mismatch (indirectly)

Note:

   ─ Often included inside Input Errors (platform-dependent)

    ✔ Output Errors (counter)

         •  Errors occurring on TRANSMITTING side (outgoing traffic)

includes:

    • Collisions (in half-duplex)

    • Interface resets

    • Buffer/queue drops

    • Late collisions (sometimes counted here depending on device)

Key notes:

    • Input errors = receive-side issues (local interface)
    • Output errors = transmit-side issues (remote interface)


✔ Collisions (counter)

    •   Typically occur in half-duplex environments

    •  Caused mainly by duplex mismatch (one side full/auto and other side half-duplex)

    •   Should NOT exist in full-duplex networks (if seen → indicates misconfiguration/mismatch)

    •  Collisions counter always increases if mismatch exists or legacy half-duplex is used.

✔ Late Collisions (counter)

    •   Collisions detected after first 64 bytes of frame

caused by:

    • Cable too long (exceeds Ethernet limits)

    • Faulty equipment

    • Duplex mismatch (sometimes)

     • Indicates timing/synchronization problem on link


 ✔ Duplex mismatch

 Result:

     •  Collisions increase (on half-duplex side)

     •  CRC/FCS and input errors may also increase


✔ Speed mismatch

  Results:

     •  Input errors may increase

     •  CRC/FCS errors

     •  Link instability / drops

─ Throughput degradation

Note:

─ Speed mismatch alone does NOT create collisions
─ Collisions are specifically a HALF-DUPLEX phenomenon

## Quick Mapping (Exam Memory Rule)

✔  Input errors = bad RECEIVED frames (CRC, runt, alignment) / Speed mismatch / general hardware issues

✔ CRC/FCS errors = Bad cable / interference / bad NIC

✔ Runt frames = Signal loss / damaged frames / hardware fault

✔ Output errors = problems sending frames (collisions, drops, buffer issues)

✔ Collisions = Duplex mismatch (half-duplex issue)

✔ Late collisions= Cable too long / timing issue / faulty link

# Interface status

Administratively shut down ─ ADMIN DOWN / DOWN
error─disabled ─             DOWN / DOWN
speed mismatch ─             DOWN / DOWN
duplex mismatch ─            up/up, up/down

TCP and UDP:

UDP:

─ The application sends small individual messages (chunks) to UDP.

─ UDP treats each message as a separate "datagram" and sends it as─is (one datagram per message).

─ Sends data packet(datagrams) individually. other side's application receive individual packet.

─ No sequence ordering, no error correction, no delivery guarantee

─ Fast, connectionless, and unreliable

─ Suitable for real─time applications (e.g. voice, video, gaming) where speed matters more than reliability

─ Smaller header (8 bytes) ─ light overhead

─ Has error detection via checksum, but no correction

TCP:

─ The application sends a continuous stream of data to TCP.

─ TCP takes that stream and breaks it into "segments" suitable for transmission.

─ sends data as a continuous stream (the other side’s application receives the same continuous stream of data).

─ Provides sequencing, flow control, windowing, error─checking, and error correction.

─ Slower but reliable.

─ connection─oriented: ensures data integrity and in─order delivery.

─ 3 way hand shake (SYN,SYN─ACK, ACK) to establish a connection.

─ Larger header (20 bytes) ─ more overhead

Note:
✔ Sequencing:

─  Segments  numbering, so receiver can reorder data correctly

✔ Flow Control:

─ Prevents sender from overwhelming receiver

─ Receiver tells sender how much it can handle

✔ Windowing:

─ Windowing is a flow control mechanism that allows the sender to send multiple packets before requiring ACK.

─ "window size" is the amount of data (in bytes) the receiver can accept and buffer before sending an ACK.

─ Uses “sliding window” to adjust dynamically

✔ Error Checking:

─ Detects corrupted segments using checksum

─ Receiver DISCARD the segment (does NOT deliver to application)

─ Receiver does NOT acknowledge that segment (no ACK or no valid ACK progression)

✔ Error Correction:

─ Error checking happens first, and error correction is the result of it

─ Sender assumes loss (because ACK is missing or not progressing)

─ Sender retransmits the missing/corrupted segment after timeout

─ Achieved using ACK + retransmission mechanism

255.254.0.0 /15

255.255.128.0 /17
255.255.192.0 /18

─ 256 is the total number of IP in each octet 2^8─256

IPv6 addressing and prefix:

Type of address:

     •  Unicast: One─to─one → Global Unicast, Unique Local, Link─Local, Loopback, IPv4─mapped IPv6

     •  Multicast: One─to─many → Not unicast — these identify a group of interfaces. Traffic sent to a multicast address is delivered to all members of the group.

     •  Anycast: One─to─nearest → An unicast IP is assigned to multiple interfaces (usually on different nodes). Anycast is technically not an unicast address — even though they look like unicast addresses. Packets go to the nearest node one based on routing.

Global Unicast:

Public IP, Routable on the internet.

2000::/3  block extends from (2000:: to 3FFF::)
2001::1, 2002::1, 2001:0db8:85a3:0000:0000:8a2e:0370:7334

Unique Local:

Private IP, between subnets, not routable on internet.

FC00::/7 block extends from (FC00:: to FDFF::) 

FD00::1, FD00::1234, FD12:3456:789A:0000:0000:0000:0000:0010
FC00::/8 /is reserved, range (FC00:: ─ FCFF::). so FC00::1 and FC00::2 are valid but not usable in practice, # valid usuable range FD00::/8 to FDFF::/8

Multicast:

One─to─many communication (Neighboury discovery protocol[NDP], routing protocols, video streaming and system management.

FF00::/8 

FF02::1 (all nodes on link)
FF02::2 (all routers on link)
FF02::1:FFc0:337e (solicited-node multicast group)

Link─Local:

Local subnet (neighbor discovery, router advertisements.)

FE80::/10 block extends from (FE80:: to FEBF::)

FE80::1,  FEBF::1, FE80::123:123:123, FEBF::123:123:123

Anycast:

2001:4860:4860::8888  (Google DNS)
2606:4700:4700::1111  (Cloudflare DNS)

─ Anycast enables one─to─any (nearest) communication (used in services like DNS, CDNs, and load balancing).

─ A normal Unicast IP address becomes an Anycast address when the SAME IP is assigned to multiple routers/servers in different geographic locations.

─ ISPs allow the same Unicast IP address to be advertised from multiple locations.

─ The prefix/route is advertised from multiple locations using BGP.

─ BGP installs multiple paths in the routing table, and routers select the best path based on routing metrics (shortest AS path, lowest cost, policy, etc.).

─ Traffic is automatically routed to the topologically nearest instance (not necessarily geographically nearest).

─ Anycast looks like Unicast, but delivers to the nearest available copy of that address.

─ There is NO special IPv6 Anycast range. It uses normal Global Unicast addresses, (2000::/3).

─ 8.8.8.8 (IPv4) and 2001:4860:4860::8888 (IPv6) are Anycast DNS servers.

multicast group:

─ “Multicast group” means the collection of hosts/interfaces that are using (listening to) a multicast address.

─ For essential Neighbor Discovery and routing, certain multicast groups are automatically joined by any IPv6─enabled interface. Typical Windows/Linux PC Automatically join 3 group.

FF02::1             (All Nodes)
FF02::1:FFDD:CCDD   (one from link-local address, Solicited-Node Multicast Address)
FF02::1:FF00:0100   (one from global address, Solicited-Node Multicast Address)

Examples:

─ FF02::1   (All─Nodes) Every IPv6─enabled interface automatically belongs to it.
─ FF02::2   (All─Routers) Only routers join it.
─ FF02::1:2 (All DHCPv6 Relay Agents and Servers)
─ FF02::5   (all OSPF routers),
─ FF02::6   (all OSPF DRs)
─ ++ many more

Broadcast (IPv4):

─ One─to─all → sends a packet to all hosts on the subnet.

─ Used by: ARP requests, DHCP discovery, routing protocols like RIP.

─ Problem: Every device must process the packet, even if irrelevant → heavy on resources.

─ Replaced by multicast in IPv6.

Multicast (IPv4):

─ One─to─many → sends a packet to specific group of hosts that are members of that group.

─ Used by: OSPF, IPTV, some routing protocol updates.

─ Only hosts that subscribe to the multicast group process the packet.

─ Better integrated in IPv6 (replaces broadcast).

Why Broadcast Was Used in IPv4:

─ Early IPv4 protocols (ARP, DHCP, NetBIOS) were designed before multicast was widely supported.

─ Broadcast was a simple way to reach everyone on a small network.

Common Multicast Groups: IPv4 → IPv6

| Purpose / Protocol        | IPv4 Multicast Address      | IPv6 Multicast    | Notes |
|───────────────────────────|─────────────────────────────|───────────────────|───────|
| All nodes                 | 224.0.0.1                   | FF02::1           | Every IPv6 interface automatically joins
| All routers               | 224.0.0.2                   | FF02::2           | Only routers join
| DHCP discovery            | 255.255.255.255/broadcast   | FF02::1:2         | IPv6 uses multicast instead of broadcast
| OSPF routers              | 224.0.0.5                   | FF02::5           | All OSPFv3 routers
| OSPF Designated Routers   | 224.0.0.6                   | FF02::6           | OSPFv3 DR only
| RIP routers               | 224.0.0.9                   | FF02::9           | RIPng in IPv6
| EIGRP routers             | 224.0.0.10                  | FF02::A           | EIGRPv6 routers
| streaming / IPTV          | 224.0.0.0 – 239.255.255.255 | FF00::/8          | Hosts "subscribe to the multicast group" to receive the stream;

Note: only subscribed hosts process the traffic

Solicited-Node:

Every IPv6 host automatically joins a special multicast group based on its own IPv6 address. This is called its Solicited-Node Multicast Address.

- FF02::1:FFXX:XXXX

where: XX:XXXX are the last 24 bits of the IPv6 address. Example

Host B: 2001:DB8::1234:5678

Take the last 24 bits: 34:5678

Create: FF02::1:FF34:5678

NO broadcast (IPv6):

─ Broadcast functionality is replaced by multicast for efficiency (send only to specific group).

─ IPv4 ARP Broadcast, (every NIC on the LAN), Every device receives it, Every CPU must inspect the packet.

─ IPv6 Send to a smaller multicast group (Solicited-node multicast). only hosts that joined that multicast group receive and process it. non targeted device discard see this and discard at NIC level, doesnt reach CPU.

NDP (Neighbor Discovery Protocol):

─ Neighbor Discovery Protocol (NDP) replaces IPv4 ARP:

─ Uses ICMPv6 multicast messages.

─ Example: Neighbor Solicitation → "Who has 2001:DB8::1?" sent to solicited-node multicast, answered by the target host.

─ NDP has combined (ARP + Router Discovery + DAD + Reachability detection).

NDP (ICMPv6) combines all of this:

├─ Neighbor Solicitation  - Request IPv6 → MAC (replaces ARP), Sent to FF02::1:FFXX:XXXX (solicited-node multicast), used for DAD and NUD.
├─ Neighbor Advertisement - Reply to Neighbor Solicitation, Provides MAC address, used for NUD reachability confirmation and address resolution.
├─ Router Solicitation    - Host requests router information, Sent to FF02::2 (All Routers), triggers router discovery.
├─ Router Advertisement   - Routers reply with Prefix, Gateway, MTU, DNS, SLAAC information, DHCPv6 flags.
├─ Redirect               - Router informs host of a better next hop
├─ DAD                    - Uses Neighbor Solicitation before assigning an address (duplicate detection before use)
├─ NUD                    - Verifies neighbor reachability (uses NS/NA exchange)
└─ SLAAC                  - Host generates its own IPv6 address using prefix information from RA.

Key Concept:

─ No broadcast in IPv6.

─ NDP uses multicast groups:

• FF02::1 (All Nodes)

• FF02::2 (All Routers)

• Solicited─node multicast (FF02::1:FFXX:XXXX, per─host specific)

Summary:

NDP combines everything into one integrated ICMPv6 system. NDP = ARP + Router Discovery + DAD + Reachability detection.

Note:

─ An IPv6 address is 128 bits long, devided into 8 blocks, each block is represents 4─digit hexadecimal number, each hex character is 4 binary number(4X4 ─16 bits) X8 =128 bits.

─ IPv6 addresses are written in hexadecimal, but prefixes like (/3, /7, /10) refer to the (binary bits) not Hex or Decimal.

─ A full IPv6 address: 2001:0DB8:85A3:1234:5678:9ABC:DEF0:1234

Prefix and how range extend:

- we have 8 block, each block is a 16 binary bits, for prefix we start from first block (left).

/3  ─  (001)0 0000 0000 0000=2000 this block can change to (001)1 1111 1111 1111 = 3FFF, /3 means first 3 binary bits are fixed or resulting at Hex 2, can’t be less then 2000. When we say /3, we are really saying: only "001" is fixed — starting at 2000::, and the block range goes up to 3FFF::. Cannot be less than 2000::."

1111 = 8+4+2+1 = 15 = F (hex)

/7  ─  When we say /7, we are really saying: only '1111 110' is fixed — starting at FC00::, and the block range goes up to FDFF::. Cannot be less than FC00::."

/10 ─ means first 10 bits is fixed for network portion, remaining 118 bits are not fixed by the prefix and can be changed. (FE80::/10), When we say /10, we are really saying: only the first 10 bits "1111 1110 10" are fixed — starting at FE80::, and the block range extends to FEBF::. Cannot be less than FE80::."

Summary Table:

Prefix   Fixed Bits    Start   End Address    Notes                          Address Type
/3       001           2000::  3FFF::         Routable on the internet       Global Unicast
/7       1111 110      FC00::  FDFF::         Private, not routed globally   Unique Local
/8       1111 1111     FF00::  FFFF::         One─to─many communication      Multicast
/10      1111 1110 10  FE80::  FEBF::         Auto─configured on interfaces  Link─Local
n/a      (unicast)     Varies  Varies         Routed to nearest              Anycast

A unicast address becomes anycast when assigned to multiple nodes.

SLAAC (Stateless Address Autoconfiguration) Method:

─ Router advertises prefix (example: 2001:DB8::/64) using Router Advertisements (RA).

─ Host generates Interface ID (last 64 bits) using EUI─64 (original method) or Randomized/temporary interface IDs (modern method).

─ Combines prefix + Interface ID.

─ Result ─ full 128─bit IPv6 address.

EUI─64 (It’s a method to generate the 64─bit interface ID.):

1. MAC address of interface.

2. Flip seventh bit of the original MAC address, from left block (first).

3. Insert FFFE at middle of MAC address.

─ MAC: 00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E

─ flip 7th bit of first block: hex 00 (in binary 00000000) >>flip 7th>> 00000010; now 00 become 02, resulting 02:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E

─ Insert FFFE 02:1A:2B:FF:FE:3C:4D:5E = Interface ID

4. Given prefix 2001:DB8::/64

─ Final IPv6 Address: Combines prefix + Interface ID

─ 2001:DB8:0:0:021A:2BFF:FE3C:4D5E/64
─ 2001:DB8::21A:2BFF:FE3C:4D5E/64 (short)

# 1111─ 8+4+2+1 ─15 ─F #

Impotant Note:

─ SLAAC Allows an IPv6 host to automatically configure an IPv6 address using a network prefix advertised by a router.

    • Traditionally, SLAAC could use EUI-64 to generate the Interface ID from the host's MAC address.

    • Modern systems commonly use randomized Interface IDs for privacy instead of exposing the MAC-derived value.

    • IPv6 Privacy Extensions can generate temporary IPv6 addresses with randomized Interface IDs for outgoing connections, reducing device tracking.

Generate IPv6 using EUI-64 from given prefix:

         ipv6 address <prefix>/64 eui-64
         R1(config)# interface g0/0
         R1(config-if)# ipv6 address 2001:db8:1:1::/64 eui-64
         R1# show ipv6 interface brief

Learn prefix via SLAAC (RA) and auto-generate address:

         R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/0
         R1(config-if)# ipv6 address autoconfig

Configure manually :

         R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/0
         R1(config-if)# ipv6 address 2001:db8:1:1::1/64

Compression IPv6:

2001:0db8:fff:fcf3:0000:0000:0000:0000:/64 ─ 2001:db8:fff:fcf3::/64

─ any leading 0s, (remove)

─ consecutive 0s, ( :: ), only the longest block, once.

─ 6to4 tunneling is a method that allows IPv6 packets to be transmitted over an IPv4 network.

─ In a dual─stack network, devices communicate natively using either IPv4 or IPv6

─ L2TPv3 is a tunneling protocol used to encapsulate Layer 2 frames (Ethernet, PPP, etc.) over an IP network, VPN, MPLS.


1. What are the three main types of network cabling?

─ Single─mode fiber

─ Multimode fiber

─ Copper cabling

2. What is Single─mode fiber?

─ A fiber─optic cable that carries light in a single straight path

─ Used for long─distance, high─bandwidth communication

3. What is the core size of Single─mode fiber?

─ Approximately 8 to 10 microns

─ Smaller core reduces signal dispersion

4. What wavelengths are used in Single─mode fiber?

─ 1310 nm

─ 1550 nm

5. What light source is used in Single─mode fiber?

─ Laser

─ Highly focused and powerful

6. What distance does Single─mode fiber support?

─ 40 km and beyond

─ Commonly used by ISPs and WAN links

7. What is Multimode fiber?

─ A fiber─optic cable that carries light in multiple paths (modes)

─ Light reflects inside the core

8. What is the core size of Multimode fiber?

─ 50 or 62.5 microns

─ Larger core causes modal dispersion

9. What wavelengths are used in Multimode fiber?

─ 850 nm

─ 1300 nm

10. What light source is used in Multimode fiber?

─ LED

─ Wider light spread than lasers

11. What distance does Multimode fiber support?

─ Up to ~2 km

─ Common in LANs and data centers

12. What type of signal does copper cabling use?

─ Electrical signals

13. What is the maximum Ethernet distance for copper?

─ 100 meters

14. What are common copper cable standards?

─ Cat5e

─ Cat6

─ Cat6a

─ Cat7

15. What typically causes collisions on an interface?

─ Duplex mismatch

─ One side half─duplex, other full─duplex

16. What error is commonly caused by speed mismatch (misconfiguration)?

─ Input errors

─ Late collisions

17. What causes late collisions?

─ Speed mismatch

─ Excessive cable length

─ Faulty hardware

─ Collision detected after first 64 bytes

18. What indicates bad cabling or a bad NIC?

─ FCS errors

─ CRC errors

19. What is FCS (Frame Check Sequence) failure?

─ Indicates corrupted Ethernet frames

─ Input errors and CRC counters increase

─ Caused by bad cables, NICs, or interference

20. What does CRC error indicate?

─ Frame corruption

─ Faulty cables

─ Bad NIC

─ Network congestion

21. What is a collision?

─ Occurs in half─duplex networks

─ Devices attempt to transmit simultaneously

─ Collision counter always increases

22. What is a runt frame?

─ Frame smaller than 64 bytes

─ Caused by damaged cables or signal loss

23. What does Administratively Down / Down mean?

─ Interface manually shut down by admin

24. What does Error─disabled / Down mean?

─ Interface shut due to error condition

─ Example: BPDU guard, port security

25. What status usually indicates speed mismatch?

─ Down / Down

26. What status usually indicates duplex mismatch?

─ Up / Up or Up / Down

TCP vs UDP

27. How does UDP send data?

─ Sends individual datagrams

─ One message ─ one packet

28. What are characteristics of UDP?

─ Connectionless

─ No sequencing

─ No retransmission

─ No delivery guarantee

─ Fast and lightweight

─ Header size: 8 bytes

29. What applications commonly use UDP?

─ VoIP

─ Video streaming

─ Online gaming

─ DNS

30. How does TCP send data?

─ Sends a continuous data stream

─ Breaks stream into segments

31. What are characteristics of TCP?

─ Connection─oriented

─ Reliable

─ Sequencing and acknowledgments

─ Flow control and windowing

─ Error correction

─ Header size: 20 bytes

32. What is the TCP 3─way handshake?

─ SYN

─ SYN─ACK

─ ACK

33. What is TCP windowing?

─ Flow─control mechanism

─ Allows sending multiple packets before ACK by receiver

─ Window size defines how much data can be sent at a time

─ Receiver defines/controls and advertises the window size, dynamically controlling the flow

IPv4 Addressing & Subnetting

34. How many total IPs exist in one octet?

─ 256

─ Calculated as 2^8

35. Why are 2 IPs subtracted in most subnets?

─ One for network address

─ One for broadcast address

36. How many usable hosts in a /24 subnet?

─ 254

37. What is special about /31 subnet?

─ Used for router─to─router links

─ No broadcast address

38. What is /32 used for?

─ Single host

─ Loopback interfaces

39. How long is an IPv6 address?

─ 128 bits

─ Divided into 8 blocks

─ Each block ─ 16 bits (4 hex digits)

40. What number system does IPv6 use?

─ Hexadecimal

─ Each hex digit ─ 4 bits

41. What is a Global Unicast IPv6 address?

─ Public IPv6 address

─ Routable on the internet

─ Range: 2000::/3 to 3FFF::

42. What is a Unique Local IPv6 address?

─ Private IPv6 address

─ Not routable on internet

─ Range: FC00::/7

─ Usable range: FD00::/8 to FDFF::/8

43. What is a Link─Local IPv6 address?

─ Used within local subnet

─ Neighbor discovery

─ Router advertisements

─ Range: FE80::/10 to FEBF::

44. What is an IPv6 Multicast address?

─ One─to─many communication

─ Range: FF00::/8

45. What is FF02::1?

─ All─nodes multicast group

─ Every IPv6 device joins automatically

46. What is FF02::2?

─ All─routers multicast group

47. What is Anycast in IPv6?

─ Same IP assigned to multiple devices

─ Traffic routed to nearest device (topologically)

─ Used in DNS, CDNs, load balancing

─ Looks like unicast but is not

─ No special prefix (uses global unicast range)

48. What does /3 mean in IPv6?

─ First 3 bits are fixed

─ Range: 2000:: to 3FFF::

49. What does /7 mean in IPv6?

─ First 7 bits fixed

─ Range: FC00:: to FDFF::

50. What does /10 mean in IPv6?

─ First 10 bits fixed

─ Range: FE80:: to FEBF::

51. What is EUI─64?

─ Method to generate IPv6 interface ID

─ Uses MAC address

52. Steps of EUI─64 generation?

─ Take MAC address

─ Insert FFFE in the middle
─ Flip 7th bit of first byte


53. Given MAC 00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E and prefix 2001:DB8::/64, what is IPv6?

─ 2001:DB8::21A:2BFF:FE3C:4D5E/64

54. Rules of IPv6 compression?

─ Remove leading zeros

─ Replace longest consecutive zero blocks with ::

─ :: can be used only once

Tunneling & Dual─Stack

55. What is 6to4 tunneling?

─ Allows IPv6 packets over IPv4 networks

─ Used during IPv6 transition

56. What is dual─stack?

─ Devices run IPv4 and IPv6 simultaneously

─ Native communication for both protocols

57. What is L2TPv3?

─ Encapsulates Layer─2 frames

─ Used over IP networks

─ Common in VPNs and MPLS
