Virtualization fundamentals:

Hypervisor:

─ Type 1 (bare─metal) : Runs directly on the physical hardware (e.g., VMware ESXi, Microsoft Hyper─V).

─ Type 2 (hosted) : Runs on top of a host operating system (e.g., VMware Workstation, VirtualBox).

─ Each VM runs its own OS and applications independently, even if they are on the same physical server.

─ hypervisor allocates physical resources (CPU, memory, storage) to VMs.

containers:

─ Unlike VMs, containers share the host OS kernel.

─ Containers package application code, libraries, and dependencies (not a full OS).

─ Container Runtime: The software responsible for running containers (e.g., Docker Engine, containerd, CRI─O).

─ Kubernetes is a container orchestration platform (manages containers), not a runtime.

─ Containers are widely used in microservices architectures (one service per container).

─ Faster startup and more lightweight than VMs.

─ Containers virtualize the OS; VMs virtualize the hardware.

Virtual Routing and Forwarding (VRF):

─ VRF creates multiple isolated routing tables on a single physical router or switch (multiple instance).

─ ISP uses VRFs to separate customer networks over the same infrastructure.

─ Allows overlapping IP address spaces (e.g., multiple customers using 10.0.0.0/8).

─ Used in MPLS VPNs, data centers, and multi─tenant environments.

─ Traffic between VRFs is isolated unless route leaking is configured.

─ Same physical device, separate logical routing instances for each client.

vSwitch (Virtual Switch):

─ Software switch inside hypervisor

─ Connects VMs

─ Can support VLANs, trunking

Public cloud providers use:

─ Hypervisors (server virtualization)

─ Containers (application isolation)

─ VRFs (network segmentation)

─ SDN (Software Defined Networking)

Network Virtualization:

─ VLAN ─ Layer 2 segmentation (separate broadcast domains)

─ VRF ─ Layer 3 segmentation (separate routing tables)

─ VXLAN ─ Layer 2 (overlay network) over Layer 3 (underlay network); encapsulates L2 frames using L3 (IP/UDP) transport; extends Layer 2 networks across a Layer 3 fabric.

Wireless Principles:

─ 3 non─overlapping channels: 1, 6, 11 (in North America) for 2.4 GHz Band.

─ 23 non─overlapping channels for 5.0 GHz.

SSID:

─ SSID: A unique identifier (name) for a Wi─Fi network.

─ SSID can be broadcast or hidden, Some routers and APs allow broadcasting multiple SSIDs.

RF (Radio Frequency):

─ typically operates in the 2.4 GHz and 5 GHz bands, with newer technologies supporting 6 GHz (Wi─Fi 6E).

Encryption:

Standard  Encryption   Authentication               Notes
WEP       RC4          Shared Key / Open System     Outdated & insecure
WPA       TKIP         PSK / 802.1X                 patch over WEP, still weak
WPA2      AES─CCMP     PSK / 802.1X (Enterprise)    Strong security, still used widely
WPA3      AES─CCMP     SAE / 802.1X (Enterprise)    Strongest, prevents offline dictionary attacks, supports AES─256 in Enterprise
          AES─GCMP

─ WEP (RC4) – Uses RC4 encryption; outdated and insecure.

─ WPA (TKIP) – Uses TKIP encryption; more secure than WEP but still vulnerable.

─ WPA2 (AES─CCMP, PSK/Enterprise) – Uses AES with CCMP; strong security. PSK─pre─shared key (personal) or Enterprise ─ 802.1X/RADIUS For authentication.

─ WPA3 (AES─CCMP, AES─GCMP, SAE/Enterprise) –Replaces PSK with SAE in WPA3─Personal for stronger authentication.

     + Introduces GCMP for faster and stronger encryption in some modes.

     + WPA3 Enterprise can support AES─256 while WPA2 usually uses AES─128.

     + Both CCMP and GCMP use AES for encryption, however:

─ CCMP ─ AES + Counter Mode + CBC─MAC + SAE (used in WPA3). {Advanced Encryption Standard (AES) is a encryption algorithm}.

─ GCMP ─ AES + Galois/Counter Mode + CBC─MAC + SAE (used in WPA3, more efficient + stronger)

─ SAE (Simultaneous Authentication of Equals): Commit/Confirm exchange ensures both sides prove knowledge of the password without ever sending it (used in WPA3).

      + Prevents offline dictionary attacks (attacker can’t just grab handshake and brute force later).

While

─ CCMP ─ AES + Counter Mode + CBC─MAC + PSK (used in WPA2).

─ PSK (pre shared key/shared password) → 4─way handshake → then encryption with AES─CCMP.

     + Weakness: (WPA2) If attacker captures the 4─way handshake, they can brute─force the PSK offline.

Switching concepts:

MAC learning and aging, Frame switching, MAC address table

Switching means:

─ Learning MAC addresses

─ Forwarding frames between interfaces (Physical or logical switch ports)

─ switch forward data based on mac─table, cam table.

─ destination MAC address is not in the table, the switch will broadcast the frame to all ports, except the incoming port.

─ after receiving a response from the correct device, switch will add destination MAC address and port to its MAC address table and will forward future frames

destined for that MAC address directly to the correct port.

Store─and─Forward Switching:

─ Entire frame received before forwarding: switch waits to receive the entire Ethernet frame before making any forwarding decisions.

─ Prioritizes error detection and correction: Since the switch holds onto the frame until it is fully received, it can perform error checking (e.g., checking the CRC value in the frame) before forwarding it. If the frame is corrupted or contains errors, it will be discarded at layer 2. [data integrity and re─transmiting

frame happen at layer 4 (TCP).]

─ "Store─and─Forward Switching" is the Default behavior for most Cisco switches

S1# show mac address─table
MAC Address Table
---------------
VLAN  MAC Address       Type     Port      Age (min)  Time (sec)
────  ──────────────    ────     ────      ─────────  ─────────
1     00:1A:2B:3C:4D:5E DYNAMIC  Gi1/0/1   10         600
1     00:1B:2C:3D:4E:5F DYNAMIC  Gi1/0/2   2          120
S1# show mac address─table aging─time
Aging time is 300 seconds              # default is 300
S1(config)# mac address─table aging─time 600   # change aging time.

1. What command is used to view IP configuration on different Client OS?

─ Windows: ipconfig

─ Linux: ifconfig (or ip addr)

─ macOS: ifconfig

─ These commands display IP address, subnet mask, MAC address, and interface status.

2. What key IP parameters are shown by ifconfig/ipconfig?

─ IPv4/IPv6 address

─ Subnet mask / Prefix length

─ Default gateway

─ DNS server(s)

─ Broadcast address (mainly IPv4)

─ MAC address (Physical Address)

─ MTU (Maximum Transmission Unit)

─ Interface status (UP/DOWN, connected/disconnected)

3. What does a subnet mask of 255.255.255.0 represent?

─ It is a /24 subnet

─ First 24 bits are network bits

─ Last 8 bits are host bits

─ Allows 256 total addresses

─ 254 usable host addresses

4. How is 255 represented in binary and why is it important?

─ 255 ─ 11111111

─ Each bit represents a power of 2 (128 64 32 16 8 4 2 1)

─ Used to calculate subnet masks and IP ranges

5. What is a broadcast address?

─ The last address in a subnet

─ Used to send traffic to all hosts in the subnet

─ Example: 192.168.1.255 in a /24 network

─ Broadcasts are not forwarded by routers

6. What is a default gateway?

─ The IP address of the router interface

─ Used to reach networks outside the local subnet

─ Example: 192.168.1.1

─ If missing, host cannot communicate outside its LAN

Virtualization Fundamentals

7. What is server virtualization?

─ Running multiple virtual machines on a single physical server

─ Achieved using a hypervisor

─ Improves hardware utilization and scalability

8. What is a hypervisor?

─ Software that creates and manages virtual machines

─ Allocates CPU, memory, and storage to VMs

─ Ensures isolation between VMs

9. What is a Type 1 (bare─metal) hypervisor?

─ Runs directly on physical hardware

─ No host OS required

─ Examples: VMware ESXi, Microsoft Hyper─V

─ Better performance and security

10. What is a Type 2 (hosted) hypervisor?

─ Runs on top of a host operating system

─ Depends on host OS resources

─ Examples: VMware Workstation, VirtualBox

─ Used mainly for labs and testing

11. How are containers different from virtual machines?

─ Containers share the host OS kernel

─ VMs run a full OS per instance

─ Containers are lightweight and faster

─ Containers package only app + it dependencies

12. What is a container runtime?

─ Software responsible for running containers (e.g., Docker Engine, containerd, runc, CRI-O)

─ Manages container lifecycle (create, start, stop, delete)

─ Uses namespaces to provide isolation between containers

─ Uses cgroups (Control Groups) to limit and monitor CPU, memory, and I/O usage

─ Relies on OS kernel features rather than a hypervisor

Namespaces = isolation ("what the container can see")

cgroups = resource limits ("how much resource the container can use"/ Container A → Max 1 CPU core, 1 GB RAM. Container B → Max 2 CPU cores, 4 GB RAM)

Namespaces provide isolation by making a process think it has its own view of system resources.

Examples:

PID namespace → Separate process IDs

NET namespace → Separate network interfaces, IPs, routing tables

MNT namespace → Separate filesystem mount points

UTS namespace → Separate hostname/domain name

IPC namespace → Separate shared memory and message queues

USER namespace → Separate users and groups

Without namespaces, every process would see the same processes, network stack, and filesystem resources.

Virtual Routing and Forwarding (VRF)

13. What is VRF?

─ Virtual Routing and Forwarding

─ Multiple isolated routing tables on one device

─ Each VRF acts like a separate router

14. Why do ISPs use VRFs?

─ To separate customer networks

─ Customers can use overlapping IP addresses

─ Same physical infrastructure, no interference

15. Where are VRFs commonly used?

─ ISP networks

─ MPLS VPNs

─ Data centers

─ Multi─tenant environments

Wireless Principles

16. What are non─overlapping Wi─Fi channels in 2.4 GHz?

─ Channels: 1, 6, 11 (North America)

─ Prevent co─channel interference

─ Only 3 usable non─overlapping channels

17. How many non─overlapping channels exist in 5 GHz?

─ 23 non─overlapping channels

─ Less interference

─ Higher throughput than 2.4 GHz

18. What is an SSID?

─ Service Set Identifier

─ Name of a wireless network

─ Can be broadcast or hidden

─ APs can broadcast multiple SSIDs

19. What is RF (Radio Frequency)?

─ Medium used for wireless communication

─ Common bands: 2.4 GHz, 5 GHz

─ Newer: 6 GHz (Wi─Fi 6E)

Wireless Security & Encryption

20. What is WEP and why is it insecure?

─ Uses RC4 encryption

─ Very weak and easily cracked

─ Deprecated and not recommended

21. What is WPA and its weakness?

─ Uses TKIP encryption

─ Improvement over WEP

─ Still vulnerable to attacks

22. What is WPA2?

─ Uses AES─CCMP encryption (AES─Advanced Encryption Standard)

─ Supports PSK and Enterprise (802.1X)

─ Strong and widely used

─ Vulnerable to offline PSK attacks

23. What is WPA3 and how is it stronger?

─ Uses AES─CCMP and AES─GCMP

─ Replaces PSK with SAE (SAE─ Simultaneous Authentication of Equals)

─ Prevents offline dictionary attacks

─ Supports AES─256 in Enterprise mode

24. What is SAE in WPA3?

─ Simultaneous Authentication of Equals

─ Commit/Confirm handshake

─ Password is never transmitted

─ Protects against offline brute─force attacks

25. Difference between CCMP and GCMP?

─ CCMP = (Counter Mode + CBC-MAC) + PSK (used in WPA2)

─ CCMP = (Counter Mode + CBC-MAC) + SAE (used in WPA3)

─ GCMP = Galois/Counter Mode + SAE (used in WPA3 and newer standards)

─ GCMP is faster and has higher performance than CCMP

Key clarification:

─ WPA3 does NOT replace CCMP

─ WPA3 mainly replaces PSK authentication with SAE

─ WPA3 can use either CCMP or GCMP depending on mode/device

─ WPA2 → PSK + CCMP

─ WPA3 → SAE + (CCMP or GCMP)

26. Why is WPA2─PSK vulnerable?

─ Uses 4─way handshake

─ If captured, attacker can brute─force PSK offline

─ Fixed in WPA3 using SAE

Switching Concepts

27. What is switching?

─ Learning MAC addresses

─ Forwarding Ethernet frames

─ Uses MAC (CAM) table

28. What is a MAC address table?

─ Stores MAC─to─port mappings

─ Built dynamically by the switch

─ Used for frame forwarding

29. How does a switch learn MAC addresses?

─ Reads source MAC from incoming frames

─ Associates MAC with incoming port

─ Stores entry in MAC table

30. What happens if destination MAC is unknown?

─ Switch floods frame

─ Sent out all ports except incoming port

─ Known as unknown unicast flooding

31. What is MAC aging?

─ Process of removing unused MAC entries

─ Prevents stale entries

─ Default aging time: 300 seconds (Cisco) / 5 minutes

32. How can MAC aging time be viewed and changed?

─ show mac address─table aging─time

─ Default: 300 seconds

─ Change: mac address─table aging─time <seconds>

Store─and─Forward Switching

33. What is Store─and─Forward switching?

─ Switch receives entire frame before forwarding

─ Performs CRC error checking

─ Discards corrupted frames

34. Why is Store─and─Forward important?

─ Ensures data integrity

─ Prevents forwarding bad frames

─ Errors handled at Layer 2

35. At which layer does retransmission occur?

─ Layer 4 (TCP)

─ Switch only discards bad frames

─ TCP handles retransmission

36. What is the default switching method on Cisco switches?

─ Store─and─Forward switching


Chapter 02. Network Access (20%):

VLAN, trunk, CDP, LLDP, etherchannel, Rapid PVST+, AP modes, WLC, LAG, TACACS+/RADIUS, WLC GUI configuration

VTP:

SW1 (Server) -

VLAN 10, VLAN 20, VLAN 30

↓

+-- SW2 (Client)

↓

+-- SW3 (Client)

Transparent mode:

─ switch forwards VTP advertisements but does not process or update its own VLAN database based on those advertisements.

─ can create, delete, and modify VLANs locally

Client mode:

─ listens and processes VTP advertisements from a VTP server.

─ Cannot create, delete, or modify VLANs locally; all VLAN changes must be made on a VTP server.

Server mode:

─ create, delete, and modify VLANs, and these changes are propagated to other switches in the same VTP domain

All switches must be configured with the same VTP domain name.

All switches must use the same VTP version.

By default cisco VTP is enabled on cisco switches and in server mode.

Without VTP domain and version match, DB will not sync.

Note:

- If an attacker or careless admin gains access to a VTP server, Delete VLANs, changes spread automatically, Without VTP, damage is limited to one switch.

- Rogue Switch, with a higher revision number, Production VLAN database overwritten.

Best Practice

- Many organizations use, VTP Transparent Mode on all switches.

- VLANs stay local

- No accidental propagation

- No revision-number disasters

- Still forwards VTP advertisements

VLANs:

Access ports (data and voice):

─ Connectivity between VLANs requires a Layer 3 device.

─ Each VLAN uses a separate address space.

─ A switch maintains a separate bridging table/MAC address tables (also known as forwarding tables) for each vlan.

─ All access ports are part of some vlan's, by default its VLAN 1.

─ Many Cisco switch ports default to “Dynamic Auto mode”. # port behaves as an access port by default, ports also can negotiate a trunk via DTP if the other side requests it

─ a dynamic auto ports negotiate DTP, trunk (passively).

─ Best practice, Explicitly configure access ports: “switchport mode access”.

 VLAN taggng:

─ Frames coming from the PC/end device → are untagged when they arrive on the switch access port.

─ The switch doesn’t “tag” frames when it receives them on an access port.

─ Switch only [maps them internally] to the VLAN.

─ If switch needs to send it out another access port in same VLAN → still untagged.

─ Later, If the switch forwards those frames out of a trunk port, it will add the correct VLAN tag at that moment.

─ Tagging only happens on trunk ports (for transmission).

─ VLAN tags exist only on the wire, not inside the switch fabric.

─ Inside any switch (L2 or L3), frames are not actually “tagged” — the switch just tracks VLAN membership in metadata.

S1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet1/0/1
S1(config─if)# no shutdown
S1(config─if)# switchport mode access
S1(config─if)# switchport access vlan 101

# Static VLAN assignment

S1(config─if)# switchport access vlan dynamic

# Dynamic VLAN assignment by VMPS server (VMPS old, not used anymore)

S1(config─if)# vmps server 10.10.10.1

# VMPS server IP

Dynamic VLAN Assignment:

─ using 802.1X or RADIUS for network access control, you can dynamically assign VLANs based on user authentication.

─ Modern networks use 802.1X, RADIUS, or static VLAN assignment instead of vmps.

By Default :

─ VLAN 1 is the default VLAN on a Cisco switch .

─ VLAN 1 is assigned for every port on the switch.

─ VLAN 1 is the "native VLAN" for trunk links.

─ VLAN range from 1002─1005 (Standard), its Cisco default for FDDI and Token Ring. You cannot delete VLANs 1002─1005. Mostly we dont use VLAN in this range.

Native VLAN:

─ Management protocols (STP, CDP, VTP, etc.) send untagged frames → thats why we need a native VLAN.

─ On a trunk link, traffic for the native VLAN is sent untagged, received untagged.

─ Ensures management/control traffic is recognized correctly across switches.

Native VLAN mismatch:

─ Incorrect "native vlan" declaration on configuration

─ SW─1 native vlan: VLAN10, SW─2 Native VLAN: VLAN100, over the trunk "untagged" traffic from sw1 to sw2 will be placed on vlan100.

─ untagged frames are misclassified, control protocols break, and VLAN isolation is compromised.

voice vlan:

 switchport mode access   ← Configured as an "access port", not a trunk.
 switchport access vlan 10    ← Data VLAN (PC traffic, untagged)
 switchport voice vlan 50     ← Voice VLAN (Phone traffic, tagged)

─ switch port is still an "access port" however it will now behave like a trunk port.

─ No trunking: The port will not be in trunk mode, but it will carry both (voice+data) traffic.

─ Voice VLAN allows the port to accept tagged frames from the IP phone (voice), while data frames from the PC remain untagged.

─ Untagged traffic ─ from the PC(data) → goes to Configured access VLAN.

─ Tagged traffic ─ from the phone(voice) → goes to voice VLAN.

Cisco IP Phone:

Cisco IP Phone ─ mini─switch (3 ports) + Phone.

1. Uplink → switch

2. PC port → user’s PC

3. Internal port → phone itself

Phone uplink behavior:

─ Voice traffic → tagged with Voice VLAN ID, by phone itself (e.g., VLAN 50).

─ PC traffic → stays untagged.

─ Uplink therefore behaves like a trunk: 1 VLAN tagged, 1 untagged.

What happens:

─ Switch uses CDP or LLDP─MED to tell the phone which VLAN ID to use for voice.

─ Once informed, the phone tags its voice traffic with the configured VLAN ID, while leaving PC traffic untagged.

─ PC traffic → untagged → mapped to Access VLAN 10 ( not “native VLAN,” that term is only for trunks).

─ Phone traffic → tagged with VLAN 50 → goes into Voice VLAN 50.

─ If no CDP/LLDP─MED info is sent, the phone doesn’t know about voice VLAN → sends all traffic untagged into the access VLAN.

─ Phone not aware of CDP/LLDP─MED needs to be manually configure with voice VLAN ID.

Private VLAN (PVLAN)

Primary VLAN

 ├─ Isolated VLAN
 └─ Community VLAN

# Private VLAN = Layer-2 isolation within a primary VLAN.

─ Extends VLAN isolation within a primary VLAN.

─ Allows hosts in the same IP subnet to have different communication permissions.

─ Types of secondary VLANs:

• Isolated → hosts can not talk to each other, only to promiscuous ports (Gateway).

• Community → hosts can talk to members of the same community + promiscuous ports (Gateway).

• Promiscuous → gateway/uplink, can talk to all secondary VLANs

• Usually each secondary VLAN gets its own subnet, Primary VLAN is like a logical container for all secondary VLANs, but doesn’t need its own subnet

Example: Hotel / Dormitory Network

Primary VLAN 100

│
├─ VLAN 101 (Isolated)
│    ├─
Guest1 192.168.1.10
│    ├─
Guest2 192.168.1.11
│    └─
Guest3 192.168.1.12
│
├─ VLAN 102 (Community)
│    ├─
Staff1 192.168.1.50
│    └─
Staff2 192.168.1.51
│
└─ Promiscuous Port (Gateway)
     └─ 192.168.1.1

Communication Rules:

Guest1 → Guest2 NO

Guest1 → Staff1 NO

Guest1 → Gateway YES

Staff1 → Staff2 YES

Staff1 → Gateway YES

Gateway → Everyone YES

Key Point:

• Same IP subnet, Same primary VLAN, Different Layer-2 communication permissions.

• All hosts can belong to the same IP subnet (192.168.1.0/24).

• PVLAN controls Layer-2 communication.

• Primary VLAN acts as the container for all secondary VLANs.


Configuration:

! Primary VLAN
vlan 100
private-vlan primary
private-vlan association 101,102
! Secondary VLANs
vlan 101                  #guests (can only talk to promiscuous/gateway)
private-vlan isolated
vlan 102                  #staff PCs (can talk to each other + promiscuous)
private-vlan community
! Promiscuous Port (Gateway / Firewall / Router)  #uplink to Gateway/router/firewall (can talk to all)
interface fa0/1
switchport mode private-vlan promiscuous
switchport private-vlan mapping 100 add 101,102
! Guest Host Port (Isolated)
interface fa0/2
switchport mode private-vlan host
switchport private-vlan host-association 100 101
! Another Guest Host (Isolated)
interface fa0/4
switchport mode private-vlan host
switchport private-vlan host-association 100 101
! Staff Host Port (Community)
interface fa0/3
switchport mode private-vlan host
switchport private-vlan host-association 100 102
! Another Staff Host (Community)
interface fa0/5
switchport mode private-vlan host
switchport private-vlan host-association 100 102

Trunk port:

Trunk Port Operation

                 │
                 ├─ Encapsulation
                 │   ├─ 802.1Q (default, modern switches)
                 │   └─ ISL (egacy Cisco, obsolete)
                 │
                 ├─ VLAN Transport
                 │   └─ By default: ALL VLANs allowed
                 │
                 ├─ Allowed VLANs
                 │   ├─ allowed vlan 10,20 → REPLACE list    (disruption)
                 │   ├─ allowed vlan add 30 → ADD VLAN       (no disruption)
                 │   └─ allowed vlan remove 20 → REMOVE VLAN (no disruption)
                 │
                 ├─ Native VLAN
                 │   ├─ default = VLAN 1
                 │   └─ Best practice = change (e.g., VLAN 999)
                 │
                 ├─ DTP
                 │   ├─ Cisco proprietary only
                 │   ├─ desirable = actively forms trunk
                 │   └─ auto = passive, waits
                 │
                 └─ Results
                       ├─ desirable + desirable = trunk ✅
                       ├─ desirable + auto      = trunk ✅
                       ├─ desirable + trunk     = trunk ✅
                       ├─ auto + trunk          = trunk ✅
                       └─ auto + auto           = access❌

Trunk Port Configuration

                   │
                   ├─ STATIC TRUNK (BEST PRACTICE)
                   │
                   │  S1
                   │  ├─ interface Gi1/0/1
                   │  ├─ switchport mode trunk
                   │  ├─ switchport nonegotiate
                   │  ├─ switchport trunk native vlan 999
                   │  ├─ switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30
                   │  └─ switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40
                   │
                   │  Result:
                   │  └─ Secure trunk (no DTP) ✅
                   │
                   ├─ DYNAMIC DESIRABLE (ACTIVE)
                   │
                   │  S1
                   │  ├─ interface Gi1/0/2
                   │  ├─ switchport mode dynamic desirable
                   │  └─ (sends DTP frames)
                   │
                   │  S2
                   │  ├─ interface Gi1/0/2
                   │  └─ switchport mode dynamic auto
                   │
                   │  Result:
                   │  └─ Trunk forms (desirable + auto) ✅
                   │
                   └─ DYNAMIC AUTO (PASSIVE ONLY)
                      │
                      │  S1
                      │  └─ switchport mode dynamic auto
                      │
                      │  S2
                      │  └─ switchport mode dynamic auto
                      │
                      └─ Result:
                         └─ Remains ACCESS ❌ (no trunk)

─ switchport mode trunk # By default all VLAN are allowed on a trunk ports.

─ switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20,30 # replace existing config, disruption, only allowed vlan are allowed.

─ switchport trunk allowed vlan add 40 # add aditional vlan, no disruption

─ switchport trunk allowed vlan remove 20 # remove a vlan, no disruption

─ "encapsulation" is 802.1q, unless explicitly specified. # ISL (legacy Cisco proprietary, obsolete)

─ "native vlan" is VLAN 1, If no native VLAN is configured.

─ Many Cisco switch ports default to “Dynamic Auto mode”. # port behaves as an access port by default, ports also can negotiate a trunk via DTP if the other side requests it

─ a dynamic auto ports negotiate DTP, trunk (passively).

─ Best practice, Explicitly configure access ports: “switchport mode access”.

Trunk port mode:

─ 3 modes─ static, desirable & auto (dynamic).

Trunk static:

 S1(config)# switchport mode trunk                      # Forces the port into trunk mode, “static” trunk mode still listens/responds to DTP frames.
 S1(config)# switchport nonegotiate                     # fully disable DTP, avoid VLAN hopping attacks
 S1(config)# switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q       # {for older switches only, newer IOS 802.1Q is set by default}
 S1(config)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 101,102      # only vlan 101,102 are allowed
 S1(config)# switchport trunk native vlan 100           # without "native vlan" command , default native  is "vlan 1"

Trunk dynamic DTP:

 S1(config)# switchport mode dynamic desirable   ← actively tries to form a trunk
 S1(config)# switchport mode dynamic auto        ← passive, waits for other side

─ DTP is Cisco proprietary → doesn’t work with other vendors.

─ Best practice: Use static trunks for security, and fully disable DTP. # switchport nonegotiate

─ Trunk forms if: desirable ↔ desirable OR desirable ↔ auto OR desirable ↔ trunk (static) or auto ↔ trunk (static).

─ auto ↔ auto ─ stays access (no trunk).

─ “static” trunk mode still listens/responds to DTP frames, so it can coexist with desirable/auto.

Best practices:

─ Disable DTP (switchport nonegotiate) and set trunks manually.

─ Use dynamic only in labs to practice negotiation.

─ Don't use VLAN 1 for production traffic.

─ Move the native VLAN to an unused VLAN (e.g., 999).

─ Move management to a dedicated VLAN.

Inter vlan routing:

Inter-VLAN Routing

                               │
          ┌────────────────────┴──────────────────────┐
          │                                           │
▼                                           ▼
Router-on-a-Stick                           Layer 3 Switch (SVI)
          │  R1                                       │
          │  ├─ interface G0/0                        │
          │  └─ no shutdown                           │
          │                                           │  S1
          │  ├─ interface G0/0.10                     │  ├─ interface vlan 10
          │  ├─ encapsulation dot1Q 10                │  ├─ ip address 192.168.10.1 255.255.255.0
          │  └─ ip address 192.168.10.1 255.255.255.0 │  └─ no shutdown
          │                                           │
          │  ├─ interface G0/0.20                     │  ├─ interface vlan 20
          │  ├─ encapsulation dot1Q 20                │  ├─ ip address 192.168.20.1 255.255.255.0
          │  └─ ip address 192.168.20.1 255.255.255.0 │  └─ no shutdown
          │                                           │
          ├─ Result:                                  ├─ Result:
          │   └─ Subinterfaces = VLAN gateways ✅     │      └─ SVIs = VLAN gateways ✅
          └─ Requires:                                └─ Requires:
              └─ SW port connected to R1 must be a TRUNK     └─ ip routing

Router on stick (router int ):

R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/0
R1(config─if)# no shutdown
R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/0.10
R1(config─if)# encapsulation dot1Q 10                     # encapsulation, must match VLAN ID.
R1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.10.1 255.255.255.0      # IP address for VLAN 10 + its Gateway for this vlan
R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/0.20
R1(config─if)# encapsulation dot1Q 20
R1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.20.1 255.255.255.0

Layer 3 switch (SVI int):

S1(config)# interface vlan 10
S1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.10.1 255.255.255.0
S1(config─if)# no shutdown
S1(config)# interface vlan 20
S1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.20.1 255.255.255.0
S1(config─if)# no shutdown
S1(config)# ip routing                 # Enable routing on Layer 3 switch, enabled by default.

# SVIs are Layer 3 interfaces on the switch, and they don't require encapsulation like subinterfaces on a router because the switch already understands which VLAN the interface belongs to.

S1(config-if)# switchport mode dot1q-tunnel  # Places the port into Q-in-Q tunnel mode, allowing customer VLAN traffic (C-VLANs) to be transported across the provider network.

Q-in-Q Tunneling (802.1ad):

- Q-in-Q is a Layer-2 VLAN tunneling technology (802.1ad) commonly used in Metro Ethernet services.

- Service provider transporting customer VLANs across a Metro Ethernet network

- It allows a provider to transport customer VLANs transparently across the provider network by adding an outer Service VLAN (S-VLAN) tag.

- The term "tunnel" refers to encapsulating customer VLAN traffic inside a provider VLAN, not to creating an L2-over-L3 IP-tunnel.

- MPLS, VPLS, or EVPN may be used elsewhere in the provider network, but they are not required for Q-in-Q operation.


- Customer VLAN (C-VLAN): The customer's original VLAN tag(s).

- Service VLAN (S-VLAN): The provider's outer VLAN tag used to identify and separate customer traffic within the provider network.

- Q-in-Q = Enable tunneling + Assign an S-VLAN

Customer_switch(config-if)# interface Gi0/1
Customer_switch(config-if)# switchport mode access
Customer_switch(config-if)# switchport access vlan 10
Customer frame:
[C-VLAN 10][Payload]
       │
       ▼
PE_switch(config-if)# interface Gi0/1
PE_switch(config-if)# switchport access vlan 200    # Defines the S-VLAN (outer tag)
PE_switch(config-if)# switchport mode dot1q-tunnel  # Enables Q-in-Q processing
       │
       ▼
[S-VLAN 200][C-VLAN 10][Payload]

Result: Customer VLAN traffic is encapsulated within the provider's Service VLAN and transported across the provider network.

How It Works:

- Customer frames arrive with a C-VLAN tag.

- The provider edge (PE) switch receives the frame on a port configured for dot1q-tunnel.

- The PE switch adds an outer S-VLAN tag.

- The frame is transported across the provider network with both tags.

   [S-VLAN][C-VLAN][Ethernet Payload]


notes

- 'switchport mode dot1q-tunnel' enables Q-in-Q processing.

- The S-VLAN ID is normally determined by the access VLAN assigned to the tunnel port (e.g. 'switchport access vlan 200').

- The command itself does not specify which S-VLAN tag is used.

─ Typically an ADVA device at your site is just aggregating traffic locally, and then it forwards your traffic to a central PE switch in the ISP metro network.

─ An ADVA customer port behaves like a plain Layer─2 Ethernet switch port.

─ VLAN transparency: your VLANs remain intact within the ISP’s Layer 2 network, but are not preserved over the public Internet.

Ingress PE:

1. Receives customer frame → [C─VLAN][payload]

2. Adds S─VLAN (Q─in─Q) for access─domain separation

3. Maps S─VLAN to L2VPN instance (EVPN/VPLS)

4. Removes S─VLAN (not needed in MPLS core)

5. Encapsulates customer Ethernet frame into MPLS core

Egress PE:

1. Receives [MPLS labels][Customer Ethernet frame]

2. MPLS label identifies L2VPN instance

3. Removes MPLS labels

4. Re─associates frame with customer service

5. Adds S─VLAN toward metro/access domain (if required)

6. Removes S─VLAN before sending to customer switch

Traffic Flow:

# Customer Site A

Access Switch (L2)
        | 802.1Q trunk (C─VLAN 10)
        ↓
Customer Edge Switch (CE)
        | 802.1Q trunk (C─VLAN 10)
        ↓
ADVA (L2 handoff / NID)
        | [C─VLAN 10][payload]
        ↓
ISP PE (Ingress - Metro / Access Edge)
        |
switchport mode dot1q─tunnel
        |
switchport access vlan 200
        |
        | Added S─VLAN 100 (Q─in─Q) and Forwards Ethernet service toward MPLS PE
        ↓
┌────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│ MPLS PE (Service Edge / Ingress L3VPN or L2VPN PE) │
│       | Based on VLAN, port, or subinterface)      │
│       | Classifies service (EVPN/VPLS/L2VPN)       │
│       | Maps attachment circuit → service instance │
│       | Pushes MPLS label(s)                       │
│       ↓                                            │ PROVIDER MPLS CORE DOMAIN
│ MPLS Core (P Routers)                              │
│       | [MPLS label(s)][payload]                   │
│       | Label switching only (NO VLAN awareness)   │
│       ↓                                            │
│ Remote MPLS PE (Egress)                            │
│       | Pops MPLS labels                           │
│       | Identifies service instance                │
│       | Outputs Ethernet frame                     │
└────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
       ↓
ISP PE (Egress Metro / Access Edge)
       | Removes/handles S─VLAN 100 if used in metro domain
       | Restores pure customer Ethernet handoff
       ↓
ADVA (L2)
       | [C─VLAN 10][payload]
       ↓
Customer CE Switch
       ↓
Access Switch

# Customer Site B

Note:

CE Router for inter─VLAN routing sits behind the [CE switch] connected to [PE switch], not on the Q─in─Q handoff interface. This way, the ISP still sees all VLANs as tagged frames, and your routing happens locally.

Configuration:

Access_Switch(config)# vlan 10
Access_Switch(config-vlan)# name CUST-VLAN-10
Access_Switch(config)# vlan 20
Access_Switch(config-vlan)# name CUST-VLAN-20
Access_Switch(config)# interface Gi0/1
Access_Switch(config-if)# description Uplink to CE Switch
Access_Switch(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
Access_Switch(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20
CE_Switch(config)# vlan 10
CE_Switch(config-vlan)# name CUST-VLAN-10
CE_Switch(config)# vlan 20
CE_Switch(config-vlan)# name CUST-VLAN-20
CE_Switch(config)# interface Gi0/1
CE_Switch(config-if)# description Downlink to Access Switch
CE_Switch(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
CE_Switch(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20
CE_Switch(config)# interface Gi0/2
CE_Switch(config-if)# description Uplink to ADVA / ISP
CE_Switch(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
CE_Switch(config-if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20
PE_Switch(config)# vlan 100
PE_Switch(config-vlan)# name S-VLAN-CUSTOMER-A
PE_Switch(config)# interface Gi0/1
PE_Switch(config-if)# description Customer A handoff via ADVA
PE_Switch(config-if)# switchport access vlan 100
PE_Switch(config-if)# switchport mode dot1q-tunnel   # do not configure trunk mode.
[S-VLAN 100][C-VLAN 10]
[S-VLAN 100][C-VLAN 20]

switchport mode dot1q-tunnel, forces the port into a special Layer 2 tunneling mode, which treats incoming frames as customer traffic (C-VLANs), adds an outer S-VLAN tag, does NOT behave like a normal trunk port.

─ Downstream network carries S─VLAN + C─VLAN to isolate multiple customers.

─ After the PE switch, provider often aggregates traffic from many customers onto a single backbone link (share the same physical link without VLAN conflicts).

─ Aftr aggregation, often provider use L2overL3 technologies and route packet to to other side or ISP PE switch (Side B)

─ Q-in-Q belongs to the Ethernet access/metro domain.

─ MPLS belongs to the provider core domain.

─ EVPN/VPLS belongs to the service control plane at the PE edge.

CDP and LLDP:

                       CDP and LLDP
                            │
          ┌─────────────────┴─────────────────┐
          │                                   │
          ▼                                   ▼
         CDP                                LLDP
          │                                   │
          ├─ Cisco proprietary                ├─ Open standard
          ├─ Enabled by default               ├─ Disabled by default
          ├─ Cisco ↔ Cisco                    ├─ Multi-vendor
          │                                   │
          ├─ Global Control                   ├─ Global Control
          │  ├─ cdp run                       │  ├─ lldp run
          │  └─ no cdp run                    │  └─ no lldp run
          │                                   │
          ├─ Interface Control                ├─ Interface Control
          │  ├─ cdp enable                    │  ├─ lldp transmit
          │  └─ no cdp enable                 │  ├─ lldp receive
          │                                   │  ├─ no lldp transmit
          │                                   │  └─ no lldp receive
          │                                   │
          └─ Timers                           └─ Timers
             ├─ timer 60 sec                     ├─ timer 30 sec
             └─ holdtime 180 sec                 ├─ holdtime 120 sec
                                                 └─ reinit 2 sec

Configuration:

                 CDP / LLDP Configuration
                           │
          ┌────────────────┴─────────────┐
          │                              │
          ▼                              ▼
      Enable CDP                   Enable LLDP
          │                              │
          │  S1                          │  S1
          │  ├─ cdp run                  │  ├─ lldp run
          │  ├─ cdp timer 60             │  ├─ lldp timer 30
          │  └─ cdp holdtime 180         │  ├─ lldp holdtime 120
          │                              │  └─ lldp reinit 2
          │                              │
          │  interface G0/1              │  interface G0/1
          │  └─ cdp enable               │  ├─ lldp transmit
          │                              │  └─ lldp receive
          │                              │
          ├─ Result:                     └─ Result:
          │     Neighbor discovery ✅          Neighbor discovery ✅
          │
          └─ CDP and LLDP will not exchange information, both ends must support the same protocol

CDP: ((Cisco proprietary ))

─ If CDP or LLDP is globally enabled, we can disable them on a specific interface.

─ If CDP or LLDP is globally disabled, we can not enable them on any interface. (security reason)

─ CDP is Enabled by default on most Cisco devices

─ LLDP is disabled by default on Cisco devices (must be enabled manually).

S1(config)# cdp run             # Enable CDP globally
S1(config)# no cdp run          # Disable CDP globally
S1(config)# cdp timer 60        # Advertisements to its neighbors, Default is 60 seconds.
S1(config)# cdp holdtime 180    # Neighbour to hold before discarding cdp info, Default is 180 seconds.
S1(config)# interface G0/1      # Enable CDP on specific interface
S1(config─if)# cdp enable
S1(config─if)# no cdp enable    # Disable CDP on this interfaces
S1(config)# lldp run            # Enable LLDP globally (Cisco, HP, Juniper, etc.)
S1(config)# no lldp run         # Disable LLDP globally
S1(config)# lldp timer 30       # LLDP advertisements to its neighbors, Default is 30 seconds.
S1(config)# lldp holdtime 120   # Neighbour to hold before discarding lldp info, Default is 120 seconds.
S1(config)# lldp reinit 2       # Reinitialize after a link failure, Default is 2 seconds.
S1(config)# interface G0/1      # Enable LLDP on specific interface
S1(config─if)# lldp transmit
S1(config─if)# lldp receive
S1(config─if)# no lldp transmit # Disable LLDP transmit on this interface
S1(config─if)# no lldp receive  # Disable LLDP receive on this interface
S1(config)# cdp run             # Both CDP and LLDP can be enabled globally and on spec interface too
S1(config)# lldp run
S1(config)# interface G0/1
S1(config─if)# cdp enable
S1(config─if)# lldp transmit
S1(config─if)# lldp receive

# Notes:

─ CDP and LLDP will not exchange information, both ends must support the same protocol.

─ CDP and LLDP are Layer 2 protocols and work without an IP address.
