 R1# show ip ospf neighbor
 Neighbor ID  Pri   State         Dead Time   Address         Interface
2.2.2.2      1   FULL/BDR        00:00:38    10.1.1.2        GigabitEthernet0/0
3.3.3.3      1   FULL/DROTHER    00:00:37    10.1.1.3        GigabitEthernet0/0
4.4.4.4      1   2WAY/DROTHER    00:00:36    10.1.1.4        GigabitEthernet0/0
5.5.5.5      0   FULL/─          00:00:40    172.16.1.2      Serial0/0/0  #p2p
6.6.6.6      1   FULL/DR         00:01:55    192.168.1.2     Serial0/1/0       #NBMA / Frame Relay
7.7.7.7      1   FULL/DROTHER    00:01:52    192.168.1.3     Serial0/1/0
8.8.8.8      1   EXSTART/DR      00:00:31    10.2.2.2        GigabitEthernet0/1
Kery Notes:
FULL/BDR                 → healthy adjacency, [neighbor is BDR], (your router may be DR or DROTHER).
FULL/DROTHER      → healthy adjacency, [neighbor is DROTHER], (your router could be DR, BDR, or DROTHER).
2WAY/DROTHER     → normal on broadcast/NBMA, [neigbour is DROTHER], (your router is DROTHER in that relationship), No full adjacency forms between DROTHER routers.
FULL/─                       → point─to─point, Full adjacency always forms, No DR/BDR exists.
EXSTART/DR             → stuck adjacency
Passive interfaces    → do not appear at all in neighbor table.
Multiple areas           → neighbors appear only if interface is in same area.
LSA:
Type 1 — Router LSA: Every OSPF router, Describes the router’s own interfaces, links, and costs within an area.
Type 2 — Network LSA: DR (Designated Router) only, Lists all routers connected to that segment, DR describes the network.
Type 3 — Summary LSA: ABR, Advertises routes from one area into another, without sharing full LSDBs.
Type 4 — ASBR Summary LSA: ABR, Tells other areas how to reach an ASBR.
Type 5 — External LSA: ASBR, re─distributed external routes and inject to ospf domain.
Type 7 — NSSA External LSA: ASBR, allowing external routes without flooding Type 5
1 & 2 ─ inside an area
3 & 4 ─ between areas (ABR)
5 & 7 ─ external (ASBR)
Forming OSPF Neighbour/Adjancency:
─ Area ID             # 0
─ Hello/Dead timers   # 10/40
─ Network type        # Loopback, Broadcast, Point─to─Point, NBMA (Non─Broadcast)
─ Authentication        # MD5/none
─ Stub flag             # stub/NSSA
─ Interface not passive # no passive
─ MTU Size                       # 1500 (Neighbor relationship is possible with MTU mismatch, but FULL adjacency will fail)
OSPF Neighbors:
─ Routers in the (same areas) form a neighbor relationship through Hello packets, no LSAs exchanged.
─ They become aware of each others presence, but they do not exchange the full set of routing information (LSDBs).
─ Routers elect a Designated Router (DR) and Backup Designated Router (BDR).
─ DR & BDR form FULL adjacency with all routers.
─ Non─DR routers form FULL adjacency ONLY with DR/BDR
─ Non─DR routers become "OSPF Neighbors" by exchanging hello packets with each other
─ All adjacencies are neighbors, but not all neighbors become adjacent.
─ Routers in different areas can be neighbors via ABRs, but no full adjacency.
OSPF Adjacency:
─ Routers in the (same area) can form full adjacencies and synchronize LSDBs if conditions are met.
─ Routers exchange Type 1 LSAs, and only DR generates Type 2 LSAs.
─ DR/BDR form full adjacency with each other and any other routers in the LAN.
─ Other routers(DROTHER) form full adjacency with DR/BDR only, but remain neighbours to each other.
─ Routers in different areas do not share full LSDBs and do not form full adjacencies
─ Routers in different areas can still become neighbors with ABRs by exchanging Hello packets
─ Inter─area routing is done via summary LSAs (Type 3) or external LSAs (Type 5) through ABRs, not via adjacency
OSPF Adjacencies (full LSDB sync):
─ DR ↔ BDR
─ DR ↔ DROTHER
─ BDR ↔ DROTHER
X ─ DROTHER ↔ DROTHER → neighbor only (no full adjacency)
ABR (Inter─Area Communication):
─ Connects Area 0 (backbone) to another area within the same Autonomous System/domain.
─ Create and exchange summary routes [Type 3 LSAs] to aggregate "routing information" between OSPF areas.
─ Maintains separate LSDB per area, Does NOT forward full LSDBs.
─ Also generates Type 4 LSAs (ASBR Summary) to tell other areas how to reach an ASBR.
ASBR (external network):
─ Redistributes external routes into OSPF by generating LSAs (Type 5 ) for external routes.
─ This is often done through route redistribution from other routing protocols (e.g., BGP, EIGRP, or static routes).
LSA type:
─ 1 & 2 ─ Inside an area
─ 3 & 4 ─ Between areas (ABR)
─ 5 & 7 ─ External routes (ASBR)
OSPF Domain (Autonomous System):
─ An OSPF domain is defined by OSPF Process, Areas, and Consistent Configurations across routers. (OSPF does not use an AS number (unlike BGP).)).
─ Each OSPF domain has a common routing policy and exchanges LSAs within the domain.
─ Multi─area OSPF domain requires a backbone area (Area 0) and ABR.
[OSPF Processes ID]:
─ The OSPF process is a local instance of OSPF running on a router.
─ Each OSPF process requires a unique Router ID within the OSPF domain.
─ [OSPF process] is the core instance managing areas, LSAs and routing decisions.
─ [Router ID] must be unique, it identifies router in the domain.
─ [multiple OSPF process] can be run on sigle router, more CPU and memory.
─ [route redistribution] injects routes from another routing domain or protocol into OSPF (often at an ASBR),(ISP─Company).
[Areas]:
─ [divide a domain] Areas divide an OSPF domain to improve scalability and efficiency..
─ [multiple area] can be created under single (OSPF Process ID). [allowed and recommended for large networks]
─ [LSDB] Each area has its own LSDB, maintained by routers within that area, not directly shared with other areas.
─ [automatically synchronize] LSDBs are automatically synchronized among routers in the same area. LSDBs {link─state database}.
─ [Unique area ID] for Group of routers and links [(ex. backbone area 0) or 0.0.0.0 same as 0 ].
─ Backbone Area (Area 0): Central hub for inter─area communication.
─ Non─backbone Areas: Other areas connected to Area 0 (regular, stub, and NSSA).
─ Internal Routers: All interfaces belong to a single area.
─ Border Routers (ABRs): Connect Area 0 with one or more non─backbone areas .
─ Boundary Routers (ASBRs): Connect external networks and inject external routes into the OSPF domain..
Real life OSPF DR/BDR:
In production networks, we almost always:
─ Control DR/BDR using OSPF interface priority
─ Use loopback interfaces for stable Router IDs
─ Do NOT rely on highest physical interface IP
─ Often avoid DR/BDR elections entirely on router links #
R1(config─if)# ip ospf network point─to─point
DR/BDR election:
─ Highest Router Priority : Highest OSPF interface priority (0–255, default 1)
─ Highest Router ID :
─ Manually configured Router ID, OR
─ highest IP, on a loopback interface, OR
─ highest IP, on a active physical interface.
─ DRs are responsible for exchanging Link─State Advertisements (LSAs) between routers on a broadcast or NBMA network.

Notes:
─ P2P links & loopbacks ─ DR/BDR election not required.
─ OSPF Priority 0 on an interface prevents it from becoming a DR/BDR.
─ For each subnet (multiple router can share same subnet), OSPF will have a DR/BDR election.” [switch in the middle]
─ "passive─interface" command in OSPF prevents sending Hello packets and forming neighbor adjacencies on an interface while still advertising the network.
─ Single Area Deployment (No Backbone Needed): simple design, all routers in one area
─ Multi─Area OSPF (Backbone Needed (area 0)): other areas must connect to Area 0
─ [Stub area] does not receive external routes from ASBR redistribution (block Type 5 LSAs); instead, ABR injects a default route (0.0.0.0).
─ [Redistribution] happens at the edge of the OSPF domain, on the last OSPF router before the external network.

Stub area:
─ Allow intra─area (Type 1) & inter─area (Type 3) routes, plus a default route injected by the ABR while block external routes from ASBR redistribution Type 5 LSAs.
─ Type 4 LSAs (ASBR Summary) by ABR are also blocked.
─ They can still reach external destinations via the default route from the ABR.
─ Reduces routing overhead and improves stability.
 R1(config)# router ospf 1
 R1(config─router)# area 1 stub     # Configures Area 1 as a stub area
 R2(config)# router ospf 1
 R2(config─router)# area 1 stub     # Configures Router 2 to be in a stub area
NSSA:
─ Allow same as stub area additionally allow external routes from local ASBR Type 7→Type 5 LSAs (converted by ABR), Type 5 LSAs from other ASBRs are still blocked..
─ local ASBR reside inside NSSA area.
#Stub Areas
              [External Network]
                     |
               +─────────────+
               |  ASBR       |  ← normal, generates Type 5 LSAs
               |─────────────

               |  ABR        |  → Backbone / Other Areas / other ASBR
               |─────────────

               | stub area   |  ← Blocks any external Type 5 LSAs
               +─────────────+  ← Allows intra─area (Type 1) & inter─area (Type 3) routes, + a default route from ABR.
# NSSA
               [External Network]
                        |
                        v
                  |  ASBR       |  ← Inside NSSA, generates Type 7 LSAs
                  |─────────────

                  | NSSA Area   |  ← Stub─like routers, see Type 7 LSAs from local ASBR, + all Type 1 & Type 3 routes
                  |─────────────

                  |     ABR     |  → Backbone / Other Areas ( ABR converts Type 7 → Type 5)
                  |─────────────

                  | other ASBR  |  ← External Type 5 LSAs from other areas blocked inside NSSA
                   ─────────────
OSPF Configuration:
 Network Diagram Overview:
  ───────────────────         ───────────────────        ───────────────────
 |      R1 (Area 0)   |──────|    R2 (ABR)       |──────|    R3 (Area 1)    |─────>>>
 |   Process ID 10    |      | Process ID 11     |      | Process ID 12     |
  ───────────────────         ──────────────────          ─────────────────
      ────────────────────────     ──────────────
     |      R4 (ASBR)         |   |   R5 (ISP)    |
 >>──| Process ID 13          |───| BGP, Static   |
     | Redistributes from R5  |   +───────────────+
R1(config─if)# interface G0/0        # Connection to R2 (Area 0)
R1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.100.1 255.255.255.0
R1(config─if)# no shutdown
R1(config)# router ospf 10           # Process ID is local, doesn't require matching. # network in Area 0
R1(config─router)# network 192.168.100.0 0.0.0.255 area 0
#
R2─ABR (config─if)# interface G0/1   # Connection to R1 (Area 0)
R2─ABR (config─if)# ip address 192.168.100.2 255.255.255.0
R2─ABR (config─if)# no shutdown
R2─ABR (config─if)# interface G0/2   # Connection to R3 (Area 1)
R2─ABR (config─if)# ip address 10.10.10.1 255.255.255.0
R2─ABR (config─if)# no shutdown
R2─ABR (config)# router ospf 11     # Process ID is local, doesn't require matching. # ABR must have area 0 and other area.
R2─ABR (config─router)# network 192.168.100.0 0.0.0.255 area 0
R2─ABR (config─router)# network 10.10.10.0 0.0.0.255 area 1   # keeping multiple area under same process consume less resource.
#
R3(config─if)# interface G0/0                                 # Connection to R2 (Area 1)
R3(config─if)# ip address 10.10.10.2 255.255.255.0
R3(config─if)# no shutdown
R3(config─if)# interface G0/1                                 # Connection to R4 (Area 1)
R3(config─if)# ip address 172.16.30.2 255.255.255.0
R3(config─if)# no shutdown
R3(config)# router ospf 12
R3(config─router)# network 10.10.10.0 0.0.0.255 area 1
R3(config─router)# network 172.16.30.0 0.0.0.255 area 1
#
R4─ASBR(config─if)# interface G0/0                            # Connection to R3 (Area 1)
R4─ASBR(config─if)# ip address 172.16.30.1 255.255.255.0
R4─ASBR(config─if)# no shutdown
R4─ASBR(config─if)# interface G0/1                            # Connection to ISP (External Network)
R4─ASBR(config─if)# ip address 203.80.80.2 255.255.255.254
R4─ASBR(config─if)# no shutdown
R4─ASBR(config)# ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 203.80.80.1         # Static Route to external ISP
R4─ASBR(config)# router ospf 13
R4─ASBR(config─router)# network 172.16.30.0 0.0.0.255 area 1  # Network in Area 1
R4─ASBR(config─router)# redistribute bgp 65001 subnets        # Redistribute BGP routes into OSPF
R4─ASBR(config─router)# redistribute static subnets           # Redistribute Static Routes into OSPF
R4─ASBR(config)# router bgp 65001                             # AS 65001 company internal, shouldnt match with ISP.
R4─ASBR(config─router)# neighbor 203.80.80.1 remote─as 100 # BGP doesn't dynamically discover neighbors like OSPF, BGP Peering with R5 (ISP).
# Dont advertise your internal nework, for BGP R5 (ISP), they dont need to know internal network.
R5─ISP(config─if)# interface G0/0       # Connection to R4 (Customer)
R5─ISP(config─if)# ip address 203.80.80.1 255.255.255.254
R5─ISP(config─if)# no shutdown
R5─ISP(config─if)# interface loopback0
R5─ISP(config─if)# ip address 8.8.8.8 255.255.255.255
R5─ISP(config─if)# interface loopback1
R5─ISP(config─if)# ip address 1.1.1.1 255.255.255.255   # dummy network, other isp bgp will not advertise those routes for R4.
R5─ISP(config─if)# interface loopback2
R5─ISP(config─if)# ip address 100.64.0.1 255.255.255.0
R5─ISP(config)# router bgp 100
R5─ISP(config─router)# neighbor 203.80.80.2 remote─as 65001     # BGP Peering with R4, R5 is connected to a larger BGP network, external routes will be advertised via BGP
R5─ISP(config─router)# network 8.8.8.8 mask 255.255.255.255
R5─ISP(config─router)# network 1.1.1.1 mask 255.255.255.255     # Advertise "Internet" networks
R5─ISP(config─router)# network 100.64.0.0 mask 255.255.255.0
R5─ISP(config─router)# network 203.80.80.0 mask 255.255.255.254 # not require for p2p, Advertise external network to R4

 Enable OSPF interface level:
─ Advertise its own netwrok automatically, seperate network command not required.
─ This matches area and process ID to form OSPF adjacency.
 R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/1
 R1(config─if)# ip ospf 1 area 1
 R1(config─if)# exit
OSPF Multicast Addresses:
Feature                        IPv4 OSPF            IPv6 OSPF (OSPFv3)
| ──────────────────────── | ───────────────────── | ──────────────────── |
| Multicast All Routers    | 224.0.0.5             | FF02::5              |
| Multicast DR/BDR Routers | 224.0.0.6             | FF02::6              |
| Hello packets            | 224.0.0.5 / 224.0.0.6 | FF02::5 / FF02::6    |
| Neighbor discovery       | IPv4 address          | Link─local IPv6      |
| Network command          | Yes                   | No (interface─based) |
Cost/Metric calculation:
─ Metrics matters, for installing routes in routing table, also for selecting route fro the installed path.
─ OSPF: cost (bandwidth)
─ EIGRP: cost (Bandwidth, delay, load, and reliability)
─ RIP: cost (Hop count)
Additional EIGRP notes:
Successor ─ primary path
─In EIGRP, when the router learns multiple routes to the same destination, it calculates the composite metric for each route.
─The route with the lowest metric becomes the successor — the one that is installed in the routing table and used to forward traffic.
Feasible successors ─ backup paths
─EIGRP keeps track of other routes that satisfy the feasibility condition (metric < successor metric) as feasible successors.
FHRP (First Hop Redundancy Protocols):
─ FHRPs provide gateway redundancy → hosts always have the same default gateway IP, by Grouping multiple routers and creating a virtual IP address (VIP), work even if gateway router fails.
Roles       VRRP(Open)       HSRP(Cisco)       GLBP(Cisco)
Active:       1 Master         1 Active          Active Group (4) AVG/AVF
Backup:       Backup(Group)    1 Standby         not required
others:       none             listen(idle)      none
Load Balance: No               No                Yes (4)
MAC    :      0000.5e00.01xx   0000.0c07.acxx  0007.b400.xxxx
Note   :      (1 VRRP group)  (1 HSRP group)   (1 GLBP group (auto load balancing))
Notes:
VRRP: One router is Master (active) others are backups. If Master fails, highest─priority backup becomes Master. Dynamic failover─Yes.
HSRP: One active, one standby, others idle. If both fail, idle routers don’t take over unless reconfigured. Limited dynamic failover.
GLBP: Up to 4 routers actively forward traffic (load balancing)  One is AVG, rest are AVFs.
─ If an AVF fails, traffic is redistributed among remaining AVFs. Dynamic + Load balanced. (AVG) – assigns virtual MACs to other routers.
HSRP States:
─ Initial – Interface just came up
─ Learn – Received Hello packets but no Virtual IP learned yet.
─ Listen – Waiting for Hello packets from Active or Standby.
─ Speak – Sending and receiving Hello packets.
─ Standby – Backup, ready to take over if Active fails.
─ Active – Actively forwarding traffic.
VRRP sample config:
 R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/1
 R1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.1.2 255.255.255.0
 R1(config─if)# vrrp 10 ip 192.168.1.1   # Group 10 with Virtual IP
 R1(config─if)# vrrp 10 priority 110     # Higher priority (Master)
 R1(config─if)# vrrp 10 preempt          # Allows takeover
 R2(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/1
 R2(config─if)# ip address 192.168.1.3 255.255.255.0
 R2(config─if)# vrrp 10 ip 192.168.1.1   # Same group number 10 with Virtual IP
 R2(config─if)# vrrp 10 priority 100     # Lower priority (Backup)
1. A router learns the same prefix via:
* OSPF (cost 10)
* Static route (AD 1)
* RIP (hop count 2)
👉 Which route is installed and why?
➡ Static route, lower AD.
2. What is the order for installing routes into the routing table?
➡ Administrative Distance
➡ Longest Prefix Match
➡ Metric
➡ Equal─Cost Multipath (if supported)
3. What is the order for selecting a route to forward a packet?
➡ Longest Prefix Match
➡ Administrative Distance
➡ Metric
➡ ECMP
➡ (Possibly first installed as last fallback) not official.
4. Two OSPF routes exist:
* 10.0.0.0/8
* 10.1.1.0/24
👉 Which one is used for traffic to 10.1.1.5?
➡ 10.1.1.0/24 (longest prefix)
5. A router has:
* 0.0.0.0/0 via ISP─A
* 192.168.0.0/16 via ISP─B
👉 Which path is chosen for 192.168.10.10?
➡ 192.168.0.0/16 via ISP─B (more specific route)
6. What is a default route and when is it used?
➡ 0.0.0.0/0 or ::/0; used when no more specific route exists.
7. What is a host route?
➡ A route to a single IP address (/32 IPv4, /128 IPv6).
8. What is a floating static route?
➡ A static route with higher AD used only when the primary route fails.
9. When would a floating static route appear in the routing table?
➡ Only when the primary route disappears
10. Difference between C and L routes?
* C (Connected): Entire subnet
* L (Local): Interface IP only (/32 or /128)
11. show ip route shows routes, but traffic still fails.  What non─routing issues could cause this?
➡ ACL blocking traffic
➡ Wrong default gateway on host
➡ Layer 2 issue (VLAN, trunk)
➡ ARP failure
➡ Interface down
12. What is the function of the Data Plane/forwarding plane?
➡ It forwards traffic based on existing tables (MAC table at L2, routing table/FIB table at L3).
13. What is the role of the Control Plane?
➡ It builds and maintains forwarding information using routing, switching, and management protocols.
14. Which protocols operate in the Control Plane?
➡ OSPF, BGP, RIP (routing)
➡ STP, ARP (L2)
➡ SNMP, NETCONF, SSH (management plane)
─ Data plane → moves packets
─ Control plane → decides where packets go
─ Management plane → lets humans control the device
15. What must match for OSPF adjacency to form?
➡ Area ID #0
➡ Hello / Dead timers #10/40
➡ Network type #broadcast/Point─to─Point/Non─Broadcast
➡ Authentication #MD5/none
➡ Stub flag #stub/NSSA (only matters if a stub or NSSA is configured on the area)
➡ MTU #1500
➡ Interface not passive #no passive
➡ Router ID → Does not need to match; must be unique. Duplicate RIDs break adjacency (EXSTART stuck).
➡ Process ID → Local to router, does not need to match.
➡ OSPF cost → does NOT need to match.
➡ Ethernet → think Broadcast
➡ Serial → think Point─to─Point
➡ Frame Relay → think NBMA
16. Difference between OSPF neighbors and adjacencies?
➡ Neighbors: Hello exchange only [ DROTHER➡DROTHER, {same area, between areas (ABR require) } ]
➡ Adjacencies: Full LSDB synchronization (DR➡BDR/➡DROTHER)
17. Two routers see each other in show ip ospf neighbor as 2WAY/DROTHER.
👉 Is this a problem? Why or why not?
➡ No problem (normal behavior)
➡ DROTHER routers do not form FULL adjacencies with each other, FULL adjacency only with DR/BDR
18. An OSPF neighbor is stuck in EXSTART.
👉 Name two likely causes.
➡ MTU mismatch
➡ Duplicate Router ID
➡ Interface misconfiguration (rare)
─ EXSTART only happens after neighbors are formed. EXSTART is the first step of the database exchange (DBD) process.
19. OSPF neighbors form, but routes never appear.
👉 What are three things you would check?
➡ Interfaces in same area
➡ Network statements / interface OSPF config
➡ another interfaces on router being Passive blocking routes
➡ Stub/NSSA mismatch
➡ Route filtering or redistribution issues
20. An interface is configured as passive─interface, but routes still appear.
👉 Why?
➡ Passive interface stops Hellos, not route advertisement. The network is still advertised via OSPF.
➡ Passive─interface is used to prevent adjanceies/OSPF neighbor formation while still advertising the network.
➡ Do not configure passive interface on router─to─router interfaces.
➡ Do configure "passive interface" on a router interfaces which connected end devices, a server, a switch etc.
21. OSPF routes appear with higher cost than expected.
👉 What interface setting should you check?
➡ Interface bandwidth setting (OSPF cost is bandwidth─based)
22. A router ID unexpectedly changes after a reload.
👉 Why?
➡ No manual router─id was set
➡ No loopback configured
➡ Router picks highest active interface IP dynamically
23. Can routers in different OSPF areas form full adjacencies?
➡ No. Only routers in the same area form full adjacencies [ABR, ASBR, and LSAs].
24. Three routers share same Ethernet LAN:
* R1 priority 100
* R2 priority 1
* R3 priority 0
 Who becomes DR, BDR, and why?
➡ DR: Priority 100
➡ BDR: Priority 1
➡ Priority 0: Never participates
25. DR/BDR election order?
➡ Highest OSPF priority # priority 100
➡ Highest Router ID # router─id 2.2.2.2
➡ Highest loopback IP # 10.10.10.10
➡ Highest active interface IP # 192.168.1.10
➡ Priority 0 ─ never becomes DR/BDR
➡ Default priority ─ 1
➡ Loopback IPs are preferred for Router ID
➡ If no loopback exists, highest active interface IP is used
26. A DR goes down.
Does a new DR get elected immediately?
➡ BDR immediately becomes DR
➡ No new election for BDR unless another router joins later.
27. Why is ip ospf priority 0 commonly used on access routers?
➡ Prevents them from becoming DR/BDR
➡ Keeps DR role on stable/core routers
28. When is DR/BDR election NOT required?
➡ Point─to─Point links and loopbacks.
➡ Stub Areas
─ DR/BDR exist to reduce LSA flooding, but area block type 5 LSA, no external flooding, only internal LSA (minimal), therefore no dr/bdr required.
29. 👉 Which LSA types:
* Stay inside an area?
* Cross area boundaries?
* Represent external routes?
➡ Inside area: Type 1, Type 2
➡ Between areas: Type 3, Type 4
➡ External routes: Type 5, Type 7
30. 👉 Why must all inter─area traffic pass through Area 0?
➡ Area 0 is the OSPF backbone
➡ All areas must connect to Area 0 for routing consistency
31. 👉 What happens if Area 0 becomes disconnected?
➡ Inter─area routing breaks
➡ OSPF domain becomes fragmented
32. 👉 Why does an ABR maintain multiple LSDBs?
➡ Prevents flooding full topology between areas
33. What is the role of an ABR? [Connects Area 0 ↔ other areas] (Area Border Router)?
➡ Connects areas and generates Type 3 (summary) LSAs.
➡ Has at least one interface in Area 0
➡ Has at least one interface in a non─backbone area
34. What is the role of an ASBR? (Autonomous System Boundary Router)?
➡ Injects external routes into OSPF using Type 5 LSAs.
➡ Imports routes from outside OSPF.
➡ Generate: Type 5 LSAs (External), Type 7 LSAs (NSSA only).
➡ Redistribute route #yes
35. What defines an OSPF domain?
➡ Common OSPF process, consistent areas, and shared LSDB policies.
➡ DR / BDR Election
36. 👉 What is a Stub Area? (OSPF)
➡ Blocks external routes
➡ Does NOT allow Type 5 LSAs, Type 4 LSAs
➡ Uses a default route instead
➡ Purpose: reduce routing table size and LSA flooding
─ also DR/BDR not required: DR/BDR exist to reduce LSA flooding, for stub area minimal LSAs, no external flooding
37. 👉 What a Stub Area allows:
➡ Type 1 (Router) LSAs
➡ Type 2 (Network) LSAs
➡ Type 3 (Summary) LSAs
38. 👉 Why can’t Area 0 be a stub or NSSA?
➡ Stub/NSSA block external LSAs
➡ Backbone must carry all LSA types
39. 👉 In which scenario is NSSA better than stub?
➡ When the area needs to inject external routes[Type 7 LSA] (e.g., branch with Internet access)
40. 👉 What LSA type allows NSSA to inject external routes?
➡ Type 7 LSA
41. What routes are blocked in a stub area?
➡ External routes (Type 5 LSAs) (Type 4 LSAs).
42. How do stub routers reach external networks?
➡ Via a default route injected by the ABR (Type 3 LSA).
43. Area Diagram:
          Area 0 (NORMAL)
         ┌──────────────┐
         │   Core       │
         │              │
         └─────┬────────┘
               │
      ┌────────┴────────┐
      │                 │
 Area 1 (ABR)       Area 2 (ASBR)
Totally Stub       NSSA
Branch             Branch w/ Internet
➡ Backbone Area (Area 0), Cannot be stub or NSSA, Must carry all LSA types, Connects all other areas.
➡ "Stub" recommended for Non─Backbone Areas [ABR](Edge / Branch / Access Areas), blocks all external routes(Type 5 + Type 4), No redistribution.
➡ "NSSA" recommended for Area with External Routes (ASBR), blocks external routes from outside(Type 5 + Type 4), but allows local redistribution (Type 7 LSAs).
➡ By default no "stub/nssa" flag is configured, default is "No stub flag set" normal.
44. OSPF metric?
➡ Cost (bandwidth─based).
45. EIGRP metric?
➡ Bandwidth and delay (default).
46. RIP metric?
➡ Hop count.
47. Purpose of FHRPs?
➡ Provide default gateway redundancy.
48. A host’s default gateway IP never changes, but MAC addresses change.
👉 Which FHRP is most likely being used?
➡ HSRP or VRRP
49. Why a host does not need to be reconfigured anything during an FHRP failover?
➡ Virtual IP and gateway stay the same
➡ MAC updates handled transparently
50. Two routers forward traffic simultaneously for the same VLAN.
👉 Which FHRP supports this?
➡ GLBP
51. Which FHRP supports load balancing?
➡ GLBP.
52. Difference between HSRP and VRRP?
➡ HSRP: Cisco proprietary, Active/Standby
➡ VRRP: Open standard, Master/Backup
53. Your static route was accepted (no error), but it’s not appearing in the routing table.
On Cisco IOU / IOS, this usually means:
The next─hop 192.168.1.1 is not reachable.
A static route only installs in the routing table if the router knows how to reach the next─hop.
on real Cisco routers:
ping 127.0.0.1 ALWAYS works
Self─ping ALWAYS works
Interface state does NOT break loopback processing
But in IOU/IOL images inside GNS3: probably doesnt

04. IP Services (10%):
NAT, QoS, SNMP,Syslog, SSH, DNS, DHCP, NTP and TFTP
Key point:
─ On a stanard routed network with no NAT, every TCP/UDP session is identified by the 4─tuple
─ Source IP + Destination IP + Source Port + Destination Port.
─ Source Port ─ random port picked by the client OS (123456..) also called ephemeral.
─ Destination Port ─ well─known service port (80/443/53/etc)
The IP header: carries Source IP + Destination IP
The TCP/UDP header: carries Source Port + Destination Port
─ The PC’s TCP stack always picks a random high source port (ephemeral) when connecting out. That’s how the server knows which TCP session to reply to.
NAT:
NAT modify the source IP (private) with (public IP) address [and sometimes source port] before sending the packet out. The NAT router then keeps a stateful NAT table so that when replies come back, it knows how to reverse the translation.
REQUEST (Client → Server)    REPLY (Server → Client):
Inside Local(Client)       Inside Local(Client)
Src: 192.168.0.10:55000    Src: 100.100.100.1:443
Dst: 100.100.100.1:443           Dst: 192.168.0.10:55000
       │                           ▲
       ▼                           │
Inside Global(SNAT)        Inside Global(SNAT)
Src: 192.168.0.10:55000          Src: 100.100.100.1:443
Dst: 100.100.100.1:443           Dst: 192.168.0.10:55000
[Re─write]                                           [Re─write back]
Src: 203.0.113.5:40001     Src: 100.100.100.1:443
Dst: 100.100.100.1:443     Dst: 203.0.113.5:40001
       │                           ▲
       ▼                           │
Outside Global(DNAT)       Outside Global(DNAT)
Src: 203.0.113.5:40001           Src: 100.100.100.1:443
Dst: 100.100.100.1:443           Dst: 203.0.113.5:40001
[Re─write]                                          [Re─write back]
Src: 203.0.113.5:40001     Src: 10.10.10.20:8443
Dst: 10.10.10.20:8443      Dst: 203.0.113.5:40001
       │                           ▲
       ▼                           │
Outside Local(Server)      Outside Local(Server)
Src: 203.0.113.5:40001           Src: 10.10.10.20:8443
Dst: 10.10.10.20:8443      Dst: 203.0.113.5:40001.
Inside Local: – Private IP inside LAN (e.g. 192.168.0.10)
Inside Global: – Public IP used by NAT router (e.g. 203.0.113.5)
Outside Global: – Public IP of Server/load balancer/Router (e.g. 100.100.100.1)
Outside Local: – Private IP of a server (e.g. 10.10.10.20), which reside behind "Outside Global".
Note:
Outside local: A web server with an Outside Global IP of 203.100.80.10. However, the server might be using a private IP internally (e.g., 10.1.1.10).
Ex. Static NAT, Port forwarding, DNAT, Load balancers / reverse proxies, (Docker, Kubernetes, Hypervisors) all actually use NAT term.
─ Public IP(server)  → Container IP.
─ 203.0.113.50 → 10.1.1.11 / 10.1.1.12 / 10.1.1.13
1. Static NAT (One─to─One):
                R1(config)# ip nat inside source static 10.10.10.10 203.0.113.5
                R1(config)# interface g0/0
                R1(config─if)# ip nat inside
                R1(config)# interface g0/1      #external interface
                R1(config─if)# ip nat outside
2. Dynamic NAT (Many─to─Many):
                R1(config)# ip nat pool PUBLIC_POOL 203.0.113.5 203.0.113.10 netmask 255.255.255.248
                R1(config)# access─list 1 permit 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255
                R1(config)# ip nat inside source list 1 pool PUBLIC_POOL
+ inside/outside
3. PAT (Port Address Translation, Many to one, NAT Overload):
                R1(config)# access─list 1 permit 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255
                R1(config)# ip nat inside source list 1 interface g0/1 overload
+ inside/outside
                R1# show ip nat translations
                R1# show ip nat statistics
Q1. What uniquely identifies a TCP/UDP session on a non─NAT routed network?
─ The 4─tuple: Source IP + Destination IP + Source Port + Destination Port.
Q2. Which headers contain those values?
─ IP Header: Source IP + Destination IP
─ TCP/UDP Header: Source Port + Destination Port
Q3. Why does the client operating system choose a random (ephemeral) source port?
─ To uniquely identify the session so return traffic can be matched to the correct application/socket.
Q4. In NAT, which values are modified before packets leave the private network?
─ The Source IP is always changed
─ The Source Port may also be changed (PAT case)
Q5. What is the Inside Local address?
─ The private IP of the internal device, seen inside the network (e.g., 192.168.1.10).
Q6. What is the Inside Global address?
─ The public IP used on the NAT device to represent the internal client on the internet (e.g., 203.0.113.5).
Q7. What is the Outside Global address?
─ The real public IP of the external destination host (e.g., 8.8.8.8).
Q8. What is the Outside Local address?
─ The private/internal representation of an external server (e.g., 10.1.1.10 behind 203.100.80.10).
Q9. Static NAT summary?
─ One inside local mapped permanently to one inside global (1:1).
Q10. Dynamic NAT summary?
─ Many inside hosts mapped to a pool of global addresses (many:many).
Q11. PAT summary?
─ Many inside hosts mapped to a single public IP by altering source ports (many:1).
Q12. Command to verify NAT translations?
─ show ip nat translations
Q13. Command to verify NAT statistics?
─ show ip nat statistics
Q14. Why does PAT require port translation?
─ To differentiate multiple sessions from multiple hosts using the same public IP.
Q15. Which address is changed during a typical outbound internet connection using PAT?
─ Inside Local → Inside Global (with port change)
key Notes:
TCP/UDP Session Identity
4─Tuple ─ SIP + DIP + SPORT + DPORT (non nat network)
IP header: SIP, DIP
TCP/UDP header: SPORT, DPORT
Ephemeral port ─ random source port chosen by client OS
QoS:
Classification: who are you (identifies traffic e.g., VoIP, HTTP, etc.)).
Marking: put a label on you (DSCP/CoS).
Queuing: stand in the right line (which packets wait where)
Congestion management / Scheduling: who goes first when the line is full.
Policing: drop traffic/packet that exceeds allowed limits(bandwidth).
Shaping: slow down the rush, keep the line orderly(smooth traffic to a set rate, controls bandwidth).
Classification:
─ Identify traffice based on source/destination address, protocol, ports, application type.
─ Classification is the process of identifying and categorizing packets.
─ Then classified packes are assigned to specific traffic classes.
─ Example, voice traffic (VoIP) may be classified as high priority, while bulk data transfers (FTP) might be classified a lower priority.
Marking:
─ Classified packets are marked with the appropriate DSCP/CoS value so that network devices (L3/L2) know their priority.
─ L2: CoS (802.1Q VLAN tag), L3: DSCP (IP header).
─ Example: Voice traffic often marked DSCP EF (46).
Queuing:
─ Placing packets into queues based on their marking/class value. Prioritised traffic when congestion occurs.
Congestion management:
─ During congestion, PHBs help to prioritize traffic flows, so important traffic (like voice calls) can still be delivered with minimal delay.
Policing:
─ Enforce traffic limits. If the traffic exceeds a defined threshold, the network may either drop the excess traffic or mark it with a lower priority.
Shaping:
─ Unlike policing, which may drop traffic, shaping buffers the traffic and sends it out at a controlled rate.
─ Traffic shaping is the process of delaying traffic so that it conforms to a predefined traffic profile.
1. Normal Network Conditions:
─ Available Bandwidth─ 10 Mbps
Queue     Classification  Marking         Volume    Action (normal)
Queue 1   VoIP            EF (High)       1 Mbps    Sent first (highest priority)
Queue 2   HTTP            AF21 (Medium)   3 Mbps    Sent second (medium priority)
Queue 3   FTP             BE (Low)        7 Mbps      Sent last (low priority)
─ Result: All packets are sent without delay. VoIP goes first, then HTTP, and finally FTP.
2. Network Congestion (Queues Start Filling):
Available bandwidth drops to 5 Mbps because of network congestion.
Queue     Classification  Marking           Volume    Action (congestion managment)
Queue 1   VoIP            EF (High)         1 Mbps    Sent first (highest priority)
Queue 2   HTTP            AF21 (Medium)     3 Mbps    Sent second (if bandwidth allows)
Queue 3   FTP             BE (Low)          7 Mbps    Likely dropped or delayed to fit within bandwidth
─ Congestion Management: Queues fill up. VoIP is always sent first, but HTTP and FTP might experience delay or dropping.
3. Policing and Shaping in Action:
─ Policing: HTTP traffic exceeds the allowed rate of 2 Mbps (Excess traffic gets dropped or marked as lower priority)
─ Shaping: Excess FTP traffic is delayed to fit within the 5 Mbps rate, so it doesn’t burst and cause congestion.
Queue     Classification  Marking        Volume   Policing/Shaping   Action
Queue 1   VoIP            EF (High)      1 Mbps   1 Mbps             Sent first (highest priority)
Queue 2   HTTP            AF21 (Medium)  3 Mbps   >2 Mbps            Policed (excess dropped/remarked)
Queue 3   FTP             BE (Low)       7 Mbps   5 Mbps*            Shaped (excess delayed, buffered to fit within rate)
Excess FTP traffic is shaped/delayed to fit within the 5 Mbps rate, so it doesn’t burst and cause congestion.
SNMP:
─ SNMP Manager queries network devices using GET requests to collect data. (Nagios SolarWinds Zabbix)
─ SNMP Agents send TRAP messages to the SNMP Manager when an issue occurs (e.g., interface down).
─ Operations:
GET → Manager polls data from device.
SET → Manager changes config remotely.
TRAP → Device pushed unsolicited alert to Manager (e.g., interface down).
─ Default version on Cisco IOS ─ v1 (unless you explicitly set version 2c or version 3).
─ SNMPv1/v2c ─ simple, community string (no encryption). SNMPv3 ─ secure, supports auth + privacy (encryption).
 Enable SNMpv2:
        R1(config)# snmp─server community PUBLIC_RO             #PUBLIC_RO >> Read─Only Community String
        R1(config)# snmp─server community PRIVATE_RW            #PRIVATE_RW >> Read─Write Community String
        R1(config)# snmp─server host 192.168.1.100 version 2c PUBLIC_RO #SNMP Trap Destination
        R1(config)# snmp─server enable traps
Note:
─ Community strings are often used in SNMP v1 and SNMP v2c but are replaced by more secure methods in SNMP v3, which uses authentication and encryption.
─ logging trap 4 #configure for Warning (level 4), Error (level 3), Critical (level 2), Alert (level 1), and Emergency (level 0), all of it.
─ Memory Trick: Every Awesome Cisco Engineer Will Need Ice Daily (Emergency, Alert, Critical, Error, Warning, Notice, Informational, Debugging )
─ ex. SNMP output messages:
          SNMPv2─MIB::sysUpTime.0 ─ 12345678
          SNMPv2─MIB::snmpTrapOID.0 ─ IF─MIB::linkDown
  Enable SNMpv3:
        R1(config)# snmp─server group SECURE─GRP v3 priv
        R1(config)# snmp─server user ADMIN SECURE─GRP v3 auth sha MyAuthPass123 priv aes 128 MyPrivKey123
        R1(config)# snmp─server host 192.168.1.100 version 3 priv ADMIN
        R1(config)# snmp─server enable traps
        R1(config)# logging trap 4
Syslog:
 Enable Syslog on a Router/Switch:
        R1(config)# logging host 192.168.1.100          #logging host >> Sends logs to a Syslog server (192.168.1.100)
        R1(config)# logging trap notifications          #logging trap >> Logs messages level 5 (Notice) and above
        R1(config)# logging facility local4             #logging facility local4 >> Uses local4 facility for logs
        R1(config)# logging on
        R1# show logging
%LINK─3─UPDOWN: Interface GigabitEthernet0/1, changed state to down
Syslog Server Setup (Ubuntu/Linux):
sudo apt update
sudo apt install rsyslog
/etc/rsyslog.conf:
$ModLoad imudp                  #Load the UDP syslog module.
$UDPServerRun 514                     #Start listening on UDP port 514 (Syslog default) for incoming messages
$ModLoad imtcp                  #Load the TCP syslog module (optional, TCP syslog).
$InputTCPServerRun 514          #Start listening on TCP port 514.
sudo systemctl restart rsyslog
tail ─f /var/log/syslog
Q1: What is the difference between classification and marking in QoS?
─ Classification identifies traffic; marking applies a label (DSCP/CoS) to enforce priority.
Q2: What header field does Layer 2 QoS marking use?
─ CoS in the 802.1Q VLAN tag.
Q3: What header field does Layer 3 QoS marking use?
─ DSCP in the IP header.
Q4: Which QoS action restricts traffic by dropping excess packets?
─ Policing.
Q5: Which QoS action controls bandwidth by buffering and sending traffic at a controlled rate?
─ Shaping.
Q6: What QoS mechanism determines which packets move first during congestion?
─ Congestion management (queuing + scheduling).
Q7: Voice traffic is commonly marked using which DSCP value?
─ DSCP EF (46).
Q8: What is the function of an SNMP Manager?
─ It polls devices for data (GET/SET) and receives alerts (TRAPS), mostly for monitoring purpose.
Q9: What do SNMP agents send when an event occurs, like an interface going down?
─ TRAP messages.
Q10: What is the major security difference between SNMP v2c and SNMP v3?
─ v2c uses community strings with no encryption; v3 supports authentication and privacy (encryption).
Q11: Which command configures a read─only community string?
─ snmp─server community <name> RO
Q12: Which version is secure and supports encryption?
─ SNMP v3.
Q13: What command defines the SNMP server where traps are sent?
─ snmp─server host <ip> version <v> <community/user>
Syslog
Q14: What is the purpose of logging host <ip>?
─ Sends logs from the router to a remote Syslog server.
Q15: What Syslog severity level does logging trap notifications represent?
─ Level 5 (Notice) and above.
Q16: Which Syslog severity level is the most critical?
─ Level 0 – Emergency.
Q17: What port does Syslog use by default?
─ UDP 514.
Q18: What does the command logging facility local4 configure?
─ Specifies the Syslog facility identifier used for log categorization.
2. Exam Memory Cheatsheet
QoS Summary
Classification: Identify traffic (apps/ports/protocols)
Marking: DSCP (L3) / CoS (L2) label applied
Queuing: Put into priority queues
Congestion Mgmt: Who goes first when congested
Policing: Drop/remark excess
Shaping: Slow down; buffer to conform
Memory Trick:
Classify > Mark > Queue > Manage > Police (drop) OR Shape (delay)
Voice ─ DSCP EF (46)
SSH:
─ SSH (Secure Shell) uses RSA public─key pair cryptography:
─ Public key: User to encrypt data sent to the server.
─ Private key: Server to decrypt received data (kept secret).
─ The switch/router must be running a k9 (crypto) IOS image.
─ Hostname must be configured.
─ The ip domain─name command must be configured on the device.
Myswitch(config)# hostname S1
S1(config)# ip domain─name example.com
S1(config)# crypto key generate rsa
S1(config)# ip ssh version 2
S1(config)# username admin privilege 15 secret StrongPassword123
S1(config)# line vty 0 4                        # a total of 5 simultaneous remote sessions allowed.
S1(config─line)# transport input ssh    # disable insecure remote access (Telnet) and allow SSH only on VTY line.
S1(config─line)# login local                    # use the local username database for authentication.
S1(config─line)# exit
S1# show ip ssh
S1# show crypto key mypubkey rsa
DNS records:
A                        # Maps a hostname to an IPv4 address.
AAAA             # map a hostname to an IPv6 address.
CNAME            # Alias record. Points one domain/subdomain to another domain name (not directly to an IP).
NS (Name server)         # tells DNS resolvers where is Name Server/DNS server for the domain.
PTR (Pointer record)     # reverse DNS lookups (IP address → hostname)..
SOA (Start of Authority) # domain serial number, admin email, refresh/retry timers, etc.
DHCP Relay (IP Helper─Address):
─ If DHCP server is not on the same local network as the client.
─ ip helper─address: points to the next─hop router's IP address, which can either be:
─ The IP address of the DHCP server if the router is directly connected to the server.
─ The IP address of another router that can reach the DHCP server.
─ Configure "ip helper─address" on the gateway interface of the client’s subnet, not globally.
R1(config)# int gig0/1
R1(config─if)# ip helper─address 192.168.1.100
S1(config)# int vlan 10
        S1(config─if)# ip helper─address 192.168.10.1
HCP server:
How dhcp server knows which IP subnet to assign?
client: DHCP Discover>>DHCP relay agent/router (IP Helper Address)/ add ─giaddr─ field ((Gateway IP Address))>>
>>DHCP Server (uses the giadd) determine client network.
DHCP Option 82: Relay agent can add more info (clients origin, port or VLAN the request came from.)
DHCP Option 50: Renew IP Address (client used this option)
#Configure the First DHCP Pool (mypool1)
 R1(config)# ip dhcp pool mypool1
 R1(dhcp─config)# network 192.168.11.0 255.255.255.0
 R1(dhcp─config)# default─router 192.168.11.1            # Gateway for the pool (connected interface IP)
 R1(dhcp─config)# dns─server 8.8.8.8 1.1.1.1             # DNS servers for clients
 R1(dhcp─config)# lease 7                                # Lease time (7 days)
 R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/1                # Configure router interface connected to this subnet (LAN)
 R1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.11.1 255.255.255.0    # IP address of the interface (DHCP gateway)
 R1(config─if)# no shutdown
# Configure second DHCP pool (mypool2)
 R1(config)# ip dhcp pool mypool2
 R1(dhcp─config)# network 192.168.12.0 255.255.255.0
 R1(dhcp─config)# default─router 192.168.12.1            # Gateway for the pool (connected interface IP)
 R1(dhcp─config)# dns─server 8.8.4.4 1.1.1.1             # DNS servers for the clients
 R1(dhcp─config)# lease 5                                # Lease time (5 days)
 R1(config)# interface GigabitEthernet0/2                # Configure router interface connected to Second subnet (LAN)
 R1(config─if)# ip address 192.168.12.1 255.255.255.0    # Interface IP (DHCP gateway)
 R1(config─if)# no shutdown
#Exclude Addresses from DHCP Pools (Optional) #To prevent DHCP from assigning specific IPs (e.g., for servers or statically configured devices), use:
 R1(config)# ip dhcp excluded─address 192.168.11.1 192.168.11.10
 R1(config)# ip dhcp excluded─address 192.168.12.1 192.168.12.10
Verify DHCP Configuration:
 R1# show arp
 R1# show ip dhcp binding
 R1# show ip dhcp pool
PC1>dhcp
PC2>dhcp
PC2>ping
Configure NTP ─server:
 R1(config)# clock set 12:00:00 Mar 25 2025             # Set the R1s clock manually or via another time source
 R1(config)# ntp master 3
 R1# show ntp status
 NTP ─client:
 R1(config)# ntp server 192.168.1.1     # Set the NTP server (point to the NTP server's IP)
 R1# show ntp associations                      # Verify NTP Sync
 R1# show clock detail
 R1# show ntp status
 NTP ─ Authentication(Optional, for security):
 R1(config)# ntp authentication─key 1 md5 SECRETKEY
 R1(config)# ntp authenticate
 R1(config)# ntp trusted─key 1
 R1(config)# ntp server 192.168.1.1 key 1
TFTP:
 R1# copy running─config tftp
 R1# copy tftp running─config
 R1# copy tftp: flash:
 R1(config)# no boot system
 R1(config)# boot system flash:c2960─lanbasek9─mz.150─2.SE11.bin
SSH:
Q1: What two prerequisites must be configured before generating RSA keys for SSH?
─ A hostname and an IP domain name must be configured.
hostname <name>
ip domain─name <domain>
Q2: What IOS image requirement must a device meet to support SSH?
─ It must run a crypto─enabled (k9) IOS image.
Q3: What does login local do under VTY lines?
─ It forces SSH authentication to use the device’s local username database.
Q4: Why use transport input ssh on VTY lines?
─ To disable insecure remote access (Telnet) and allow SSH only.
Q5: Which command verifies SSH status on a Cisco device?
─ show ip ssh
DNS RECORDS:
Q6: What DNS record maps a hostname to an IPv4 address?
─ A record.
Q7: Which record maps IPv6 addresses?
─ AAAA record.
Q8: Which record is used for reverse DNS lookups?
─ PTR record.
Q9: Which record identifies the authoritative DNS server for a domain?
─ NS record.
Q10: Which record redirects one domain name to another?
─ CNAME record.
DHCP Relay / IP Helper:
Q11: Why is ip helper─address required?
─ To forward broadcast DHCP Discover messages to a remote DHCP server.
Q12: Where is ip helper─address configured?
─ On the gateway interface of the client’s subnet.
Q13: What field does a router insert to tell the DHCP server which subnet the client originates from?
─ The giaddr (Gateway IP Address) field.
Q14: What does DHCP Option 82 provide?
─ Relay agent information (origin port/VLAN/client location), insert by a router or even a switch.
DHCP Server:
Q15: How does a DHCP server know which IP pool to assign to a client?
─ It examines the giaddr (gateway IP) from the relay agent.
Q16: What command prevents DHCP from assigning IPs that must stay static?
─ ip dhcp excluded─address <range>
Q17: How do you view active DHCP leases on router (DHCP server) ?
─ show ip dhcp binding
NTP:
Q18: What is the function of ntp master 3?
─ Makes the router act as an NTP server with stratum 3 priority.
Q19: How do you set an NTP client to sync to a time server?
─ ntp server <server─ip>
Q20: How do you enforce authenticated NTP updates?
A20:
ntp authentication─key <id> md5 <key>
ntp authenticate
ntp trusted─key <id>
ntp server <ip> key <id>
TFTP:
Q21: What command backs up the running configuration to a TFTP server?
─ copy running─config tftp:
Q22: How do you restore a config from a TFTP server?
─ copy tftp: running─config
Q23: Which command sets a device to boot a specific IOS file?
─ boot system flash:<filename>
2. Exam Memory Cheatsheet (Ultra─Compact)
SSH Setup Order:
hostname NAME
ip domain─name DOMAIN
crypto key generate rsa
ip ssh version 2
username USER privilege 15 secret PASS
line vty 0 4
 login local
 transport input ssh
Rule: Hostname + Domain─name → RSA keys → SSH v2 → Local auth
05. Security Fundamentals (15%):
ACL, DHCP snooping, dynamic ARP inspection, and port security, IPsec, site─to─site VPNs, WPA, WPA2, and WPA3, WLAN within the GUI using WPA2 PSK,threats, user awareness, training, and physical access control, MFA.
ACL:
Route                                   TO(Network)       VIA
R1(config)#ip route 172.16.3.0 255.255.255.0 192.168.2.4
R1(config)#access─list 100 permit ip 172.16.3.0 0.0.0.255 192.168.2.0 0.0.0.25
access─list─name                FROM(NETWORK)     TO(NETWORK)
PC(192.168.1.10)
↓
[ g0/0 ]  R1  [ g0/1 ]
↓
↓
[ g0/0 ]  R2  [ g0/1 ]
↓
Server(10.1.1.10)
1_99, 1300_1999) Standard ACL: → place closer to the Destination (less specific), # Filter traffic only by source IP address.
Numbered ACL (legacy style):
 R2(config)# access─list 10 deny 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255   ← LAN subnet block
 R2(config)# access─list 10 permit any                   ← Allow everything else. #not "ip any any", require source/destiantion
 R2(config)# int g0/1                                    ← Apply outbound, closer to the Destination
 R2(config─if)# ip access─group 10 out
Named ACL (modern):
 R2(config)# ip access─list standard DENY_LOCAL     ← Create named ACL, with seq number, also possible without seq number not recommended.
 R2(config─std─nacl)# 10 deny 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255 ← Block LAN traffic
 R2(config─std─nacl)# 20 permit any                 ← Allow everything else
 R2(config)# int g0/1                               ← Apply outbound, closer to the Destination
 R2(config─if)# ip access─group DENY_LOCAL out
(100_199, 2000_2699) Extended ACL: → Close to the Source (more specific), Filter traffic by source/destination ports and IP address.
Numbered ACL (legacy style):
 R1(config)# access─list 100 deny tcp 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255 host 10.1.1.10 eq 22
 R1(config)# access─list 100 permit ip any any    # Deny SSH from LAN to server
 R1(config)# int g0/0                             # Permit other traffic from LAN, require "ip any any", source and destination.
 R1(config─if)# ip access─group 100 in            # apply inbound, closer to source
Named ACL (modern):
 R1(config)# ip access─list extended Block_SSH    # Create named ACL, with seq number, better.
 R1(config─ext─nacl)# 10 deny tcp 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255 host 10.1.1.10 eq 22
 R1(config─ext─nacl)# 20 permit ip any any        # Deny SSH from LAN to server
# Same permit other LAN traffic
 R1(config)# int g0/0                             # "deny ip any any" (implicit deny, invisible, default by Cisco)
 R1(config─if)# ip access─group Block_SSH in      # apply inbound, closer to source
Named ACL Syntax with Sequence Numbers: With sequence numbers, you can insert, delete, or modify individual lines within the ACL without re─configuring the entire list.
Insert: While inside ACL mode, use a sequence number to insert a new rule without affecting existing rules.
 R1(config─ext─nacl)# 15 permit icmp any any
Delete: While inside ACL mode, use the no <sequence_number> command to remove a specific line, ← deletes line 10.
 R1(config)# ip access─list standard DENY_LOCAL
 R1(config─std─nacl)# no 10
or
 R1(config)# ip access─list extended Block_SSH
 R1(config─ext─nacl)# no 10
Modify: Delete a line and re─add it with the updated settings.
─ Key Difference Between Standard and Extended ACLs:
─ Standard ACL: Filters only based on (source IP address).
─ Extended ACL: Filter based on (source/destination IP, ports and protocol numbers).
Access Class:
Used to control management access (like SSH, Telnet) to a device, rather than filtering general network traffic.
Applied to VTY (Virtual Terminal) lines for remote access control.
Prevents unauthorized devices from accessing the router via SSH or Telnet.
R1(config)#access─list 20 permit 192.168.2.0 0.0.0.255  # using numbered ACL
R1(config)#line vty 0 4
R1(config─line)#access─class 20 in
OR
R1(config)#ip access─list standard ALLOW_SSH_ACCESS             # using Named ACL
R1(config─std─nacl)#permit 192.168.10.0 0.0.0.255
R1(config─std─nacl)#permit 10.0.0.0 0.0.0.255
R1(config)#line vty 0 4
R1(config─line)#access─class ALLOW_SSH_ACCESS in
Summary Notes:
─ Standard ACL ─ Source IP only → Place near Destination
─ Extended ACL ─ Protocol + Source + Destination → Place near Source
─ use the "ip access─group" command under the int level, not "ip access─list"
─ use the "ip access─class" command under VTY to control management access (like SSH, Telnet) to a device
─ An ACL does nothing until applied to an interface or VTY.
─ Every ACL ends with an implicit "deny ip any any" (automatic, invisible, default by cisco).
─ after creating an ACL, If someone forget to add a "permit ip any any", they may unintentionally block all other traffic because the implicit deny will drop it.
─ Exam Memory Phrase: “If it’s not permitted, it’s denied.”
protocol,Source and destination are must must.
DHCP Snooping (Defense):
─ "DHCP Snooping" is switch security feature that prevents "DHCP spoofing" by controlling which ports can a DHCP server connetc to.
─ Rogue server/untrusted hosts/unauthorized DHCP servers giving fake IP configurations.
─ DHCP Snooping Binding Table:  create records of IP–MAC–VLAN–Port from DORA (DHCP Discover, Offer, Request, and Acknowledge) messages.
─ Untrusted Ports: Client ports; DHCP Server packets blocked. by default all ports are untrusted when DHCP Snooping is enabled.
─ Trusted Ports: Uplink or server─facing ports where trusted DHCP servers are connected. The network administrator manually configures these ports as trusted.
─ protect against: Rogue DHCP servers, DHCP spoofing, DHCP starvation, Fake IP assignment
        S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping                # Enables the DHCP snooping feature,
        S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping vlan 10,20,30  # Enable for VLANs, DHCP snooping operates per VLAN, doesnt apply automatically for all VLAN by default.
        S1(config)# interface g0/1
        S1(config─if)# ip dhcp snooping trust   # Trust only uplinks / server ports, for trusted (DHCP server)
# untrusted port still allow Allows DHCP Discover / Request (client → server) and
# Blocks DHCP Offer / ACK (server → client).
        S1(config─if)# ip dhcp snooping database flash:dhcp_snoop.db  # Enable database persistence accross reboots.
        S1(config)# interface range g0/2─24            # additionally, Rate limit access ports, If a host floods DHCP requests faster than limit(hub)/fake server
        S1(config─if)# ip dhcp snooping limit rate 10  # protect against DHCP flooding/DHCP starvation attack.
# if limit exceed, drop DHCP packets or place the port in err─disabled state.
Dynamic ARP Inspection (DAI) [defense]:
─ Attacker sends fake ARP
Sender IP: 10.0.0.1
Sender MAC: AA:AA:AA:AA:AA:AA
Switch checks DHCP Snooping table, IP was offered to
10.0.0.1 → 00:11:22:33:44:55
─Mismatch detected → fake ARP packet dropped
─ DAI uses the DHCP Snooping binding table to validate ARP packets.
─ Switch checks the IP─to─MAC mappings against the DHCP snooping table or static entries.
─ Protects against: ARP spoofing / ARP cache poisoning / man─in─the─middle (MITM) attacks.
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping                # Ensure DHCP Snooping is enabled first
S1(config)# ip arp inspection vlan 10       # Enable DAI for VLAN 10
S1(config)# interface g0/1                  # Trusted port (uplink / server side / devices with static IP)
S1(config─if)# ip arp inspection trust      # Mark the port as trusted (legitimate ARP replies bypass inspection)
IP Source Guard (Defense):
─ Do NOT enable on a trusted port (uplink / server side / devices with static IP).
─ Blocks devices that manually assign fake/static IPs.
─ Only allows hosts whose IP address was assigned by DHCP (valid binding).
─ Uses the DHCP Snooping binding table to allow only the correct IP per port.
─ Protects against rogue clients / IP address spoofing.
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping vlan 10
S1(config)# interface g0/1
S1(config─if)# ip verify source     # enable IP Source Guard
Storm control (defense):
─ Traffic flood / flood attacks / broadcast storm attack.
─ Storm control enables the switch to monitor traffic levels and drop if storm control level—is exceeded.
─ Limits broadcast, multicast, unknown unicast storms.
        S1(config)# interface g0/2
        S1(config─if)# storm─control broadcast level 50
        S1(config─if)# storm─control multicast level 60
        S1(config─if)# storm─control unknown─unicast level 50
        S1(config─if)# storm─control action shutdown
Port security (defense):
S1(config)#int g0/1
S1(config─if)#switchport port─security
S1(config─if)#switchport port─security maximum 2
S1(config─if)#switchport port─security violation shutdown
Mode      Frame   Logging      Port State
Protect   Dropped No           Up
Restrict  Dropped log          Up
 Shutdown  Dropped log          down (Err─disabled) default mode.
Port Security Configuration:
        switchport port─security                               # Enable port security on the interface
        switchport port─security mac─address 0050.7966.6800    # Manually allow specific MAC (e.g., printer, server, camera)
        switchport port─security violation restrict            # Action on violation: shutdown / restrict / protect
        switchport port─security mac─address sticky            # Learn MACs dynamically and make them persistent
        switchport port─security maximum 2                     # Allow max 2 MACs (for PC + IP Phone setups)
        switchport port─security aging time 30                 # Clear dynamic MACs after 30 mins of inactivity
Violation Recovery:
errdisable recovery cause psecure─violation            # Enable auto─recovery for port security violations
errdisable recovery interval 30                        # Wait 30 seconds before auto─recovery kicks in
Manual recovery: shutdown and no shutdown
Port Security (by Default):
Status: Disabled
if enabled (Default Parameters):
Maximum MAC Addresses: 1
Violation Action: shutdown (If a second device connects to this port,  port goes into err─disabled state)
Sticky MAC Addresses: Disabled
Aging Time: Disabled (no automatic removal of MAC addresses)
802.1X Port─Based Network Access Control:
─ authenticates devices and users before granting them access to the network, using a combination of:
─ Supplicant: The client device requesting access (e.g., PC, phone) .
─ Authenticator: Switch or AP by enforcing access control.
─ Authentication Server: RADIUS
MAC Flooding (attack):
─ Large number of fake MAC/ HUB mode/man─in─the─middle attacks.
defense: Port Security
S1(config)#interface f0/1
S1(config─if)#switchport port─security
S1(config─if)#switchport port─security maximum 2
S1(config─if)#switchport port─security violation shutdown
 VLAN Hopping (Attack):
─ Attacker gains access to other VLANs by exploiting trunk negotiation or double─tagging.
Defense: Disable DTP, use manual trunking, restrict VLANs.
S1(config)#interface g0/1
S1(config─if)#switchport mode trunk
S1(config─if)#switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20
S1(config─if)#switchport nonegotiate
Port Security Violations (Attack):
─ Plug in unauthorized devices to gain network access.
Defense: Port Security to limit devices per port.
ARP Spoofing / ARP cache Poisoning (Attack):
─ Attackers manipulate the ARP cache to intercept or redirect network traffic.
Defense: Dynamic ARP Inspection (DAI)
Denial of Service (DoS) Attacks:
─ Overload network with excessive broadcast traffic, consuming bandwidth and resources, causing the network to become unavailable or unresponsive.
Defense: ACLs & Traffic Filtering
Broadcast Storm / Traffic Flooding (Attack):
─ Flood network with broadcast/multicast to cause DoS.
Defense: Storm Control
 IP Spoofing (Attack):
─ Forged source IP address to disguise their identity and gain unauthorized access to the network.
Defense: IP Source Guard (depends on DHCP Snooping)
Routing Table Poisoning (Attack):
─ Inject malicious routes into the routing table, potentially diverting or intercepting traffic.
Defense: Routing protocol authentication (OSPF, EIGRP, etc.)
R1(config)#router ospf 1
R1(config─router)#area 0 authentication message─digest
        R1(config)# interface g0/0
R1(config─router)#interface GigabitEthernet0/0
R1(config─if)#ip ospf authentication message─digest
DNS Spoofing (Attack):
─ Modify the DNS cache on the router, leading users to malicious websites instead of legitimate ones.
Defense: DNS Validation/ DNSSEC (DNS Security Extensions)
R1(config)#ip dns secure cache
R1(config)#ip dns validate domain
Summary notes:
─ DHCP Snooping default; all ports untrusted
─ DAI requires DHCP Snooping to fuction
─ IP Source Guard requires DHCP Snooping binding table
─ Default action of Port Security violation; shutdown (err─disabled)
─ Storm control is for broadcast; multicast, unknown unicast floods
─ MAC table flooding attack defense; Port security (max MAC, sticky, violation mode).
─ VLAN hopping defense; Disable DTP + manual trunk
─ ARP Spoofing defense; DAI
─ IP Spoofing defense; IP Source Guard
─ Rogue DHCP server defense; DHCP Snooping trusted ports
Q1: Difference between standard and extended ACL?
─ Standard: filter by source IP only, place near destination.
─ Extended: filter by protocol, source/dest IP/ports, place near source.
Q2: Implicit behavior of every ACL?
─ Implicit "deny ip any any" at the end.
Q3: How to apply ACLs to interfaces vs VTY lines?
─ Interface: ip access─group <ACL> in|out
─ VTY: ip access─class <ACL> in
Q4: How to insert, delete, or modify a line in a named ACL?
─ Insert:
R1(config─ext─nacl)# 15 permit icmp any any
─ Delete:
R1(config─ext─nacl)# no 10
─ Modify: Delete + re─add with new settings
Q5: Example extended ACL to block SSH from LAN 192.168.1.0/24 to server 10.1.1.10 but allow all else?
access─list 100 deny tcp 192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255 host 10.1.1.10 eq 22
access─list 100 permit ip any any
int g0/0
ip access─group 100 in
Q6: Purpose of DHCP Snooping?
─ Prevent rogue DHCP servers.
─ Build DHCP binding table (IP─MAC─VLAN─Port).
─ Protect against DHCP spoofing and starvation attacks.
Q7: Default port trust state in DHCP Snooping?
─ All ports untrusted by default. Trusted ports must be manually configured.
Q8: Commands to enable DHCP Snooping on VLAN 10?
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping vlan 10
S1(config)# interface g0/1
S1(config─if)# ip dhcp snooping trust
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping database flash:dhcp_snoop.db
Q9: How does rate limiting protect DHCP Snooping?
─ Limits DHCP requests per second from untrusted ports.
S1(config─if)# ip dhcp snooping limit rate 10
Q10: Purpose of DAI?
─ Protect against ARP spoofing, cache poisoning, MITM.
─ Validates ARP packets against DHCP Snooping binding table or static entries.
Q11: Why trusted ports required for DAI?
─ Uplink/server ports or devices with static IP are trusted so legitimate ARP traffic passes.
Q12: Commands to enable DAI for VLAN 10 and trust uplink?
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping
S1(config)# ip arp inspection vlan 10
S1(config)# interface g0/1
S1(config─if)# ip arp inspection trust
Q13: Purpose of IP Source Guard?
─ Blocks clients manually assigning fake/static IPs.
─ Only allows hosts with valid DHCP─assigned IP per port.
─ Uses DHCP Snooping binding table.
Q14: Should IPSG be enabled on uplink/trusted port?
─ No, only on access ports.
Q15: Commands to enable IPSG on port g0/1?
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping
S1(config)# ip dhcp snooping vlan 10
S1(config)# interface g0/1
S1(config─if)# ip verify source
Q16: Default violation mode and behavior?
─ Shutdown (err─disabled), drops frames and logs violation.
Q17: Three violation modes for Port Security?
Protect   → drop frames, port stays up
Restrict  → drop + log, port stays up
Shutdown  → drop + log, port err─disabled
Q18: Command to enable sticky MAC learning and max 2 MACs?
S1(config)# interface g0/1
S1(config─if)# switchport port─security
S1(config─if)# switchport port─security maximum 2
S1(config─if)# switchport port─security mac─address sticky
S1(config─if)# switchport port─security violation shutdown
Q19: How to recover from port─security shutdown?
─ Auto: errdisable recovery cause psecure─violation
─ Manual: shutdown / no shutdown
# 🔹 Storm Control
Q20: Purpose of Storm Control?
─ Protects against broadcast, multicast, unknown unicast floods.
─ Drops traffic when threshold exceeded.
Q21: Example command to enable on g0/2?
S1(config)# interface g0/2
S1(config─if)# storm─control broadcast level 50
S1(config─if)# storm─control multicast level 60
S1(config─if)# storm─control unknown─unicast level 50
S1(config─if)# storm─control action shutdown
# 🔹 VLAN Hopping & Other L2/L3 Attacks
Q22: VLAN Hopping attack and defense?
─ Attack: Access unauthorized VLAN via DTP / double─tagging.
─ Defense: Disable DTP, manual trunking, restrict allowed VLANs.
S1(config)# interface g0/1
S1(config─if)# switchport mode trunk
S1(config─if)# switchport trunk allowed vlan 10,20
S1(config─if)# switchport nonegotiate
Q23: MAC Flooding attack and defense?
─ Flood switch with fake MACs → CAM overflow → hub mode → MITM
─ Defense: Port Security
Q24: ARP Spoofing / ARP cache poisoning defense?
─ DAI (validate against DHCP Snooping table)
Q25: IP Spoofing defense?
─ IP Source Guard
Q26: Rogue DHCP server defense?
─ DHCP Snooping (trusted server ports only)
Q27: DoS attack defense?
─ ACLs, traffic filtering, rate limiting, storm control
Q28: Routing table poisoning defense?
─ Routing protocol authentication (OSPF/EIGRP)
R1(config─router)# area 0 authentication message─digest
Q29: DNS spoofing defense?
─ DNSSEC / DNS validation
R1(config)# ip dns secure cache
R1(config)# ip dns validate domain
# 🔹 Exam Memory Phrases
─ ACL: "If it’s not permitted, it’s denied."
─ Standard ACL: Near destination
─ Extended ACL: Near source
─ DAI/IPSG depend on DHCP Snooping table
─ Port Security default violation: shutdown (err─disabled)
─ Storm Control protects broadcast/multicast/unknown unicast floods
─ MAC Flooding → Port Security
─ VLAN Hopping → Disable DTP + manual trunk
─ ARP Spoof → DAI
─ IP Spoof → IP Source Guard
─ Rogue DHCP → DHCP Snooping (trusted ports)
VPN Types and Their Real─Life Uses:
─ Routers and firewalls are the physical devices that typically create, manage, and terminate VPN tunnels (or sometimes virtual devices, depending on the setup).
1. Site─to─Site VPN: Connect branch offices to HQ securely over public Internet (IPsec) or private WAN (MPLS).
IPsec VPN:
─ IPsec Tunnel + Layer 3 + (Router─to─Router) + Connects branch offices to HQ securely over the internet.
MPLS VPN:
─ MPLS (Service Provider) + Layer 2/3 + (CE Router─PE Router) + Private WAN provided by ISP with QoS and SLA guarantees.
─ The ISP uses MPLS labels inside its network instead of IP routing between sites.
─ Data travels through the ISP’s isolated, controlled network. expensive but more secure and reliable then IPsec VPN.
2. Remote Access VPN:
IPsec VPN:
─ IPsec Tunnel + Layer 3 + (Host─to─Router) + Individual users connect from home, using VPN client software (e.g Cisco AnyConnect, in IPsec mode)
─ Full network access as if inside corporate LAN.
SSL VPN:
─ SSL/TLS + Layer 7 + (Host─to─Router) + Access via browser or VPN client (e.g., Cisco AnyConnect in SSL mode).
─ Full network access when using VPN client or partial network access when using "client less".
3. Clientless VPN:
─ SSL/TLS (HTTPS) + Layer 7 + Browser─to─Router + User logs in via web portal to access internal web apps.
─ Subset of SSL VPN; no full network access.
4. Advanced Enterprise VPN Models: (Cisco proprietary)
DMVPN (Dynamic Multipoint VPN):
─ [IPsec + mGRE + NHRP] + Layer 3 + Dynamic Router─to─Router + Scalable, on─demand tunnels between multiple branch sites.
GETVPN (Group Encrypted Transport VPN):
─ [IPsec + GDOI] + Layer 3 + Group of Routers + Encrypts WAN traffic between multiple trusted sites using centralized key management.
Layer 3 Tunnel:  MPLS, IPsec, SSL VPN, GRE (with/without IPsec):
Use / Real─life Scenarios:
─ Companies often need to link multiple branch offices securely over the internet. L3 tunnels create a private path so the offices can communicate safely.
─ Employees working from home can access the company network through secure tunnels, keeping data protected.
─ Some tunnels let users securely log into web portals or internal apps from anywhere without exposing the network to the public.
─ Sometimes two networks need to “talk” over the internet or another network. L3 tunnels provide a simple, temporary, or secure path for data to flow between them.
─ MPLS:        Service provider VPN transport, Used with labels; scalable, traffic engineering, (complex, expensive but secure).
─ IPsec:       Encryption/security protocol for L3 tunnels     Used on public internet; site─to─site or remote access
─ SSL VPN:     Encrypted tunnel using SSL/TLS  Can be L7 (portal) or L3 (full tunnel)
─ GRE:         GRE is suitable for "simple tunnel, not secure, but with IPsec its secure.
Layer 2 Tunnels: VXLAN, L2VPN, EoIP (Ethernet over IP) and MPLS (L2.5),:
Use / Real─life Scenarios:
─ Extending Layer 2 domains across Layer 3 networks
─ Datacenter interconnects (multi─site DCs), remote office connectivity
─ VM migration in virtualized environments
─ Multi─site VLAN extension for enterprises or cloud tenants
─ Encapsulate Layer 2 Ethernet frames (MAC, ARP, DHCP, STP) for transport over an IP network.
─ Preserves original Layer 2 information such as MAC addresses, STP behavior, VLANs, and broadcast traffic.
VXLAN:
─ Encapsulates L2 frames inside UDP packets to traverse L3 networks.
─ Common in data centers and virtualization platforms (VM migration, multi-tenant networks).
─ Extends VLAN limitation (4094) using VNIs (16 million segments).
─ Does not replace VLAN; instead it extends Layer 2 networks across a Layer 3 underlay and massively increases segmentation scale beyond VLAN limits.
MPLS (often called Layer 2.5):
─ Sits between Layer 2 and Layer 3 (uses labels, not MAC or IP directly).
─ Used by service providers for scalable VPNs and traffic engineering (QoS, TE).
L2VPN (Layer 2 Virtual Private Network):
─ Service provider solution for extending customer LANs across WANs (E─Line, E─LAN, etc.).
─ Transparent to customer switching; preserves Layer 2 topology.
EoIP (Ethernet over IP):
─ Encapsulates Ethernet frames for transport over IP.
─ Often used on Mikrotik devices; simple L2 extension over routed networks.
VPN encryption:
In Transport Mode of VPN encryption (such as with IPsec), only the payload (data) of the packet is encrypted, while the header (including the source and destination IP addresses) remains unencrypted.
The routers or intermediate devices can still read the IP address information for routing purposes.
Summary note:
─ Site─to─Site ─ Router ↔ Router
─ Remote Access ─ Host ↔ Router
─ IPsec ─ Secure L3, payload encrypted
─ MPLS ─ ISP network, labels, QoS, SLA
─ SSL VPN ─ L7 browser or client tunnel
─ Clientless ─ SSL portal only
─ DMVPN ─ Dynamic branch─to─branch tunnels
─ GETVPN ─ Group WAN encryption, centralized keys
─ Layer2 VPN ─ VLANs across L3, preserves MAC/STP
─ VXLAN ─ L2 over L3/UDP, 16M segments via VNI
─ GRE ─ Tunnel only, not secure, use IPsec for encryption
MPLS:
─ MPLS is NOT the Internet. Think of MPLS as a private highway, a private WAN built by a service provider (like AT&T).
─ MPLS is used for internal company resource/connectivity.
─ While on mpls network, employees access the public Internet via:
branch → MPLS → HQ/Data Center → (Firewall + NAT) → Internet 🌍
─ Sometimes combined with SD─WAN:
─ Internal traffic → MPLS
─ Internet traffic → local ISP
─ SD─WAN decides per─packet / per─app
─ Most providers use PRIVATE IP addresses in the MPLS core, which is not reachable from the public Internet.
─ MPLS forwards traffic using labels, not IP routing, in the core.
─ MPLS labels can traverse only MPLS─capable routers.
─ If MPLS traffic enters a normal/public IP network, labels are removed and the traffic behaves like regular IP.
─ OSPF / IS─IS provide IP reachability in the provider core.
─ MPLS is an underlay that can carry L2VPN, L3VPN, and can be used by SD─WAN.
─ Providers use leased Layer─2 circuits, wavelengths, or dark fiber for long distances.
─ Packet forwarding happens via the LFIB using label push / swap / pop.
─ MPLS is: Expensive, Slow to provision, Rigid (static routing, manual QoS)
─ Applications need high reliability and low latency (VoIP, video conferencing, ERP, trading systems)
─ Bank connecting 50+ offices globally, Retail chain connecting multiple DCs
SD─WAN:
─ Dynamic Path Selection: SD─WAN constantly monitors traffic, network conditions, bandwidth, congestion, link failures, etc., to dynamically send data through the fastest, cheapest, or most reliable path at that moment.
─ Performance & Prioritization (Application Awareness): Standard routers see "packets"; SD─WAN sees "applications."
─ Prioritizes important traffic (VoIP, video, critical apps). Salesforce, Teams, and ERP systems get the "fast lane."
─ Low─priority traffic, such as social media or personal browsing, takes the "back roads" or is throttled to save bandwidth.
─ Reliable: SD─WAN can bond multiple connection types (Internet, broadband, 5G/LTE) to balance load and automatically reroute traffic if a link fails.
─ Cost Savings: Delivers the same level of performance and security while using cheaper broadband instead of expensive MPLS.
─ Security & Zero─Touch Provisioning:
─ Always─on VPN: Creates encrypted tunnels automatically between branches. Most modern SD─WAN solutions follow a SASE (Secure Access Service Edge) model, integrating firewalls directly into the cloud.
─ Plug and Play: A pre─configured device can be shipped to a new branch. A non─technical person plugs it in, and it downloads its instructions from the cloud—no on─site engineer required.
─ SD─WAN is software that builds and manages one or more secure "overlay tunnels" on top of underlay networks (Internet, MPLS, LTE, 5G) and intelligently steers traffic between multiple WAN uplinks
─ It provides centralized, application─aware, and dynamic control over multiple WAN transports, enabling better performance, resilience, and cost efficiency than traditional routing.
SD─WAN does not replace MPLS or the Internet; it controls how traffic uses them:
─ Software decides which path to use
─ When to fail over
─ Which application gets priority
SD─WAN does NOT have a single physical controller box like DNA Center; controllers are software components:
─ vManage   Management plane (GUI, configuration, monitoring)
─ vSmart    Control plane (routing policies, OMP)
─ vBond     Orchestration / authentication
─ WAN Edge  Data plane (ISR, ASR, vEdge, Catalyst 8000)
Controller components are hosted as software (VMs) in:
─ ESXi
─ KVM
─ Public cloud (AWS / Azure)
─ Overlay tunnels are between WAN Edge devices, not with controllers. Each tunnel is measured for latency, loss, and jitter by the SD─WAN Edge routers.
─ SD─WAN uses application─aware routing (application intent─based), not static metrics or admin distance.
Example policies:
If application ─ Voice:
loss < 1%
latency < 150 ms
jitter < 30 ms
prefer MPLS, else Internet
If application ─ Web:
prefer Internet
fail over to MPLS if loss > 5%
If application ─ Backup:
use cheapest available path
─ When a path degrades, SD─WAN Edge routers detect it within milliseconds and reroute traffic without waiting for link failure, much faster than OSPF, BGP, or static routing.
─ Edge routers make data─plane decisions based on real─time measurements and policies received from vSmart.
─ In SD─WAN, overlay tunnels are created automatically between edge routers; technically, they are IPsec tunnels enhanced with SD─WAN intelligence.
─ Controllers are NOT in the data path and never terminate overlay tunnels.
─ SD─WAN controllers communicate with WAN Edge routers over secure control─plane connections using standard IP connectivity (TLS/DTLS), while user traffic flows only through IPsec overlay tunnels between WAN Edge devices.
Communication:
Controllers (vSmart / vManage / vBond) — multiple VMs or cloud
↑
| Control─plane (TLS/DTLS) → Policies, routing info
↓
WAN Edge Router  ──(Overlay IPsec tunnel)── WAN Edge Router
↑
| Edge router builds & maintains tunnel automatically
Definitions:
─ Underlay ─ The real physical network, Internet, MPLS, Private fiber, LTE / 5G.
─ Overlay network — A virtual network built on top of the underlay using encapsulation.
─ SD─WAN and some tunnels (like GRE, VXLAN, IPsec) are overlay networks (not MPLS).
─ L3 tunnels (IPsec, GRE, SD─WAN) carry only IP traffic and require different IP subnets on each side of the tunnel.
─ L2 tunnels (VXLAN, L2TPv3, EoIP) carry full Ethernet frames and can extend the same Layer─2 LAN across a WAN or the Internet.
─ VXLAN and L2TPv3 already include their own Layer─3 encapsulation and do not require an additional GRE or IPsec tunnel to function. (IPsec may be added optionally for encryption.)
─ They transport Layer─2 Ethernet frames while using an IP (Layer─3) underlay.
Layer 2 Tunnels: Two DCs need VLAN extension for clustering, Running private MPLS lab or overlay network, Legacy apps requiring same subnet across sites.
Layer 3 Tunnels: when secure inter─office connectivity over the Internet is required, Small to medium business connecting branch office to HQ, Remote site─to─site connectivity.
Network Topology vs Network Fabric
Network Topology:
─ Defines the physical or logical layout of devices and connections.
─ Shows how switches, routers, and links are connected.
─ Static structure (cabling, ports, links).
─ Does NOT control traffic behavior.
─ Analogy: Topology is the roads.
Network Fabric:
─ An architecture/A virtualized overlay network built on top of an underlay, using centralized intelligence to dynamically control traffic flow, path selection, and policy enforcement.
─ Fabric is not just “a tunnel.”
It is: Overlay + Control Plane + Policy + Automation
This combination is what makes it a fabric.
─ Analogy: Fabric is the intelligent traffic system managed by an SDN controller.
Fabric dynamically:
─ Chooses paths (using ECMP, not STP)
─ Re─routes traffic during failures
─ Balances load across available links
─ Applies segmentation
─ Enforces policy
Important:
─ Fabric does NOT change the wiring.
─ It changes how traffic flows across the existing topology.
Key terms:
─ SDN controller, SD─WAN controller (centralized intelligence)
─ Overlay tunnels (GRE, IPsec, VXLAN)
─ ECMP (instead of STP)
Common confusion:
─ Cisco Data Center “FabricPath” is a specific implementation that replaces STP with ECMP in a leaf–spine design.
─ The general term “network fabric” refers to the architectural concept (overlay + intelligence + policy).
─ Cisco’s “fabric” is a product implementation of that concept.
─ They are related, but not exactly the same.
Used by:
