─ Cloud providers
─ Modern data centers
Q1: What is a Site─to─Site VPN and its common use?
─ Router─to─Router tunnel
─ Secures communication between branch offices and HQ
─ Uses IPsec over Internet or MPLS for private WAN
Q2: What is Remote Access VPN and how is it different from Site─to─Site?
─ Host─to─Router tunnel
─ Individual users connect from home or remote
─ Uses IPsec or SSL VPN
─ Provides full network access (IPsec) or partial/browser access (SSL)
Q3: What is Clientless VPN?
─ SSL/TLS tunnel via web browser
─ Provides access to internal apps only
─ No full network access
Q4: What is DMVPN and when is it used?
─ Dynamic Multipoint VPN (IPsec + mGRE + NHRP)
─ Layer 3, dynamic branch─to─branch tunnels
─ Scalable for multiple branches on─demand
Q5: What is GETVPN?
─ Group Encrypted Transport VPN (IPsec + GDOI)
─ Layer 3, group of routers
─ Encrypts WAN traffic with centralized key management
Q6: Difference between L2 and L3 tunnels?
─ L3 tunnels (IPsec, GRE, SD─WAN): carry IP traffic only, require different subnets
─ L2 tunnels (VXLAN, L2TPv3, EoIP): carry full Ethernet frames, extend LANs across WAN
Q7: What is VXLAN and its use case?
─ Encapsulates L2 frames over UDP/L3 network
─ Extends VLANs for multi─tenant data centers
─ Supports VM migration and large─scale L2 extension (16M segments via VNIs)
Q8: What is MPLS and why is it used?
─ Private WAN using label switching
─ Supports L2VPN and L3VPN
─ Provides QoS, SLA, and traffic engineering
─ Expensive, slow to provision, rigid
Q9: How does SD─WAN improve WAN connectivity?
─ Creates overlay tunnels over underlay networks (Internet, MPLS, LTE/5G)
─ Application─aware, dynamic path selection
─ Centralized policies via vSmart/vManage/vBond
─ Edge routers automatically reroute traffic on degradation
─ Prioritizes important apps (VoIP, ERP) and fails over lower─priority traffic
Q10: Difference between underlay and overlay networks?
─ Underlay: Physical network (Internet, MPLS, fiber, LTE/5G)
─ Overlay: Virtual network over underlay using encapsulation (GRE, IPsec, VXLAN)
─ SD─WAN and some tunnels are overlay networks; MPLS can be underlay
Q11: Network topology vs Network fabric?
─ Topology: Fixed physical/logical connections; shows how devices are connected
─ Fabric: Virtualized overlay with centralized intelligence controlling traffic dynamically
─ Fabric ─ Overlay + Control Plane + Policy + Automation
─ Fabric dynamically chooses paths (ECMP), re─routes traffic, balances load, applies segmentation, enforces policy
─ Fabric does not change physical wiring
Q12: Common confusion with Cisco FabricPath?
─ FabricPath: Cisco DC implementation replacing STP with ECMP in leaf─spine design
─ Network fabric: Architectural concept (overlay + intelligence + policy)
─ Related but not identical
Q13: Key components/terms of a network fabric?
─ SDN controller or SD─WAN controller
─ Overlay tunnels (GRE, IPsec, VXLAN)
─ ECMP (not STP)
─ Policy enforcement and automation
06. Automation and Programmability (10%):
controller─based networking, SDN, Northbound and Southbound,  REST─based APIs, JSON─encoded data, Ansible, terraform,
Controller─based networking (Services model):
(IaaS) Infrastructure:[AWS, azure, google cloud]
─ control and manage virtualized infrastructure, but without the need to own or
─ maintain physical hardware. Operating System, applications.
(PaaS) Platform: [Red Hat OpenShift/AWS beanstalk/Googles App Engine]
─VM with a preinstalled OS and all software/framework required to run your application.
(SaaS) Software:
─Gmail/ office 365
SDN platform:
─ Think of SDN as a “remote control” for your network — an architecture for centralized control and programmability inside a data center (or large campus/WAN).
SDN Network Controller:
─ Centralized control point with built─in northbound and southbound APIs.
─ Northbound and southbound describe "directions of communication" in SDN architecture — not specific technologies themselves.
─ Communication flow: Applications <──> (Northbound APIs) ── Controller ── (Southbound APIs) <──> Network Devices (e.g., routers, switches).
─ Control layer: Part of SDN architecture where the SDN controller resides.
─ Infrastructure layer: Network devices (e.g., switches, routers) that implement forwarding (data plane).
Notes:
─ Management Plane ─ Configuration, monitoring (e.g., SNMP, NETCONF, RESTCONF; runs on controller)
─ Control Plane ─ Decision making (e.g., routing, policies); depends on SDN platform (Enterprise SDN → devices | OpenFlow SDN → controller)
─ Data Plane ─ Forwarding actual data traffic (e.g., switches, routers)
A. Controller─Assisted SDN (Enterprise):
─ Cisco DNA Center/ACI/SD─WAN/Meraki (Enterprise SDN solutions, datacenter, campus, branch).
─ The controller is management-plane–centric:
- It monitors devices.
- Pushes configuration, influences decisions via policy, but does not execute them on the controller.
- Supports automation/orchestration.
─ Routers and switches keep their own control plane.
─ Distributed control plane (Device─centric control plane):
─ Devices (Router, Switch) keep their brain, make forwarding/decision processes locally; the controller assists decisions and pushes policies.
─ Local control plane (routing table, ACLs, VLANs, CAM table, MAC address learning, spanning-tree topology, QoS policies, interface counters, packet forwarding logic).
─ Intent─based, policy─driven, device─centric (distributed control plane), supports automation & orchestration.
─ Traditional protocols still used (ex. OSPF, BGP, STP).
─ Automation without losing control.
Note:
─ Intent─based    → what you want (defined by administrator), Example: “HR users can access HR apps only”
─ Controller      → translates intent into policies and applies them
─ Policy─driven   → derived from intent (rules to achieve it)
─ Device─centric  → devices execute decisions locally
B. Controller─Dominant SDN (Cloud/Virtualized):
─ OpenStack, OpenDaylight, ONOS, VMware NSX (Cloud providers/virtualization SDN platforms, Telecom / Service Providers).
─ Classical SDN model, Controller─centric control plane (centralized), Controller─driven decisions.
─ Controller thinks & decides, devices forward (data plane); flow─based forwarding.
─ Controller pushes every decision (flow entries, ACLs, VLANs, QoS, MPLS labels, tunneling rules, forwarding tables) using Openflow protocol.
─ Controller handles: centralized control plane, management plane, forwarding decisions, policy, and automation; end devices operate only the data plane.
─ Devices do NOT maintain: routing table, ACL database, VLAN/ACL logic, packet inspection.
─ Devices maintain locally: flow table, MAC/CAM cache, VLAN mapping, tunnel endpoints, interface/port counters, queues.
─ VMware NSX (special case: enterprise─grade, hybrid, cloud─focused, controller─assisted at hypervisor level; uses distributed switching; not pure OpenFlow SDN).
─ Traditional protocols not used at all (OSPF, BGP, STP).
Northbound Communication::
─ "Northbound API" Application─facing interface on the SDN controller.
─ A conceptual interface on the controller for external apps and monitoring tools, through which the controller receives instructions.
─ The controller uses software APIs (like REST API, gRPC, WebSocket) to expose the northbound interface for external applications (orchestration tools, monitoring systems, automation scripts) to communicate with it.
─ Applications instruct the controller (e.g., create VLAN, apply QoS, block traffic).
─ Conceptually: the application layer (manager) defines goals; the controller (brain) interprets them and executes through the network infrastructure.
─ Controller use its southbound interface to collect data and reports back statistics, status, performance metrics, and topology information.
─ Real─world controllers exposing northbound APIs: Cisco DNA Center, OpenDaylight, ONOS, Ryu, VMware NSX.
─ In SDN terminology, Northbound API examples: REST API, gRPC, WebSocket, automation/orchestration scripts;
─ and External systems using northbound APIs: OpenStack, Kubernetes, Nagios.
Southbound Communication:
─ "Southbound API" Device─facing interface used by the SDN controller.
─ A conceptual interface on the controller for end devices, through which the controller retrieves data and sends/pushes instructions so devices can receive configuration and control commands.
─ The controller uses southbound protocols (like OpenFlow, NETCONF,RESTCONF, SNMP, gNMI) to implement the southbound interface.
─ The controller program, configure, and collect operational data from network devices in the data plane (switches, routers, firewalls) using these protocols.
─ The controller pushes configurations, policies, and forwarding rules to devices.
─ Devices send feedback — such as flow statistics, link status, or topology data — back to the controller.
─ In SDN terminology, Southbound API examples: OpenFlow, NETCONF, RESTCONF, SNMP, gNMI.
─ SNMP ─ primarily monitoring/telemetry (not flow programming).
─ RESTCONF/NETCONF ─ configuration & state management.
─ OpenFlow ─ direct flow─table programming (pure SDN model).
─ Analogy: Controller (brain) sends commands to devices (muscles); devices execute and return status.
─ Southbound layers usually do not have a visible GUI for human operators; instead, they are machine─to─machine communications handled internally by the controller.
Applications / OSS / Orchestration
|
|  Northbound APIs (REST, gRPC, SDKs)
v
        |    SDN Controller    |
^
|  Southbound APIs (OpenFlow, NETCONF, RESTCONF, gNMI)
|
   Network Devices (Switches / Routers / Firewalls)
Orchestration:
─ Orchestration is the process of automating the configuration, coordination, and management of multiple network devices and services, ensuring they work together efficiently and in sync to meet network policies and goals.
─ e.g Automatically provisions a VPN, its required virtual network components (routers, switches, firewalls, etc.), configures the routing policies,
─ sets up network security rules, and ensures that the network is ready to use without manual configuration for each individual device.
─ e.g Cisco DNA Center, Ansible Network Automation.
Cisco DNA Center:
─ SDN controller for enterprise
─ 4500 Series, 3500 Series
Cisco ACI:
─ SDN controller for data centers
─ Cisco APIC (1U or 2U) or virtualized (vAPIC)
Cisco SD─WAN:
─ SD─WAN controller
─ vSmart,vBond  Controller is virtualized and runs on VMs in data centers, private or public cloud environments (e.g., AWS, Microsoft Azure).
Cisco Meraki:
─ Cloud─managed controller, Meraki Cloud cisco's own cloud infrastructure.
─ Meraki Cloud is hosted on Ciscos data centers, uses Ciscos own cloud infrastructure (which includes servers, storage, and networking equipment distributed globally)
─ It is multi─tenant, meaning many different Meraki customers share the same cloud platform, but the data and configurations are kept isolated for each customer.
─ Meraki Dashboard is the web─based interface that allows network administrators to configure, monitor, and troubleshoot Meraki devices (routers, switches, access points, and security appliances).
OpenStack:
─ Cloud Platform solution (Cloud Networking)
─ It can be installed on any server, virtual machine.
OpenDaylight:
─ Virtualized solution (Enterprise SDN, Data center)
─ It can be installed on any server, virtual machine, or container.
ONOS (Open Network Operating System):
─ Virtualized solution (telecom, inter─data center traffic)
─ generally installed on virtual machines or as containers
VMware NSX:
─ Virtualized solution (data center, create  virtual switches, routers, and firewalls in a fully automated environment..)
─ runs on VMware vSphere environments, vCenter Server
─ VMware NSX is installed on a VMware vSphere environment.
CISCO DNA Center Appliance:
─ Client Initial setup, includes assigning a management IP, enabling SNMP, CDP/LLDP, and SSH.
─ Automatic device discovery using protocols like CDP, LLDP, SNMP, or REST APIs.
─ Template─based automation: VLANs, ACLs, QoS, and security policies can be auto─applied via pre─defined templates.
─ Automated software image management for network devices.
─ Cisco ISE (Identity Services Engine) is a separate identity/access control solution that can be integrated with DNA Center.
─ Using Cisco ISE and Cisco TrustSec, enforce security policies VLAN, ACL, QoS across devices.
─ A device in "Managed State" means it is actively monitored and configured by Cisco DNA Center.
Security Models:
Controller─Based (e.g., Cisco DNA Center):
─ Uses a whitelisting model — everything is blocked unless explicitly allowed.
Traditional Networks:
─ Use a blacklisting model — everything is allowed unless explicitly blocked.
Summary notes:
─Controller─Assisted ─ Devices decide, Controller suggests → Uses OSPF/BGP/STP
─Controller─Dominant ─ Controller decides, Devices act → Flow─based forwarding
─Northbound API ─ App → Controller
─Southbound API ─ Controller → Device
─Orchestration  ─ Automate multi─device config & policies
─Intent─based   ─ Define outcomes
─Policy─driven  ─ Rules control behavior
─VMware NSX     ─ Hybrid SDN at hypervisor, distributed switching
REST─based APIs:
─ Definition: Any API that follows the principles of REST ( Representational State Transfer) but may not fully implement all REST constraints.
REST API:
─ Definition: An API that fully follows REST principles: stateless, client─server, layered, cacheable, uniform interface.
─ [A stateless device doesn't remember information about ongoing connections]
─ General─purpose communication over HTTP protocol between clients and servers..
─ REST is Not a protocol, but a set of guidelines that applications can follow to ensure that they are designed in a stateless, scalable, and flexible manner.
─ GET, POST, PUT, and DELETE is used as standard HTTP verbs to manage resources.
─ REST is stateless, means each request from the client must contain all necessary information, Server does not store client context between requests.
─ JSON/XML is typically used to transfer data.
─ REST APIs use structured data formats (JSON/XML) but the data schema is custom and API─specific, unlike YANG─based network models.
─ Custom JSON schema for how data "field name" should be structured. This could vary from device to device.(e.g., "interface": "Gig0/1" or "name": "Gig0/1)
─ No native support for YANG, or deep network model logic, or network device configurations, generally for (e.g., monitoring systems, SDN dashboards, telemetry).
─ The data or services that the API exposes are typically accessed via URLs (Uniform Resource Locators).
─ Ciscos DNA Center and Meraki Dashboard use REST APIs.
RESTconf(JSON):
─ RESTconf is a protocol, type of REST API which uses REST principles, but it is more specific in its use case,
─ Designed to simplify the interaction with network devices.
─ Like REST, its Stateless and each request carry all necessary context.
─ JSON is the default data format for RESTCONF, is lightweight and human─readable.
─ YANG data models/structure is used typically to represent configuration and state data, so data format is consistent across different vendors.
─ Simplified way to access and manipulate YANG─modeled data over HTTP.
─ Good for modern network devices (e.g., Cisco IOS XE, Juniper, Nokia)
─ RESTCONF is lightweight, easy to work with, and integrates well with existing IT tools (e.g., Ansible, Terraform).
─ No full transaction support (e.g., cant validate, commit, rollback like NETCONF).
─ Not atomic (atomic ─ configuration changes are entirely applied  or not at all; RESTCONF may apply some changes even if others fail).
─ For most DNA Center tasks, RESTCONF and REST APIs are the default protocols, making it accessible, simple to use, and easy to automate configuration, monitoring.
─ While RESTCONF itself doesnt have native transaction support, Cisco DNA Center Provides "Built─In" configuration Validation and Rollback at a higher layer.
─ and NETCONF is also supported and comes into play when you need more advanced control over device configurations in cisco DNA center.
─ REST APIs, including RESTCONF, integrate easily with DevOps pipelines, ITSM systems, and other automation tools (like Ansible, Terraform, and Python scripts).
─ RESTCONF require  "restconf" service to be running on the network device.
─ In most modern network devices ( Cisco IOS─XE, Junos..) these services are built─in and enabled via configuration.
─ Communication: HTTP/HTTPS
─ Operation style: REST verbs (GET, PUT, PATCH, DELETE)
─ RESTCONF doesn’t have POST because device configuration is strictly defined by YANG models, and all create/modify actions are handled via PUT (replace) or PATCH (partial update).
─ POST is only needed for RPC─style actions, not standard CRUD.
─ RESTCONF can encode data in XML for devices or tools that require it. Less common today, but still supported.
NETCONF(XML):
─ Unlike REST, its a Stateful protocol; maintains session between client and server.
─ XML for data encoding and also relies on YANG data models for configuration and state data.
─ More complex compared to REST and RESTconf, but powerful, mostly used in network management.
─ NETCONF is built from the ground up for managing network devices.
─ Strong control over device configs, atomic transactions, building robust, safe automation workflows.
─ NETCONF support Full Transaction Model, Candidate Configuration,
─ You edit a "candidate" datastore first, not the live config, Validate, Commit and Automatic rollback if needed.
─ Communication: SSH (RPC)
─ Operation style: RPC calls (edit─config, get─config)
─ NETCONF require  "netconf" service to be running on the network device.
─ In most modern network devices ( Cisco IOS─XE, Junos..) these services are built─in and enabled via configuration.
REST Notes:
Client: The administrator’s automation tool, script, or application that sends the API request.
Content─Type: application/json  ─ Client sending data in JSON format (POST or PUT).
Accept: application/json        ─ In response client expecting data in JSON format.
REST API vs CRUD(Action (Concept):
HTTP Verb   CRUD Operation    Example (Network Context)
"POST"      Create            Configure a new interface
"GET"       Read              Retrieve interface details
"PUT"       Update (Replace)  Replace full interface configuration
"PATCH"     Update (Partial)  Change only IP address
"DELETE"    Delete            Remove interface configuration
HTTP Status Codes for REST APIs:
REST API:
1. 200 ─ OK (Request succeeded; the response contains the requested data.)
2. 400 ─ Bad Request (invalid request, wrong syntax, missing field, etc.)
3. 401 ─ Unauthorized (you need permission, haven’t logged in or provided valid credentials.)
4. 403 ─ Forbidden (You’re authenticated, you dont have permission, not allowed to perform the action.)
5. 404 ─ Not Found  (resource doesnt exist, an interface doesnt exist, but you send GET requet for the interface)
6. 500 ─ Internal Server Error (unexpected error on the server side, API service crashes or has a bug.)
7. 502 ─ Bad Gateway (no response from an upstream server, Controller forwards your request to a switch API — switch didn’t respond)
8. 503 ─ Service Unavailable (down for maintenance, or facing a temporary issue, DNA Center API temporarily unavailable for upgrade)
──
key note:
2xx ─ Success ✅
4xx ─ Client─side problem (your request is wrong or unauthorized) 🚫
5xx ─ Server─side problem (API or controller issue) 💥
Q1: What is SDN (Software Defined Networking)?
─ A networking architecture that centralizes control and enables programmability of network infrastructure.
Q2: What are the three layers of SDN architecture?
─ Application Layer, Control Layer, Infrastructure Layer.
Q3: Where does the SDN controller reside?
─ Control Layer.
Q4: What is the infrastructure layer in SDN?
─ Network devices (routers, switches, firewalls) responsible for forwarding traffic.
Q5: What is the role of the SDN controller?
─ Centralized control point that manages network behavior through APIs.
Q6: What are the three network planes?
─ Management Plane, Control Plane, Data Plane.
Q7: What does the Management Plane do?
─ Configuration, monitoring, and device management.
Q8: Which protocols commonly operate in the Management Plane?
─ SNMP, NETCONF, RESTCONF.
Q9: What does the Control Plane do?
─ Makes forwarding decisions (routing, policies).
Q10: What does the Data Plane do?
─ Forwards actual data packets based on control─plane decisions.
Q11: What is a Northbound API in SDN?
─ Application─facing interface used by applications to communicate with the SDN controller.
Q12: What is a Southbound API in SDN?
─ Device─facing interface used by the controller to communicate with network devices.
Q13: What protocols commonly implement Northbound APIs?
─ REST API, gRPC, WebSocket.
Q14: What protocols commonly implement Southbound APIs?
─ OpenFlow, NETCONF, RESTCONF, SNMP, gNMI.
Q15: Which protocol is used for direct flow─table programming in SDN?
─ OpenFlow.
Q16: What is SNMP mainly used for in SDN?
─ Monitoring and telemetry.
Q17: What is Controller─Assisted SDN (Enterprise SDN)?
─ A model where the controller pushes policies but network devices keep their own control plane.
Q18: What is another name for Controller─Assisted SDN?
─ Device─centric SDN.
Q19: In Controller─Assisted SDN, where does the control plane reside?
─ On the network devices.
Q20: Are traditional protocols used in Controller─Assisted SDN?
─ Yes (OSPF, BGP, STP).
Q21: Examples of Controller─Assisted SDN platforms?
─ Cisco DNA Center, Cisco ACI, Cisco SD─WAN, Cisco Meraki.
Q22: What is Controller─Dominant SDN?
─ A model where the controller makes all network decisions and devices only forward traffic.
Q23: Where is the control plane located in Controller─Dominant SDN?
─ On the SDN controller.
Q24: What do devices maintain in Controller─Dominant SDN?
─ Flow tables and forwarding functions.
Q25: Are traditional routing protocols used in pure SDN?
─ No.
Q26: Examples of Controller─Dominant SDN platforms?
─ OpenDaylight, ONOS, OpenStack, VMware NSX.
Q27: What is Cisco DNA Center?
─ Enterprise SDN controller for campus networks.
Q28: What is Cisco ACI used for?
─ SDN solution for data center networking.
Q29: What controller manages Cisco ACI?
─ Cisco APIC.
Q30: What is Cisco SD─WAN?
─ Controller─based solution for centralized WAN management.
Q31: What controllers are used in Cisco SD─WAN?
─ vSmart and vBond.
Q32: What is Cisco Meraki?
─ Cloud─managed networking platform controlled through the Meraki Dashboard.
Q33: What is the Meraki Dashboard?
─ Web─based interface used to configure and monitor Meraki devices.
Q34: What is orchestration in networking?
─ Automation of configuration, coordination, and management of multiple network devices and services.
Q35: Examples of orchestration tools?
─ Cisco DNA Center, Ansible.
Q36: What security model is commonly used in controller─based networking?
─ Whitelisting (deny by default).
Q37: What security model is commonly used in traditional networks?
─ Blacklisting (allow by default).
Q38: What does REST stand for?
─ Representational State Transfer.
Q39: Is REST a protocol?
─ No, it is an architectural style.
Q40: What transport protocol do REST APIs use?
─ HTTP or HTTPS.
Q41: What data formats are commonly used in REST APIs?
─ JSON or XML.
Q42: What does stateless mean in REST?
─ Each request contains all necessary information; the server does not store session state.
Q43: Which HTTP method retrieves data?
─ GET.
Q44: Which HTTP method creates resources?
─ POST.
Q45: Which HTTP method replaces a full resource configuration?
─ PUT.
Q46: Which HTTP method partially updates a resource?
─ PATCH.
Q47: Which HTTP method deletes a resource?
─ DELETE.
Q48: What is RESTCONF?
─ REST─based protocol used to configure network devices using YANG models.
Q49: What data modeling language does RESTCONF use?
─ YANG.
Q50: What data format does RESTCONF commonly use?
─ JSON.
Q51: Is RESTCONF stateless or stateful?
─ Stateless.
Q52: Does RESTCONF support full transactions?
─ No.
Q53: What transport protocol does RESTCONF use?
─ HTTP/HTTPS.
Q54: What is NETCONF?
─ A protocol used to install, manipulate, and delete network device configurations.
Q55: Is NETCONF stateless or stateful?
─ Stateful.
Q56: What data encoding format does NETCONF use?
─ XML.
Q57: What data modeling language does NETCONF use?
─ YANG.
Q58: What transport protocol does NETCONF use?
─ SSH.
Q59: What key feature does NETCONF support?
─ Full transaction model (validate, commit, rollback).
Q60: What is the candidate configuration datastore in NETCONF?
─ A staging configuration where changes are validated before committing to the running configuration.
Q61: What does HTTP status code 200 mean?
─ Request succeeded.
Q62: What does HTTP status code 400 mean?
─ Bad request.
Q63: What does HTTP status code 401 mean?
─ Unauthorized (authentication required).
Q64: What does HTTP status code 403 mean?
─ Forbidden (authenticated but not allowed).
Q65: What does HTTP status code 404 mean?
─ Resource not found.
Q66: What does HTTP status code 500 mean?
─ Internal server error.
Q67: What does HTTP status code 502 mean?
─ Bad gateway.
Q68: What does HTTP status code 503 mean?
─ Service unavailable.
JSON─encoded data:
Why JSON:
─ JSON is not a protocol, neither a programming language; JSON is a foramt of storing data.
─ Most northbound interfaces are REST─based (HTTP/HTTPS), and REST typically serializes data in JSON.
─ JSON as a Standard Data Format in APIs (REST APIs), RESTCONF and NETCONF.
─ Applications (Python, JavaScript, orchestration engines, cloud services) understand JSON natively.
─ JSON is Compact, lightweight, easy to read, and faster to transmit than XML — ideal for APIs, network automation, and real─time data exchange.
─ Controllers query device information automatically via southbound protocols (NETCONF, RESTCONF, OpenFlow, gNMI, SNMP, etc.), and build/store a live inventory in its DB; then the controller uses JSON format to represent and exchange this data via northbound APIs (REST), enabling real-time topology/status awareness and dynamic inventory/automation.
─ Storing and Retrieving Configuration Data: Using northbound APIs (REST), device configurations can be exported in JSON format, stored externally, and later re-imported for restoration or automation.
─ Interoperability with Third─Party Tools and APIs, (modern automation and cloud platforms (AWS, Azure, Python, Postman, Grafana, etc.) natively speak JSON).
JSON Structure:
Object: ─ Collection of key–value pairs inside curly brace {}  # { "name": "John" }
Key:  ─ Always a string in double quotes, followed by :        # "age": 25
Value: ─ Can be one of the following types:
─ String: Enclosed in double quotes ("")                     # "New York"
─ Number: A numeric value, does not require quotes.
─ Boolean: A true or false value, does not require quotes.
─ Array: An ordered list of values, enclosed in square brackets [].     # ["Math", "Science"]
─ Object: A nested set of key─value pairs, enclosed in curly braces {}.
─ Null: (null) Represents a null key value (no value).
"" string.
:  Separates keys and values in objects.
,  Separates items (key─value pairs in objects, elements in arrays).
[] Array
"* Semicolons ; / Hipen ─ are not a part of the JSON specification and should not be used in the structure of JSON data.
{ 'name': 'Alice' }                ❌ Single quotes not allowed
{ name: "Alice" }                  ❌ Missing quotes around key
{ "name": "Alice"; }               ❌ Semicolon not allowed
{ "name"─ "Alice"}                 ❌ Hiphen not allowed
{ "name": "Alice", }               ❌ no trailing comma allowed after very last element.
{ "name": "Alice" } { "age": 30 }  ❌ Multiple root objects no allowed.
# this is a comment                ❌ Comments are not allowed at all in standard JSON
───── example JSON file
{
"name": "John",                # KEY:VALUE
"age": 30,                     # Number
"isStudent": false,            # Boolean
"subjects": ["Math", "Civil"], # Array
"address": {                   # Object (nested object, as key:value)
"_comment": "Following is an address field", # special way of keeping comments in JSON
"street": "123 Main St",
"city": "New York",
"postalCode": "10001"
},
"preferences": {              # Another nested Object
"color": "blue",
"food": "pizza"             # last element doesnt end with comma.
}
}
───── Real life network config (compact style).
{
"deviceName": "Branch─RTR─01",
"mgmtIP": "10.10.20.5",
"isCoreDevice": false,
"roles": ["edge","wan","branch"],
"interfaces": {
"_comment": "Interface config",
"Gig0/0": { "desc": "WAN─Uplink", "ip": "192.168.10.1/24", "status": "up" },
"Gig0/1": { "desc": "LAN─Side",   "ip": "10.0.0.1/24",    "status": "up" }
},
"vlans": [
{ "id": 10, "name": "Users",  "gw": "10.10.10.1/24" },
{ "id": 20, "name": "Guests", "gw": "10.10.20.1/24" }
],
"routing": { "protocol": "OSPF", "area": 0, "neighbors": ["10.10.20.6","10.10.20.7"] },
"telemetry": { "restconf": true, "netconf": true, "gnmi": false }
}
REST API in JSON:
POST /network/device/config HTTP/1.1
Host: device.example.com
Content─Type: application/json
Accept: application/json
{
"interface": "GigabitEthernet0/1",
"ip_address": "192.168.1.1",
"subnet_mask": "255.255.255.0"
}
Note: custom JSON schema for how data should be structured. This could vary from device to device.
Summary notes:
─ REST ─ Stateless, HTTP verbs, JSON/XML, general APIs
─ RESTCONF ─ REST + YANG + network device config, JSON, lightweight
─ NETCONF ─ Stateful, XML, YANG, candidate config, atomic transactions
─ JSON ─ Key─Value, Double quotes, No trailing comma, Nested objects allowed
─ HTTP Codes ─ 2xx ✅, 4xx 🚫, 5xx 💥
─ CRUD → POST/Create, GET/Read, PUT/Replace, PATCH/Partial, DELETE/Delete
RESTCONF in JSON (YANG): “RESTCONF ─ REST + YANG + network device focus”
POST /restconf/data/network─device:configurations HTTP/1.1
Host: device.example.com
Content─Type: application/yang─data+json
Accept: application/yang─data+json
{
"network─device:interface": {
"name": "GigabitEthernet0/1",
"enabled": true,
"ipv4": {
"address": {
"ip": "192.168.1.1",
"netmask": "255.255.255.0"
}
}
}
}
Note: This JSON follows the YANG data model, with fields like interface, ipv4, and address explicitly defined.
NETCONF in XML(YANG): “NETCONF ─ Stateful, XML, full transaction, strong network control”
<rpc xmlns─"urn:ietf:params:xml:ns:netconf:base:1.0">
<edit─config>
<target>
<running/>
</target>
<config>
<interfaces xmlns─"urn:ietf:params:xml:ns:yang:ietf─interfaces">
<interface>
<name>GigabitEthernet0/1</name>
<enabled>true</enabled>
<ipv4 xmlns─"urn:ietf:params:xml:ns:yang:ietf─ip">
<address>
<ip>192.168.1.1</ip>
<netmask>255.255.255.0</netmask>
</address>
</ipv4>
</interface>
</interfaces>
</config>
</edit─config>
</rpc>
Note:  edit─config operation allows for atomic changes to the running configuration. You can later commit or rollback if needed.
─ NETCONF is RPC/XML─based, whereas RESTCONF uses REST/HTTP + JSON/XML.
Terraform: Infrastructure provisioning
─ Create VPCs, subnets, networks
─ Create VMs / instances
─ Create load balancers, databases
─ Allocate infrastructure resources via provider APIs
Ansible / Chef / Puppet / NETCONF / RESTCONF: Configuration management
─ Configure operating systems
─ Install and manage packages
─ Manage services
─ Configure routers, switches, firewalls (via device APIs / SSH / NETCONF / RESTCONF)
─ Enforce desired configuration state
─ Retrieve device state and operational data (YANG─modeled configuration)
─ JSON or XML payloads for configuration (RESTCONF JSON, NETCONF XML/YANG)
SDN Controller + OpenFlow: Control packet forwarding (Data Plane Programming)
─ Control packet forwarding behavior
─ Install flow rules on switches
─ Centralize network control logic
─ Program the data plane
─ Real─time traffic steering and forwarding decisions
Ansible:
─ Ansible is a configuration management, automation, and orchestration tool.
─ Uses YAML playbooks to define desired state of infrastructure or network devices.
─ Agentless — communicates over SSH or APIs.
─ Ansible is mostly used for Linux/Windows server configuration: Application deployments and service management, Firewall rules, packages, users, Network device configuration (routers, switches, firewalls).
─ Often used to: Deploy SDN controller software, Configure device interfaces, VLANs, ACLs, Push baseline configurations.
data structure:
─ Ansible playbook should start with `───`.
─ Ansible playbooks use YAML syntax.
─ YAML uses spaces — not tabs — for indentation.
─ Use 2 spaces per level (the most common YAML indentation style).
─ Ansible ensures idempotency, re─apply changes only if required.
ansible_project/
├── inventory.ini          # Lists hosts or groups of hosts (IP, credentials, connection type)
├── router_config.yml      # Playbook YAML file describing tasks and desired stat
├── roles/                 # Optional, Collection of playbooks, tasks, templates for modularity
│   ├── network/
│   │   ├── tasks/         # Single action to execute on target host
│   │   │   └── main.yml
│   │   ├── templates/
│   │   └── vars/
├── group_vars/            # Optional, variables for groups of hosts
└── host_vars/             # Optional, variables for individual hosts
# Module, Task library (e.g., cisco.ios.ios_int, file, package)
inventory file: #inventory.ini
[routers]
router1 ansible_host─192.168.1.254 ansible_user─admin ansible_password─your_password
router2 ansible_host─192.168.1.253 ansible_user─admin ansible_password─your_password
#command
ansible─playbook ─i inventory.ini router_config.yml
 Router config file: router_config.yml
───                                  # Ansible playbook always starts with ───
─ name: Base configuration           # PLAY name (overall objective)
hosts: routers                     # Inventory group to target
gather_facts: no                   # Network devices don't use Linux facts by default
connection: network_cli            # Use network device CLI over SSH
become: yes                        # Enter enable mode (privilege escalation)
vars:                              # Play─level variables
ospf_process_id: 1
ospf_area: 0
tasks:                             # list (executed top to bottom)
─ name: Gather Cisco IOS facts   # TASK 1
cisco.ios.ios_facts:           # MODULE (collects device facts)
gather_subset:
─ all                      # Hostname, interfaces, OS version, etc.
register: ios_facts_output     # Save results to variable # Facts now available: ansible_net_hostname, ansible_net_interfaces, ansible_net_version etc
─ name: Get hostname           # TASK 2
      debug:                         # MODULE: debug
msg: "Router hostname is {{ ansible_net_hostname }}" #display a fact
─ name: Configure interface    # TASK 3
cisco.ios.ios_int:             # MODULE: interface configuration
name: GigabitEthernet0/0
description: "LAN Interface"
enabled: true                # no shutdown
ip_address: 192.168.1.1
netmask: 255.255.255.0
state: up                    # Desired interface state
notify: save running config    # Trigger handler if config changes
─ name: Configure OSPF                 # TASK 3
cisco.ios.ios_ospf:            # MODULE: OSPF configuration
process_id: "{{ ospf_process_id }}"
router_id: 1.1.1.1
networks:
─ network: 192.168.1.0
wildcard_mask: 0.0.0.255
area: "{{ ospf_area }}"
state: present               # Ensure OSPF exists
─ name: Apply config only on IOS XE # TASK 4
      debug:
msg: "This router is running IOS XE"
when: "'XE' in ansible_net_version"   # Conditional execution using facts
handlers:                          # HANDLERS (run only if notified)
─ name: save running config
cisco.ios.ios_command:         # MODULE: raw IOS command execution
commands:
─ write memory             # Save config only when changes occurred
───────────── teraform:
─ Terraform is an Infrastructure as Code (IaC) tool.
─ Used to provision and manage cloud infrastructure in a declarative way.
─ Supports multi─cloud: AWS, Azure, GCP, VMware, etc.
─ Ensures consistent and reproducible infrastructure deployments.
─ Terraform never logs into VMs. It talks only to provider APIs, only provisions infrastructure, never configures OS packages.
─ HCL is the standard and expected format, use .tf as file extension.
terraform_project/
├── main.tf           # Main resources (VPCs, subnets, servers)
├── variables.tf      # Input variables
├── outputs.tf        # Values to display after apply
├── provider.tf       # Cloud/provider configuration
├── terraform.tfstate # State file (auto─generated)
└── .terraform/       # Provider plugins (auto─generated)
# main.tf
# TERRAFORM SETTINGS
terraform {
required_version ─ ">─ 1.5"
required_providers {
aws    ─ { source ─ "hashicorp/aws" version ─ "~> 5.0" }
google ─ { source ─ "hashicorp/google" version ─ "~> 5.0" }
}
}
# PROVIDERS
provider "aws" {
region ─ "us─east─1"
}
provider "google" {
project ─ "my─gcp─project─id"
region  ─ "us─central1"
zone    ─ "us─central1─a"
}
# AWS NETWORK
resource "aws_vpc" "main_vpc" {
cidr_block ─ "10.0.0.0/16"
tags       ─ { Name ─ "aws─vpc" }
}
resource "aws_subnet" "public_subnet" {
vpc_id                  ─ aws_vpc.main_vpc.id
cidr_block              ─ "10.0.1.0/24"
map_public_ip_on_launch ─ true
}
# AWS EC2 INSTANCE
resource "aws_instance" "aws_vm_1" {
ami           ─ "ami─0c02fb55956c7d316"
instance_type ─ "t2.micro"
subnet_id     ─ aws_subnet.public_subnet.id
tags          ─ { Name ─ "aws─demo─vm" }
}
# GCP NETWORK
resource "google_compute_network" "main_network" {
name                    ─ "gcp─vpc"
auto_create_subnetworks ─ false
}
resource "google_compute_subnetwork" "public_subnet" {
name          ─ "gcp─subnet"
ip_cidr_range ─ "10.10.1.0/24"
  network       ─ google_compute_network.main_network.id
region        ─ "us─central1"
}
# GCP VM
resource "google_compute_instance" "gcp_vm_1" {
name         ─ "gcp─demo─vm"
machine_type ─ "e2─micro"
zone         ─ "us─central1─a"
  boot_disk {
initialize_params { image ─ "debian─cloud/debian─11" }
}
network_interface {
    network    ─ google_compute_network.main_network.id
subnetwork ─ google_compute_subnetwork.public_subnet.id
access_config {}   # Public IP
}
}
terraform init    # Initialize working directory
terraform plan    # Preview infrastructure changes
terraform apply   # Apply changes and create resources
terraform destroy # Tear down resources
─────────── chef:
Chef is a configuration management and automation platform that uses a pull─based agent model to enforce desired state configurations on infrastructure devices.
Servers & OS Management (Most Common): Linux / Windows servers,Package installation, Service management, User accounts, Firewall rules
Cloud Infrastructure: AWS / Azure / GCP VMs, Auto─scaling groups, Immutable infrastructure
Network Devices: (Less Common, but Valid)
──── chef is complex
Admin → Chef Workstation → Chef Infra Server → Chef Infra Client (Node)
Chef Workstation: (Write cookbooks/recipes, Test them, Upload them using knife to infra server)
Chef infra server:(Central repository Stores:Cookbooks,Recipes,Policies, Node data.
Chef Infra Client (Agent): Runs on managed nodes periodically(servers, routers, VMs), Pulls configuration from Chef Infra Server, not push.
knife cookbook upload network
knife cookbook upload router_config
Cookbook:Collection of configuration logic, Recipes, Attributes, Templates, Metadata
Recipe: Ruby file, Describes desired state, Declarative (what, not how)
my_cookbook/
├── recipes/        # Recipes are here
│   ├── default.rb  # Default recipe
│   └── router_config.rb
├── attributes/     # Optional, for default variables
│   └── default.rb
├── templates/      # Optional, for configuration templates
│   └── template_file.erb
├── files/          # Optional, static files to copy to nodes
│   └── example.conf
├── libraries/      # Optional, custom Ruby code
├── resources/      # Optional, custom resources
├── metadata.rb     # Describes cookbook (name, version, dependencies)
├── README.md       # Documentation
└── test/           # Optional, test recipes/specs
router_config.rb (Chef Recipe):
# router_config.rb
# Set the router's int  configuration
network_int  'GigabitEthernet0/0' do
ip_address '192.168.1.1'
netmask '255.255.255.0'
action :add
          description 'LAN int '
end
# Enable OSPF routing protocol
ospf 'ospf' do
process_id 1
          network '192.168.1.0 0.0.0.255'
          area '0'
action :enable
end
# Optionally, configure another network for OSPF
ospf 'ospf' do
process_id 1
          network '10.1.1.0 0.0.0.255'
          area '0'
action :enable
end
───────────── puppet:
─ Puppet is a configuration management and automation tool, Supports push or pull model (agent or agentless mode).
─ Puppet is less common in network automation than Chef or Ansible but still valid. Mostly used for Linux / Windows servers,Package installation, Service management, User accounts, Firewall rules and Cloud instances (AWS, Azure, GCP).
Admin → Puppet Master (Server) → Puppet Agent (Nodes)
─ Admin writes manifests/modules on Puppet Master
─ Puppet Agent on node requests configuration
─ Master provides catalog (compiled instructions)
─ Agent applies configuration on node
─ Reports compliance back to Master
Puppet Master (Server)
├── Manifests
│   └── .pp files defining desired state for nodes
├── Modules
│   ├── Classes
│   ├── Templates
│   ├── Files
│   └── Facts
├── Catalog Compiler
│   └── Compiles manifests and modules into node─specific catalogs
├── Certificate Authority (CA)
│   └── Manages SSL certificates for secure agent─server communication
├── Report Processor
│   └── Receives compliance reports from Puppet Agents
└── Configuration Repository
    └── Stores environment configurations, hiera data, policies
#router_config.pp
# Configure int  GigabitEthernet0/0
exec { 'configure_int _GigabitEthernet0/0':
command ─> 'ssh admin@192.168.1.1 "int  GigabitEthernet0/0\nip address 192.168.1.1 255.255.255.0\nno shutdown"',
  unless  ─> 'ssh admin@192.168.1.1 "show ip int  brief | grep GigabitEthernet0/0 | grep ─q 192.168.1.1"',
path    ─> ['/usr/bin', '/usr/sbin'],
sensitive ─> true,
timeout ─> 60,
require ─> Package['openssh─client'], # Ensure SSH client is installed
}
Standalone Mode (agentless):
puppet apply router_config.pp //semi complex
────────────── OpenFlow:
─ OpenFlow is a southbound SDN protocol, Used by the SDN controller.
─ In OpenFlow SDN, both switches and the controller do not run traditional routing or switching protocols; the controller calculates paths and installs flow rules to achieve the same network behavior.
─ Admin expresses intent via applications using northbound APIs; the controller translates that intent into flow rules and installs them on switches using OpenFlow.
─ This allows a centralized controller to install flow─based forwarding rules on switches that operate only at the data plane.
─ Used for controller─dominant (classical) SDN: data centers, network virtualization, dynamic QoS, traffic engineering.
─ SDN Controller: Central “brain” of the network, Makes all forwarding decisions.
─ Controller doesn’t run OSPF/BGP/STP; it uses its own global topology and policies to compute flows.
─ OpenFlow “replaces” distributed control─plane protocols, not the forwarding hardware.
─ VLANs, ACLs, QoS etc., can be implemented by flow rules; no device─based STP is needed.
─ OpenFlow Switch: Physical switch, Virtual switch, Contains only the data plane.
─ Steps: Packet arrives at switch, Switch checks flow table, execute action, If no match then Packet sent to controller, Controller decides, Installs a new flow entry,Future packets follow that rule.
─ What OpenFlow Switches Do NOT Have
        No routing table
        No OSPF, BGP, STP
        No distributed control plane
        No autonomous decisions
─ What OpenFlow Switches DO Have
Flow table
Packet counters
CAM/MAC cache
        Interface statistics
#example config in json generated by controller and installed in switch.
{
"cookie": 12345,
"priority": 1000,
"match": {
"eth_type": "0x0800",    // IPv4
"ipv4_dst": "192.168.1.10"
},
"actions": [
{
"type": "OUTPUT",
"port": "1"              // Forward to port 1
}
]
}
Q1: Why is JSON commonly used in controller─based networking and APIs?
─ Because most northbound interfaces are REST─based and REST commonly uses JSON as the data format.
Q2: Why do modern automation tools prefer JSON?
─ JSON is lightweight, easy to parse, human─readable, and natively supported by languages like Python and JavaScript.
Q3: Where is JSON commonly used in networking automation?
─ REST APIs, RESTCONF, controller northbound APIs, telemetry data, and automation tools.
Q4: What type of structure does JSON use?
─ Key–value pair structure.
Q5: What are the main JSON data types?
─ String, Number, Boolean, Array, Object, and Null.
Q6: How are JSON objects defined?
─ Using curly braces {}.
Q7: How are JSON arrays defined?
─ Using square brackets [].
Q8: How must JSON keys be written?
─ As strings enclosed in double quotes.
Q9: Are comments allowed in standard JSON?
─ No, JSON does not support comments.
Q10: Are trailing commas allowed in JSON?
─ No, trailing commas are not allowed.
Q11: Can JSON use single quotes?
─ No, JSON requires double quotes.
Q12: Can JSON contain nested objects?
─ Yes, objects can contain other objects or arrays.
Q13: What symbol separates keys and values in JSON?
─ Colon (:).
Q14: What symbol separates elements in JSON objects or arrays?
─ Comma (,).
Q15: Why do SDN controllers store inventory data in JSON?
─ Because JSON allows structured storage of device data such as interfaces, roles, and topology information.
Q16: What type of information can controllers store in JSON inventory?
─ Device roles, interfaces, VLANs, routing protocols, telemetry capabilities, and topology data.
Q17: Why is JSON suitable for dynamic network inventory?
─ Because it is lightweight and easy to generate, update, and exchange via APIs.
Q18: What is a REST API?
─ An API that follows REST principles and uses HTTP methods to interact with resources.
Q19: What protocol does REST use for communication?
─ HTTP or HTTPS.
Q20: What does stateless mean in REST APIs?
─ Each request must contain all required information because the server does not store session state.
Q21: What data formats are commonly used in REST APIs?
─ JSON or XML.
Q22: What is the purpose of the Content─Type header in REST?
─ Specifies the format of data sent by the client (e.g., application/json).
Q23: What is the purpose of the Accept header in REST?
─ Specifies the data format expected in the response.
Q24: What are the common HTTP verbs used in REST APIs?
─ GET, POST, PUT, PATCH, DELETE.
Q25: What does GET do in REST APIs?
─ Retrieves resource information.
Q26: What does POST do in REST APIs?
─ Creates a new resource.
Q27: What does PUT do in REST APIs?
─ Replaces an existing resource configuration.
Q28: What does PATCH do in REST APIs?
─ Updates part of a resource.
Q29: What does DELETE do in REST APIs?
─ Removes a resource.
Q30: What is RESTCONF?
─ A REST─based protocol used to configure and manage network devices using YANG data models.
Q31: What modeling language does RESTCONF use?
─ YANG.
Q32: What data format does RESTCONF typically use?
─ JSON.
Q33: What transport protocol does RESTCONF use?
─ HTTP or HTTPS.
Q34: Is RESTCONF stateless or stateful?
─ Stateless.
Q35: What is the advantage of RESTCONF over generic REST APIs?
─ It uses standardized YANG models for consistent network device configuration.
Q36: What is NETCONF?
─ A network management protocol designed to install, manipulate, and delete device configurations.
Q37: What data encoding format does NETCONF use?
─ XML.
Q38: What modeling language does NETCONF use?
─ YANG.
Q39: What transport protocol does NETCONF use?
─ SSH.
Q40: Is NETCONF stateless or stateful?
─ Stateful.
Q41: What key feature does NETCONF support for configuration safety?
─ Transaction─based configuration with validation and rollback.
Q42: What is the candidate configuration in NETCONF?
─ A staging configuration datastore used to validate changes before committing them.
Q43: What is Infrastructure as Code (IaC)?
─ Managing infrastructure using machine─readable configuration files instead of manual setup.
Q44: What tool is commonly used for infrastructure provisioning?
─ Terraform.
Q45: What language does Terraform use for configuration?
─ HashiCorp Configuration Language (HCL).
Q46: What type of tasks does Terraform perform?
─ Provisioning infrastructure such as VPCs, subnets, VMs, and load balancers.
Q47: Does Terraform configure operating systems or applications?
─ No, it only provisions infrastructure resources via provider APIs.
Q48: What is Ansible?
─ An automation and configuration management tool used to configure systems and network devices.
Q49: What language is used to write Ansible playbooks?
─ YAML.
Q50: Is Ansible agent─based or agentless?
─ Agentless.
Q51: How does Ansible communicate with network devices?
─ Using SSH or APIs.
Q52: What concept ensures Ansible only changes configuration when needed?
─ Idempotency.
Q53: What is an Ansible playbook?
─ A YAML file defining automation tasks and desired system state.
Q54: What is an Ansible inventory file?
─ A file listing hosts or groups of hosts to manage.
Q55: What is an Ansible module?
─ A reusable automation component that performs specific tasks.
Q56: What is Chef?
─ A configuration management platform that enforces desired state using a pull─based agent model.
Q57: What programming language is used for Chef recipes?
─ Ruby.
Q58: What is a Chef cookbook?
─ A collection of recipes, templates, and configuration logic.
Q59: What is a Chef recipe?
─ A set of instructions defining how a system should be configured.
Q60: What is the Chef Infra Client?
─ The agent running on nodes that pulls configuration from the Chef server.
Q61: What is Puppet?
─ A configuration management and automation tool that manages infrastructure state.
Q62: What language does Puppet use for configuration?
─ Puppet DSL (.pp manifest files).
Q63: What is the Puppet Master?
─ The central server that stores manifests and distributes configuration catalogs.
Q64: What is a Puppet Agent?
─ A client running on nodes that applies configuration from the Puppet Master.
Q65: What is OpenFlow?
─ A southbound SDN protocol used by controllers to program switch forwarding behavior.
Q66: What type of SDN architecture uses OpenFlow?
─ Controller─dominant (classical) SDN.
Q67: What is the role of the SDN controller in OpenFlow networks?
─ Compute forwarding decisions and install flow rules on switches.
Q68: What happens when a packet does not match any flow rule in an OpenFlow switch?
─ The packet is sent to the controller for a decision.
Q69: What type of table does an OpenFlow switch use?
─ Flow table.
Q70: Do OpenFlow switches run routing protocols like OSPF or BGP?
─ No.
Q71: What main functions do OpenFlow switches perform?
─ Execute flow rules, forward packets, and collect statistics.
Q72: What information can flow rules include?
─ Match conditions and actions such as output port forwarding.
Q73: What is the difference between Terraform and Ansible?
─ Terraform provisions infrastructure, while Ansible configures systems and network devices.
Q74: What is the difference between RESTCONF and NETCONF?
─ RESTCONF uses REST/HTTP with JSON, while NETCONF uses SSH with XML and supports full transactions.
Q75: What is the difference between JSON and XML in network APIs?
─ JSON is lightweight and easier to parse, while XML is more verbose but structured for complex configurations.
LAB: simulation

Question #1041
Tasks
Physical connectivity is implemented between the two Layer 2 switches, and the network connectivity between them must be configured.
1. Configure an LACP EtherChannel and number it as 44; configure it between switches SW1 and SW2 using
interfaces Ethemet0/0 and Ethernet0/1 on both sides. The LACP mode must match on both ends.
2. Configure the EtherChannel as a trunk link.
3. Configure the trunk link with 802.1q tags.
4. Configure VLAN 'MONITORING' as the untagged VLAN of the EtherChannel.
SW1(config)# interface range Ethernet0/0-1
SW1(config-if-range)# channel-group 44 mode active
SW1(config-if-range)# interface Port-channel44
SW1(config-if)# switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q
SW1(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
SW1(config-if)# switchport trunk native vlan 746
SW1(config-if)# no shutdown
SW1(config-if)# end
SW2(config)# interface range Ethernet0/0-1
SW2(config-if-range)# channel-group 44 mode active
SW2(config-if-range)# interface Port-channel44
SW2(config-if)# switchport trunk encapsulation dot1q
SW2(config-if)# switchport mode trunk
SW2(config-if)# switchport trunk native vlan 746
SW2(config-if)# no shutdown
SW2(config-if)# end
Notes
- EtherChannel uses LACP (`mode active`).
- The EtherChannel is assigned to Port-channel44.
- Port-channel44 is configured as an 802.1Q trunk.
- VLAN 746 is configured as the native VLAN.
- Assumption: VLAN 746 is the MONITORING VLAN.
Question #1043
Connectivity between four routers has been established. IP connectivity must be configured in the order presented to complete the implementation. No dynamic routing protocols are included.
1. Configure static routing using host routes to establish connectivity from router R3 to the router R1 Loopback address using the source IP of 209.165.200.230.
2. Configure an IPv4 default route on router R2 destined for router R4.
3. Configure an IPv6 default router on router R2 destined for router R4.
R3(config)# ip route 192.168.1.1 255.255.255.255 209.165.200.229
R3(config)# end
R3# copy running-config startup-config
R2(config)# ip route 0.0.0.0 0.0.0.0 209.165.202.130
R2(config)# ipv6 route ::/0 2001:db8:abcd::2
R2(config)# end
R2# copy running-config startup-config
Question #1052
Connectivity between three routers has been established, and IP services must be configured in the order presented to complete the implementation. Tasks assigned include configuration of NAT, NTP, DHCP, and
SSH services.
1. All traffic sent from R3 to the R1 Loopback address must be configured for NAT on R2. All source addresses must be translated from R3 to the IP address of Ethernet0/0 on R2, while using only a standard access list named PUBNET. To verify, a ping must be successful to the R1 Loopback address sourced from R3. Do not use NVI NAT configuration.
2. Configure R1 as an NTP server and R2 as a client, not as a peer, using the IP address of the R1 Ethernet0/2 interface. Set the clock on the NTP server for midnight on May 1, 2018.
3. Configure R1 as a DHCP server for the network 10.1.3.0/24 in a pool named NETPOOL. Using a single command, exclude addresses 1 - 10 from the range. Interface Ethernet0/2 on R3 must be issued the IP address of 10.1.3.11 via DHCP.
4. Configure SSH connectivity from R1 to R3, while excluding access via other remote connection protocols. Access for user netadmin and password N3t4ccess must be set on router R3 using RSA and 1024 bits. Verify connectivity using an SSH session from router R1 using a destination address of 10.1.3.11. Do NOT modify console.
NTP Configuration
R1(config)# ntp master 1
R2(config)# ntp server 10.1.2.1
R2(config)# end
R1# clock set 00:00:00 Jan 1 2019
R1(config)# ip dhcp pool TEST
R1(dhcp-config)# network 10.1.3.0 255.255.255.0
R1(config)# ip dhcp excluded-address 10.1.3.1 10.1.3.10
R3(config)# interface Ethernet0/2
R3(config-if)# ip address dhcp
R3(config-if)# no shutdown
R3(config)# crypto key generate rsa modulus 1024
R3# copy running-config startup-config
Question #1054
All physical cabling between the two switches is installed. Configure the network connectivity between the switches using the designated VLANs and interfaces.
1. Configure VLAN 12 named Compute and VLAN 34 named Telephony where required for each task.
2. Configure Ethernet0/1 on SW2 to use the existing VLAN named Available.
3. Configure the connection between the switches using access ports.
4. Configure Ethernet0/1 on SW1 using data and voice VLANs.
5. Configure Ethernet0/1 on SW2 so that the Cisco proprietary neighbor discovery protocol is turned off for the designated interface only.
SW1# configure terminal
SW1(config)# vlan 100
SW1(config-vlan)# name COMPUTE
SW1(config)# vlan 200
SW1(config-vlan)# name TELEPHONY
SW1(config)# interface Ethernet0/1
SW1(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW1(config-if)# switchport access vlan 100
SW1(config-if)# switchport voice vlan 200
SW1(config)# interface Ethernet0/0
SW1(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW1(config-if)# end
SW1# write memory
SW2# configure terminal
SW2(config)# vlan 99
SW2(config-vlan)# name AVAILABLE
SW2(config)# interface Ethernet0/1
SW2(config-if)# switchport mode access
SW2(config-if)# switchport access vlan 99
SW2(config-if)# end
SW2# write memory

Additional notes:
Note:
CIMC → Still exists, Manage one physical server C-series, E-series.
UCSM → Still exists, FI in UMM mode / (new)IMM alternative. Manage one UCS domain, manages the UCS hardware/domain (servers, Fabric Interconnects, policies, firmware, service profiles).
UCS Central → Intersight, An on-premises management software platform that manages multiple UCS domains, does not replace UCSM/CIMC (lives in VM/appliance).
UCS Director → Intersight automation/orchestration. UCS Director was designed for orchestration and automation(lives in VM/appliance).
HyperFlex → Now Nutanix HCI on Cisco UCS, Cisco's hyperconverged infrastructure (HCI) software/platform that combines compute and distributed storage across a HyperFlex cluster. It mostly uses UCS C-Series servers in UCSM mode, where each server contributes its own compute resources and local storage to the cluster. This removes the need for a separate SAN. UCSM or CIMC still manage the underlying hardware (lives Across HX server nodes).
Cisco Intersight – A Cisco-hosted, cloud-based infrastructure management platform (similar to the Meraki Dashboard). It connects to and provides centralized management/visibility for one or multiple UCS domains, HyperFlex clusters, Nexus platforms, and other supported Cisco infrastructure. It provides a unified management dashboard and modernizes/replaces the roles of UCSM/UCS Central/UCS Director, but it does not replace CIMC. A UCS FI cluster can be connected to Intersight in either UMM or IMM mode; however, IMM is the modern/recommended management model, where Intersight becomes the management plane for the UCS domain.
UCSM Service Profile → Intersight Server Profiles.
